Data Attributes

Dimensionality – Concept  

More data is required to achieve generalization on higher dimensional data. 


1024x1024 pixels image 
= one million dimensional object 


Challenges with high dimensional data: 
1.
Data sampling requirement: 10^11 versus 10^3

2.
Computational complexity 

3.

Data Attributes

Dimensionality – Challenges  



overfitting


Data Attributes

Dimensionality – Definition

Types of Data Dimensionality:
1.
Low Dimensionality: When the number of features is relatively small, the data is said to have low dimensionality. 

2.
High Dimensionality: When the number of features is large, the data has high dimensionality. 




Data volume can relate to the number of individual samples or the relative size of each sample
Datasets have historically grown at 0.1 and 0.2 orders of magnitude (OOMs) per year; in some cases that translates to ~7 OOM over ~30 years 
Datasets for training AI models have gone from 1e3 samples to 1e12 samples in most cases. This growth is persistent across many different application domains.

Data Attributes

Challenges (Volume)

Data Volume refers to the size, amount, and scale of the data


Figure from https://epochai.org/blog/trends-in-training-dataset-sizes 


Processing Performance is a major bottleneck: 
•
Remedied by new techniques such as parallel processing, parallel data structures, data partitioning, and cache design 

•
Lead to the development of new generations of CPUs, GPUs, and TPUs (Tensor Processing Units)


Curse of dimensionality:
Def.: refers to the difficulties that exist when working with data that exists in a high dimensional space such as images, videos, and large quantities of text
Feature Engineering: 
Def.: Features refer to the components that construct each input sample. 

Data Attributes

Challenges (Dimensionality)

The increase in data volume manifests into a number of challenges 

Data imbalance is typical in real data 
Def.: Data imbalance occurs when the distribution of classes in a dataset is not uniform, meaning one class significantly outnumbers the others.
Consequence: Data imbalance could lead to biased models that perform well on the majority class but poorly on the minority class.
Examples: 
-
Medical Diagnosis: healthy versus non-healthy population in the dataset 

-
Fraud Detection: fraudulent transactions might make up only 0.1% of the dataset, while legitimate transactions make up the remaining 99.9%.

-
Image Recognition: a dataset may contain 85% images of cats and only 15% images of dogs 




Variance and Bias: 
The goal of training any model is to generalize towards unseen samples related to a task of interest. Generalization error can be broken into a variance and bias component. 
Bias is the difference between the average prediction of a model and the correct value trying to be predicted. High bias trends towards simplifying the model while not fitting optimally towards the training data. 
Variance refers to the spread of model predictions for a given data point. High variance models tend to perform well on training data, but do not generalize to unseen test data. 
Oftentimes, bias and variance exhibit an inversely proportional relationship which is known as the bias-variance trade off.  

Data Attributes

Challenges (Dimensionality)

Bias and Variance Tradeoff is a goal that could be achieved by techniques such as cross-validation and regularization

Example: Predicting Houses Prices 
Based on number of rooms → a line is the model (simple) ➔ high bias 
Based on a high-degree polynomial → complex model that fits every data point known ➔ difficult to fit to a new data point, thus, high variance 
High Bias (Underfitting):
-
The model is too simple.

-
It fails to capture the complexity of the data.

-
Error due to bias is high.


High Variance (Overfitting):
-
The model is too complex.

-
It captures the noise in the data.

-
Error due to variance is high.




Data Attributes

Challenges (Dimensionality)

Bias and Variance 


predictions close to target and don’t vary much

Predictions in the vicinity of target but vary widely

Predictions away from target but don’t vary

Predictions far away from target and vary widely


Data Heterogeneity refers to collecting data from a variety of sources. 
•
Syntactic heterogeneity refers to diversity in data types, formats, and encoding of the data, e.g., multimodal data in AV applications 

•
Semantic heterogeneity refers to diversity in the number of semantic categories, e.g., 1000 classes in the ImageNet dataset.



Data Attributes

Challenges (Variety)

Variety refers to different data modalities, structural variation in its construction, different sources from which the data is drawn from, and diversity in its semantic categories.

Corrupted and Noisy Data 
- both data and labels could be noisy

Learn some basic terminologies about model 


Def.: Data Structure is a particular way of storing and organizing data in a computer so that it can be used efficiently.  
•
Different kinds of data structures are suited to different kinds of applications.  


Categories of Data Structures: 
1.
Primitive Data Structure, aka data type •
Integer, floating point (real), string, Boolean 




2.
Linear Data Structure: elements arranged in a sequence or a linear order •
Array: sequence of elements from the same data type  

•
List: sequence of elements from various data types 

•
Linked List: good for insertion and deletion  operations 






Data Structures 

Introduction 

Categories of Data Structures (cont’d): 
•
Queue: most common in software and programming; FIFO strategy 

•
Stack: LIFO strategy  


3.
Non-linear Data Structure: •
Tree: hierarchical structure; every node could have endless offsprings 

•
Binary Tree: finite set of node; each node has at most two children 

•
Graph: vertices (nodes) and edges  

•
Hash Table: uses keys as indices to store data values   







•
Arrays are efficient in terms of access time (O(1) for accessing an element by index) and manipulation, making them a fundamental data structure in AI

•
Diverse applications of graph data structures in solving complex problems

•
Versatility of binary trees makes them essential for efficient data management, retrieval, & processing



Data Structures 

Array versus Graph versus Tree 

•
Array: Linear, elements are stored in a contiguous block of memory.

•
Graph: Non-linear, nodes connected by edges, can have cycles.

•
Tree: Hierarchical, non-linear, each node has only one parent, no cycles.

•
Example Algorithms: •
Array: Binary search (efficient for sorted arrays) 

•
Graph: Dijkstra's algorithm (finding shortest path), Depth-First Search (DFS) 

•
Tree: In-order traversal, pre-order traversal, post-order traversal







Types of conditions | Machine Learning | Google for Developers

•
Can be used in a variety of applications, e.g., classification and regression

•
The process builds a model in the form of a tree structure that mimics rule-based human reasoning

 The process builds a model in the form of a tree structure that 

 mimics rule

•
A decision tree is a tree where•
each node represents a feature (attribute),

•
each link (branch) represents an attribute value (condition), and 

•
each leaf represents an outcome (categorical or continues value).




•
The objective is to build the tree based on the data in order to process and label (make decision on) new unlabeled vectors.



Decision Trees

Introduction

The decision tree once constructed is stored as a lookup table

attribute value (condition), and

(categorical or continues value).

The objective is to build the tree based on 

(make decision on) new unlabeled vectors.


•
Builds the tree through a top-down repetitive process starting at root

•
At each node, determine which feature to assign to the node, and creates children links for each value

•
The decision which feature is assigned to a node is based on splitting criteria/measure

•
ID3 uses Entropy and Information Gain as criteria

Understand basic search algorithms and Dijkstra’s 


▪
A greedy algorithm always makes the choice that looks best at the moment•
Everyday examples: o
Driving in Atlanta

o
Invest on stocks

o
Choose a university•
The hope: a locally optimal choice will lead to a globally optimal solution•
For some problems, it works













▪
greedy algorithms tend to be easier to code



Greedy Algorithms 

Definition

▪
Many optimization problems can be solved more quickly using a greedy approach•
The basic principle is that local optimal decisions may may be used to build an optimal solution

•
But the greedy approach may not always lead to an optimal solution overall for all problems

•
The key is knowing which problems will work with  this approach and which will not







❑
Dynamic programming•
We make a choice at each step

•
The choice depends on solutions to subproblems

•
Bottom-up solution, from smaller to larger subproblems





❑
Greedy algorithm•
Make the greedy choice and THEN

•
Solve the subproblem arising after the choice is made 

•
The choice we make may depend on previous choices, but not on solutions to subproblems

•
Top-down solution, problems decrease in size

•
Doesn’t always yield an optimal solution






Greedy Algorithms 

Definitions and Comparisons 


•
Graph G = (V,E)•
Set V of vertices (nodes)

•
Set E of edges •
Elements of E are pair (v,w) where v,w  V.

•
An edge (v,v) is a self-loop. (Usually assume no self-loops.)







•
Weighted graph•
Elements of E are ((v,w),x) where x is a weight.




•
Directed graph (digraph)•
The edge pairs are ordered




•
Undirected graph•
The edge pairs are unordered•
E is a symmetric relation

•
(v,w)  E   implies   (w,v)  E

•
In an undirected graph (v,w) and (w,v) are the same edge

Greedy Algorithm

Dijkstra’s Algorithm – Graphs Overview 

•
Queue - Store the vertices in a first-in, first out (FIFO) queue as follows. Put the starting node in a queue first. Dequeue the first element and add all its neighbors to the queue. Continue to explore the oldest unexplored vertices first. Thus the explorations radiate out slowly from the starting vertex. This describes a traversal technique known as breadth-first search.

•
Stack - Store vertices in a last-in, first-out (LIFO) stack. Explore vertices by going along a path, always visiting a new neighbor if one is available, and backing up only if all neighbors are discovered vertices. Thus our explorations quickly move away from start. This is called depth-first search. 



Stacks and queues in C

•
In an edge-weighted graph, the weight of an edge measures the cost of traveling that edge.

•
For example, in a graph representing a network of airports, the weights could represent: distance, cost or time.

•
Such a graph could be used to answer any of the following:•
What is the fastest way to get from A to B?

•
Which route from A to B is the least expensive?

•
What is the shortest possible distance from A to B?




•
Each of these questions is an instance of the same problem:


The shortest path problem!

In an edge


weighted graph, the weight of an edge 

measures the cost of traveling that edge.

For example, in a graph representing a network of 

airports, the weights could represent: distance, cost 

or time.


following:


•
If all the edges in a graph have non-negative weights, then it is possible to find the shortest path from any two vertices.•
Shortest path in unweighted graph means the route with the minimum number of layovers 

•
Shortest path in weighted graph means the route with the minimum number of miles 




•
For example, in the figure, the shortest path from B to F is { B, A, C, E, F } with a total cost of nine.

•
Thus, the problem is well defined for a graph that contains non-negative weights.



Greedy Algorithm

Dijkstra’s Algorithm – Shortest Path 



•
Dijkstra's algorithm solves the single-source shortest path problem for a non-negative weights graph.

•
It finds the shortest path from an initial vertex, say A, to all the other vertices.



Greedy Algorithm

Dijkstra’s Algorithm 

•
Pick a starting node

•
Initialize 

•
Loop for all unvisited nodes: •
Compute distances to next unvisited nodes 

•
Update distances on table 

•
Update previous nodes on table 

•
Pick next node and update the two lists: visited and unvisited nodes

Minkowski


▪
A greedy algorithm always makes the choice that looks best at the moment•
Everyday examples: o
Driving in Atlanta

o
Invest on stocks

o
Choose a university•
The hope: a locally optimal choice will lead to a globally optimal solution•
For some problems, it works













▪
greedy algorithms tend to be easier to code



Recap: Greedy Algorithms 

Definition

▪
Many optimization problems can be solved more quickly using a greedy approach•
The basic principle is that local optimal decisions may may be used to build an optimal solution

•
But the greedy approach may not always lead to an optimal solution overall for all problems

•
The key is knowing which problems will work with  this approach and which will not

Proximity Measures




Mahalanobis Distance

The Mahalanobis distance can be visualized as the distance of a point from the center of a dataset, onto an ellipse (if there are two variables) whose main direction is that of the data, as illustrated in the figure. 
The ellipse drawn is that of equal Mahalanobis distance, so any point lying on that ellipse will be equally far from the centroid. 
The axes of the ellipse are scaled so that they are both of the same length for points at equal Mahalanobis distance from the center.

Euclidean 


•
The choice depends on the domain

•
Definition of similarity varies from one domain to another•
e.g., shape, alphabet/word frequency, magnitude … etc. 





 
Choice Parameters 

A good distance measure can be defined based on the domain and the measure properties 

Measure Properties to become a metric: 
-
Non negativity, 0 ≤  d(x,y) 

-
Identity, d(x,y) = 0 ⇔x = y 

-
Symmetry, d(x,y) = d(y,x) 

-
Triangle inequality, d (x , y ) ≤ d (x , z ) + d (z , y ) 


Other properties: 
-
Tolerance to noise 

-
Tolerance to outliers 

-
… etc.

Minkowski


•
Information theory is a well-developed and fundamental field•
Communication systems 

•
Compression: JPEG, MP3, AAC




•
Some similarity measures are based on information theory •
Mutual information in various versions

•
Maximal Information Coefficient (MIC) and related measures

•
General and can handle non-linear relationships

•
Can be complicated and time intensive to compute






Information-based Measures

•
The more certain an outcome, the less information that it contains and vice-versa•
For example, if a coin has two heads, then an outcome of heads provides no information




•
More quantitatively, the information is related to the probability of an outcome•
The smaller the probability of an outcome, the more information it provides and vice-versa







… a measure of how much information one set of values provides about another, given that the values come in pairs, e.g., height and weight. 
•
If the two sets of values are independent, i.e., the value of one tells us nothing about the other, then their mutual information is 0.




Mutual Information 

Definition 


Mutual Information 

Definition


Mutual Information 

Example

•
I(x, y) = H(x) + H(y) − H(x, y) = 1.9502






•
Mutual information of Student Status and Grade =  0.9928 + 1.4406 - 2.2710 = 0.1624

Minkowski


Recall that KL divergence, also known as relative entropy, measures the difference between two probability distributions X and Y. It quantifies the amount of information loss when Y is used to approximate X. 
Cross entropy measures the average number of bits needed to encode events from one probability distribution (X) using the optimal code for another probability distribution (Y). 
➔ Cross-Entropy: Average number of total bits to represent an event from Y instead of X.
➔ Relative Entropy (KL Divergence): Average number of extra bits to represent an event from Y instead of X.

Cross Entropy 

Definition 



Recall that KL divergence, also known as relative entropy, measures the difference between two probability distributions X and Y. It quantifies the amount of information loss when Y is used to approximate X. 
Cross entropy measures the average number of bits needed to encode events from one probability distribution (X) using the optimal code for another probability distribution (Y). 
➔ Cross-Entropy: Average number of total bits to represent an event from Y instead of X.
➔ Relative Entropy (KL Divergence): Average number of extra bits to represent an event from Y instead of X.

Cross Entropy

Cross Entropy 

Definition – a second one  

Bernoulli distribution is a random variable which takes the value 1 with probability p and the value 0 with probability (1-p). Less formally, it can be thought of as a model for the set of possible outcomes of any single experiment that asks a yes–no question. 
 
{\displaystyle p}
{\displaystyle q=1-p}

Review the concepts of basis vectors and basis 


▪
Data is information that has been translated into a form that is more convenient to process

▪
As information take different forms,  the most efficient  way is to represent all forms of information using a universal format.



Data 

One Possible Definition 


Word processing programs

Engineering programs

Image processing programs

Audio play program

Video display programs








Engineering 


•
Human senses deal with a variety of information (signals).

•
Input devices of computer translates these information into electrical signals 



Data 

Coding and Decoding for a Computer  

•
Electrical signals are then translated into universal format (0s,1s), this is known as coding.

•
After processing, output devices transform back data into their original form, this is known as decoding





•
A bit is the smallest unit of data that the computer deals with.•
a bit can take two values (0 or 1).




•
In computer memory data are stored as blocks of bits (bit-patterns), the length of bit-patterns is the number of bits in the bit-patterns.•
A bit-pattern of 8 bits length is called a byte






Data 

Bit Stream 

For Text, 
•
Written text is made of alphabetical symbols (letters). For example, in English there are 26 uppercase and 26 lowercase symbols.

•
Each of those symbols is represented by distinctive bit-pattern (code)

•
Once alphabetical symbols are represented by a bit-pattern, any word that is made of combination of letters can be represented.  





•
The number of possible bit-patterns (symbols) made of N number of bits, M is given by:


                                           M = 2N 
•
Inversely, the number of bits needed to construct M number of symbols is given by: 


                        N= Log2 M ≈ 3.2 Log10 M 
    (Note: N must be rounded to next bigger integer)
•
Ex: for M = 26, what is the min number of bits?


    N= Log2 26 = 3.2 Log10 26 = 4.5 = 5 bits 

Data 

Bit Stream 


•
Although some refer to assigning bits to data as data representation, we will refer to it as bit assignment, or code assignment 

•
Data representation is the process of expressing data in a domain(space) that may not necessarily be the natural form of sensing, collecting, or displaying the data. •
We can achieve this via a predefined model, e.g., Fourier Transform, DFT, Wavelets … etc. 

•
Or we can use data-centric models such as Autoencoders, CNNs, Transformers, LLMs ... etc. 




•
Representing the data in a different domain is usually motivated by the processing needs



Data Representation 

•
The choice of the domain, e.g., time versus frequency, depends on the application and the processing needs •
RGB versus Hue/Saturation/Brightness

Time Domain:
•
Tells us how properties (air pressure in a sound function, for example) change over time:


Amplitude = 100
Frequency = number of cycles in one second = 200 Hz
Frequency Domain: 
•
Tells us how properties (amplitudes) change over frequencies:



Transform

Time-Frequency 

Usually, transforms are energy compactors 

Different frequencies of sound waves are associated with differences in activation of cilia along different areas of the basilar membrane. Low-frequency sounds are perceived as lower pitch due to activation of cilia deep in the cochlea, whereas high-frequency sounds are higher pitched and processed closer to the base of the cochlea.




Image from https://opentext.wsu.edu/psych105/chapter/5-5-hearing/

Unitary Transform 


If V is the space of real, two-dimensional, vectors and A is any rotation or reflection, then A is orthogonal.

Unitary Transform 

If V is a real/complex inner product space, then a linear map A:V→V is orthogonal/unitary if it preserves the inner product:v,w= Av,Aw for all v,wV.

If V is the space of functions defined in 1D and A is any translation, then A is orthogonal.










A=

v2

v1

A(v2)




A=












If V is the space of functions defined on a circle and A is any rotation or reflection, then A is orthogonal.

Unitary Transform 

So what?!
In shape matching we have to deal with the fact that rotations do not change the shape of a model. 
 

A=






























V


What is the best orthogonal transform for a given source ?
In other words, what is the most important aspect of such a transform? 
The answer is to design a transform that utilizes the dependencies between samples. 
Thus, the question becomes as follows: Is it possible to find an orthogonal transform that generates completely decorrelated transform coefficients? 
KLT is an orthogonal/unitary transform that produces completely decorrelated transform coefficients 

KLT

Karhunen Loève Transform 

What is the best orthogonal transform for a given

Outline and Objectives

Objectives: 
1.
Understand Reasoning within an AI system

2.

Knowledge and Reasoning

•
The main component in the cycle isKnowledge Representation and Reasoningwhich shows the human-like intelligence in the machines. Knowledge representation is all about understanding intelligence. Instead of trying to understand or build brains from the bottom up, its goal is to understand and build intelligent behavior from the top-down and focus on what an agent needs to know in order to behave intelligently. Also, it defines how automated reasoning procedures can make this knowledge available as needed.





Deductive Reasoning – Making specific conclusions from a general set of facts and logical premises.  If the premises are true, then the conclusions will deterministically be true.

Reasoning

Deductive Reasoning

•
Examples of Deductive Reasoning


Example:
1.
First premise: All men are mortal

2.
Second Premise: Martin is a man

3.
Conclusion: Therefore, Martin is mortal


Example in AI: Rule-Based / Expert Systems – such as decision trees. 


Monotonic Reasoning – 
Holding certain conclusions that do not change regardless of new information being added.  Deductive reasoning is a type of Monotonic Reasoning.

Reasoning

Monotonic Reasoning

Examples of Monotonic Reasoning
Example 1: 
We conclude the earth revolves around the sun.  No matter what other information we learn about the earth or the sun, the initial conclusion of the earth revolving around the sun does not change.
Examples in AI: 
Recommendation systems – Recommender systems collect user rankings and assume that the collection of additional rankings does not change previous rankings.
Malware detection – Malware can be represented as only a small percentage of content in an effort to fool an algorithm to accept it as harmless.  A monotonic malware detector will ignore the extent of benign information and deny access based on any presence of malware regardless of how small.


•
Self-driving cars operate in complex and dynamic environments that require adaptation and decision beyond the rigidness of monotonic reasoning.

•
Monotonic reasoning ignores additional data in favor of adhering to the rule while non-monotonic reasoning allows adaptation based on new scenarios.

•
Example: Emergency Vehicle Approaching•
Monotonic reasoning: If the traffic light is red, stop.

•
Non-Monotonic reasoning: If an emergency vehicle is approaching, yield and clear the way even if the light is red.




•
Example: Unexpected Obstacle•
Monotonic reasoning: Maintain lane position

•
Non-monotonic reasoning: If an object suddenly appears (e.g., fallen tree) break lane position to avoid collision




•
Example: Ethical Dilemma – Choosing to save passengers or a pedestrian•
Monotonic reasoning not equipped to handle potential ethical complexities

•
Non-monotonic reasoning allows for use of context-dependent choices (even if they are can’t be judged as “right” or “wrong”)






Reasoning

Monotonic Reasoning

Monotonic vs Non-Monotonic Reasoning: Self-driving cars (Autonomous vehicles)


Inductive Reasoning – Making general conclusions from a set of specific observations.  The conclusion will be associated with a probability or likelihood of truth and not deterministically true.

Reasoning

Inductive Reasoning

•
Examples of Inductive Reasoning


Example: A doctor working in an area with a high case of malaria (which always accompanies fever) will infer that a new patient with a fever probably has malaria.  Conversely, a doctor in an area where the observed cases of malaria are rare will infer that a new patient with a fever probably does not have malaria and look for an alternative disease as an explanation.
Example in AI: Machine Learning Models - Machine learning is designed to analyze copious amounts of data in order to build models that can detect and recognize patterns with high probability of accuracy (i.e., image classification, spam filtering, etc.).


Reasoning

Inductive Reasoning

Inductive Reasoning

`A feed-forward reasoning approach that is aimed at detecting generalizations, rules, or regularities 1’

[1] Klauer, Karl Josef, and Gary D. Phye. "Inductive reasoning: A training approach."Review of Educational Research78.1 (2008): 85-123.

Abductive Reasoning – 
Making general conclusions to the best explanation based on current available information.  Inferences are drawn without a complete assurance of accuracy.
“Once you eliminate the impossible, whatever remains, no matter how improbable, must be the truth.” -Sherlock Homes ―Arthur Conan Doyle

Reasoning

Abductive Reasoning

•
Examples of Abductive Reasoning


Example: You observe the street outside your house is wet and consider several possibilities: (1) It rained (2) water pipe burst (3) A street cleaning vehicle passed by.  You observe that only the street is wet and there was no news report of a water pipe burst.  You also observe what looks like tracks in the street that look to be left by a large vehicle so you infer the best explanation to be a street cleaning vehicle.
Example in AI: Natural Language Understanding - Interpretation of human language involves more than word recognition.  It is critical to build advanced AI models that can infer missing context and understanding of ambiguous statements from the words present in a sentence or conversation.


Reasoning

Testing

An abductive reasoning approach creates hypothesis and tests its validity

Knowledge and Reasoning - Summarizing

•
Summarizing



Reasoning Type

 Important Points

 Advantages

 Disadvantages

 Real

 

Deductive Reasoning

 •
Starts with general principles and applies to specific cases

•
Deals with reasoning towards certain conclusions based on accepted facts and premises



 * Guarantees logical correctness if premises are accurate

 •
Requires accurate premises; false premises lead to false conclusions.

•
Limited applicability to uncertain or incomplete information.



 •
Rule-Based / Expert Systems: These systems use predefined rules to make decisions. For instance, an expert system diagnosing diseases based on symptoms.



 
Inductive Reasoning

 •
Generalizes from specific observations to form broader conclusions.

•
Deals with probability and involves reasoning toward likely conclusions based on data.



 •
Useful for pattern recognition and prediction.

•
Handles uncertainty and incomplete data.



 •
Conclusions are not certain; they are probabilistic.

•
Risk of overgeneralization.



 •
Machine Learning Models: Training models on historical data to predict future outcomes (e.g., predicting stock prices).



 
Abductive Reasoning

 •
Seeks the simplest and most likely explanation for an observation. It is used when data is incomplete or ambiguous.



 •
Useful for hypothesis generation.

•
Handles incomplete information.



 •
Conclusions are speculative; not guaranteed to be true.

•
Requires additional validation.



 •
Natural Language Understanding: Inferring missing context in conversations or understanding ambiguous statements.



 
Monotonic Reasoning

 Monotonic reasoning involves adding new information without retracting previous conclusions. It is used in knowledge representation.

 •
 Simple and straightforward

•
 Supports incremental updates.



 - Cannot handle non-monotonic situations (e.g., exceptions). - May lead to incorrect conclusions when new information contradicts old assumptions.

 •
Malware detection: AI model catches malware even it’s surrounded by mostly benign information.



 



•
What is Abductive Reasoning?: The Guide to Guessing Like a Genius!

•
Have you ever played detective and tried to solve a mystery with just a few clues? That’s a bit like what abductive reasoning is all about! It’s a fancy term introduced by a smart thinker named Charles Sanders Peirce from America. Imagine you’re trying to figure out a puzzle, but you don’t have all the pieces. Abductive reasoning helps you make your best guess with the pieces you do have. It’s like being a detective in your own mind, and it’s not just for people; even robots and computers use it to think and solve problems!



Abductive Reasoning 

•
Contrast with Other Reasoning Styles: Abductive reasoning starts with incompletedata, seeking the most likely explanation, unlike deductive reasoning’s general-to-specific approach and inductive reasoning’s specific-to-general pattern.

•
Crucial for Hypothesis Formation: Essential in AI, it facilitates the formation of plausible hypotheses from limited or ambiguous data, a key step in intelligent problem-solving.

•
Integral in Decision-Making: It empowers AI systems to make informed decisions and solve problems efficiently, even when faced with incomplete or uncertain information.

Why Spoonbill?


Shallow-water bird with flattened beak and football shaped body. They are pale pink birds with pink shoulders and rump. They have a white neck and a partially feathered, yellow green head.


Contrastive visual explanations – answers to `Why P, rather than Q?’ Questions


Why Spoonbill, rather than Flamingo?

Spoonbills have shorter legs and necks compared to Flamingos

ECE 2806: AI First (AI Foundations) Lecture 09: Ethics and AI –Introduction

Pamela Bhatti and Ghassan AlRegib



Ethics and AI

Learning Objectives: After completing this module, each student should be able to:
•
Recognize and recall three common ethical frameworks 1) deontological ethics2) consequentialist/utilitarian ethics 3) virtue ethics 

•
Access ethical standards as outlined by the United Nations resolution and ECE-related professional societies. 

•
Identify and discuss ethical issues when implementing and analyzing Artificial Intelligence models and systems. 

•
Explain basic ethical concepts like rights, justice, fairness, responsibility, and negligence.

Deontological Ethics (deontology)

•
Individuals should follow established moral rules or ethical codes.

•
Focus on the nature of actions vs. consequences.

•
Immanuel Kant: 18th-century German philosopher (1724-1804)

•
Categorical imperative: One should act according to rules that can be universally applied.

•
The Golden Rule: Buddhism: “Hurt not others in ways that you yourself would find hurtful.” (Udanavarga 5:18)



What is an example from AI?


Consequentialist/Utilitarian Ethics

•
Attributed to U.K. philosophers: 1) Jeremy Bentham (1748-1832)2) John Stuart Mill (1806-1873)

•
Hedonistic calculus is implemented to measure the pleasure and pain generated by an action.




Consequentialist/Utilitarian Ethics (continued)

•
The goal is to maximize good and minimize suffering for all.

•
Impartiality: In making an ethical choice each person is equally factored.



A person looking at another person

Description automatically generated
What is an example from AI?


Virtue Ethics

•
A virtue is a positive trait or characteristic that is considered morally good.

•
Developing virtues as an individual is at the core.

•
Aristotle: (384 – 332 BC) Ancient Greek philosopher and polymath.

•
The Golden Mean: Confidence is the balance between arrogance (excess) and self-deprecation (deficiency)



What is an example from AI?


Ethical Theories …

Deontological (Kant)The Golden Rule

Utilitarian(Bentham & Mill)The Golden Sacrifice

Virtue(Aristotle)The Golden Mean

What is ethical decision-making?

Considering all the possibilities, gathering evidence and information, talking with stakeholders, and finally deciding.

Wood human figure

United Nations Resolution

Goal is to Establish Ethics of AI

•
 Established a global consensus approach•
193 UN Member States

•
March 2024




•
AI for sustainable development

•
The resolution affirms that safe, secure, and trustworthy AI systems can accelerate progress toward UN’s 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in all three dimensions – economic, social, and environmental – in a balanced way.



Floating globe showing Atlantic Ocean

UN Resolution Principles

Thirteen strategies (First four)
•
Cooperating with and providing capacity building and technical and financial assistance to developing countries;

•
Closing the AI divides and other digital divides that exist between and within countries;

•
Promoting equitable access to the benefits of AI systems;

•
Respecting, protecting, and promoting human rights and fundamental freedoms throughout the life cycle of AI systems;

Gaussian/Normal Distribution 



Detour - Gaussian/Normal Distribution 

Defined by two numbers, mean and standard deviation 























X

f(X)








Changing μ shifts the distribution left or right.

Changing σ increases or decreases the spread.


This is a bell-shaped curve with different centers and spreads depending on  and 





Note constants:
=3.14159
Detour 


•
No matter what  and  are, •
the area between - and + is about 68%; 

•
the area between -2 and +2 is about 95%; and 

•
the area between -3 and +3 is about 99.7%.  





Almost all values fall within 3 standard deviations.

Mini-batch Gradient Descent


Convergence path of mini-batch GD

theta = theta -(2/b)*learning_rate*(np.matmul(X[i:i+b].T, (np.matmul(X[i:i+b],theta)-y_i)))

•Epoch:  a sequence of repetitions which completes the processing of the entire dataset

•Iteration: processing samples to calculate a single gradient descent step and update coefficients



Gradient Descent

Epoch vs Iteration

Batch GD

 Stochastic GD

 Mini

 

In one iteration:

 The entire dataset is processed to calculate a GD step in a single iteration.

 a single data sample is processed at random to calculate GD step and update coefficient.

 A mini-patch subset of dataset is processed in one iteration (dataset is divided into 𝑏 batches).
 


An epoch completes:

 in one iteration.

 in 𝑁 iterations (when all data samples are processed)
 

when all batches are processed an epoch is completed (𝑁/𝑏 iterations) 
A picture containing table, map, text, indoor

Description automatically generated
•
All GD methods end up near the minimum

•
Batch GD’s path stops at the minimum

•
Both Stochastic GD and Mini-batch GD continue to walk around

•
However, Batch GD takes a lot of time to take each step

•
Stochastic GD and Mini-batch GD would also reach the minimum if a good learning schedule is used



Gradient Descent

Convergence


•Step size (learning rate) affects convergence speed and accuracy:•
Too small → slow convergence

•
Too large → oscillates around minimum and does not converge precisely




•Local Minima Problem•
MSE is a convex function and, therefore, has only one minimum.

•
Non-convex functions have more than one minima, and, depending on initialization, Gradient Descent may miss the global minimum and converge onto one of the local minima.






Gradient Descent

Pitfalls






•

Ridge Regularization

Robustness to Noise

Large variance of slope (coefficients) of linear regression models (gray lines) with induced noise samples (gray dots)
Large variance of slope (coefficients) of linear regression 

models (gray lines) with induced noise samples (gray dots)


The linear regression model (red line) overfits to the small amount of training data (red dots), while ridge regression (blue line) generalizes better to the test data (green dots) with lower variance

Performance Metrics

Mean Square Error (MSE): Larger errors are well noted (better than MAE). But the disadvantage is that it also squares up the units of feature as well. 
Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE): Solves the problem of squaring the units.
Mean Absolute Error (MAE): Fails to punish large errors in prediction.
The lower these metrics, the better the quality of the corresponding trained regression model.

Distance Measures


Performance Metrics

•
R2is a number in [0,1]. The closer to 1 the better 

•
R2is a measure of the ratio of variability that the model can capture versus the natural variability in the target variable.

•
IfR2= 1, all the data points fall perfectly on the regression line.

•
IfR2= 0, the estimated regression line is perfectly horizontal (mean of all y)




Distance Measures

Assess model validation 




•
Training/Validation/Test Split•
Testing subset kept away and never used during model training

•
Training subset used for training and typically split further into Training/Validation

•
Validation subset for model validation




•
Learning Curves•
Show how training and validation scores compare as a function of increasing training set size

•
Gives insight on a model’s generalization ability 




•
Cross-Validation•
Used for assessing how the training results of a model will generalize to unseen data






Model Validation


•
Test Dataset•
Only used once a model is completely trained (using the train and validation sets)

•
Generally used to evaluate competing models

•
Contains data that spans the various classes in the real world.




•
Training Dataset•
The actual dataset use to train the model.

•
The model sees and learns from this data




•
Validation Dataset•
Used to evaluate a given model and fine-tune its hyperparameters

•
The model sees this data during training but never learns directly from it

•
Typically implemented as cross-validation




•
Split ratio•
Typically: %20-30% test, and the remaining training/validation

Model Validation




Learning Curves

models
learning_curves
Conclusion: Underfitting

Linear Regression model

Model Validation

a 10th-degree Polynomial model



•
Examples of two models trained on the same data.  

•
Evaluation metric: RMSE

•
Linear regression model (top)•
High error rates

•
Quickly reaches a plateau both in training and validation (underfitting)




•
Polynomial regression model •
Has lower error than linear model, 

•
Wider gap between the curves (overfitting)




•
How to combat underfitting? Increase complexity of model

•
How to combat overfitting? More training data



Learning Curves


•
Helps determine hyperparameters

•
More rigorous and randomized than single validation split

•
A cross-validation procedure is performed for each combination of hyperparameters

•
The general procedure is as follow:1.
Shuffle the dataset randomly

2.
Split the dataset into k groups

3.
For each group1)
Take that group as a hold-out or validation dataset

2)
Combine the remaining k-1 groups as one training dataset

3)
Fit a model on the training set and evaluate it on the validation (hold-out) set

4)
Retain the evaluation scores and discard the model




4.
Average the scores of the model to get a single k-fold validation score






Model Validation

Cross Validation

https://cdn-images-1.medium.com/max/1600/1*me-aJdjnt3ivwAurYkB7PA.png

•
Model complexity increases with increasing degrees of freedom, e.g., the degree of polynomial regression

•
Increasing model complexity:•
consistently decreases training error at first

•
model starts to overfit to the training data after testing error decreases to a certain complexity level






Model Validation

Model Complexity vs Prediction Error


A screenshot of a cell phone

Description automatically generated

Linear regression (red)
2-degree polynomial regression (blue)
300-degree polynomial regression (green)

•
Assumingyou could train a Linear Regression model more than once, each time based on new data.

•
The resulting models will have a range of prediction scores.

•
Bias is the average prediction scores of these models.

•
Models that give high average error are considered to have high-bias, and this is directly related to the model’s low complexity, and lack of flexibility



Model Validation

Bias-Variance Tradeoff

https://player.slideplayer.com/78/12848836/slides/slide_7.jpg

https://player.slideplayer.com/78/12848836/slides/slide_8.jpg
•
Assume the same with a high-degree Polynomial Regression model

•
Average prediction error (bias) of the resulting polynomial models will be much lower. 

•
However, the predictions for a given test point will vary largely between the models.

•High-variance occurs when a model is too flexible that it overfits to the specific data it was trained on.

•
High-variance models fail to generalize and therefore give high prediction errors with unseen data



Model Validation

Bias-Variance Tradeoff










•
A model’s error score and its failure to generalize is directly related to its complexity (flexibility).

•
Optimal model complexity is a tradeoff between Bias and Variance•
More complexity decreases Bias

•
More complexity increases Variance






Model Validation

Bias-Variance Tradeoff

http://scott.fortmann-roe.com/docs/docs/BiasVariance/biasvariance.png


Model Validation

Bias-Variance Tradeoff

predictions close to target and don’t vary much

Predictions in the vicinity of target but vary widely

Predictions away from target but don’t vary

Predictions far away from target and vary widely


Linear Model

 Low

 High

 

Bias

 VERY HIGH
Linear model cannot fit non-linear data.
Underfitting

 LOW
Training error slightly lower than test error 

 VERY LOW
Model is well fixed on training data.
Overfitting

 
Variance

 LOWNo significant difference in error when measured across different datasets

 LOWModel performance on other datasets will not vary significantly.

 HIGH
Model will show variance performance on other datasets.

Notation

Examples of Classification

•
Classification:


Finds decision boundary(ies) between labeled data and uses that boundary to label new objects.
•
Regression:


Finds a linear or non-linear real-valued function to predict mapping to a single value
•
Clustering:


Grouping together similar non-labeled data into a number of clusters.  The number of clusters is generally pre-determined, but it could be estimated by the clustering method.

Overview

Classification vs Regression vs Clustering

Overview

Terminologies 

Iris flower dataset 



• Samples: A sample is a data item to process (classify).  It can be a document, a picture, audio clip, video, row in database or CSV file, or whatever you can describe with a fixed set of quantitative traits.

• Features: The collection of distinct traits that can be used to describe each data item in a quantitative manner

• Feature Vector:  An n-dimensional vector that contains the concatenation of all features that represent a sample



Overview

Terminologies



• Feature Extraction: A step that transforms the raw data in the high-dimensional space to a space of fewer dimensions (feature vector)

• Learning Model: A class of functions in which an algorithm searches to find one that best estimates the mapping between domain data and target output.  On the implementation level, a model can have either of the following two related meanings: a)
The structure (usually a graph) that expresses the computational process of how a prediction will be computed,

b)
The weights and biases of the computational structure which are determined by learning/training.





• Training Algorithm: The algorithm which trains the model by updating its parameters based on the training dataset to improve its mapping performance.

Multi


•
Addition of a single point dramatically changes the MMH line



Support Vector Machines

Classifiers

Addition of a single point dramatically changes the MMH line

•
Clearly, the case of perfect separability is an ideal one. Most "real world" datasets will not have such perfect separability via a linear hyperplane

•
However, if there is no separability then we are unable to construct a MMC by the optimization procedure above. So, how do we create a form of separating hyperplane?

•
Essentially, we must relax the requirement that a separating hyperplane will perfectly separate every training observation on the correct side of the line (i.e. guarantee that it is associated with its true class label), using what is called asoft margin. This motivates the concept of asupport vector classifier(SVC).



Support Vector Machines

Classifiers


Support Vector Machines

Soft Margin Classifiers


•
A Soft Margin Classifier allows some observations to be on the incorrect side of the margin (or hyperplane), hence it provides a "soft" separation. 



Support Vector Machines

Soft Margin Classifiers

Observations on the wrong side of the margin and hyperplane




Support Vector Machines




Soft Margin Classifiers

Different values of the tuning parameter C




Larger C = Higher penalty for misclassification, results in narrower margin

Introduced to 



Learning in an AI System 

Machine Learning 


•
Classification:


Finds decision boundary(ies) between labeled data and uses that boundary to label new objects.
•
Regression:


Finds a linear or non-linear real-valued function to predict mapping to a single value
•
Clustering:


Grouping together similar non-labeled data into a number of clusters.  The number of clusters is generally pre-determined, but it could be estimated by the clustering method.

Overview

Classification vs Regression vs Clustering

A screenshot of a cell phone

A close up of a logo

A close up of a map

Description automatically generated
A picture containing sky, different, photo, map

Description automatically generated



Introduction

Types of Learning


Clustering is the unsupervised task of grouping a set of samples in such a way where samples in the same group (called a cluster) are more similar (in some sense) to each other than to those in other groups (clusters).  
•
This implies a need for:

-
Specific features to compare samples

-
Proximity measures to assess the similarity of the samples

-
Clustering algorithms to compute proximity and assigns same labels to similar samples

•
Unlike classification or regression, clustering is an unsupervised learning task since data is unlabeled (no given target labels).



Introduction

What is Clustering?

https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Cluster-2.svg/1280px-Cluster-2.svg.png
A close up of a logo

Description automatically generated



•
The process of partitioning a digital image into multiple segments (regions).

•
The result of image segmentation is a set of segments that collectively cover the entire image, or as a set of contours of such regions.

•
Each of the pixels in a region are similar with respect to some characteristic or computed property (feature) such as color, intensity, or  texture.

Image Segmentation

•
News articles dynamically listed in groups and subgroups

•
Each article is a data instance

•
Words in articles constitute features based on which articles are clustered

Clustering

Applications


The following phrases are all taken from two different sources. Can you cluster them into two groups based on their content:
•
"The Seven Social Sins are: Wealth without work.Pleasure without conscience.Knowledge without character.Commerce without morality.Science without humanity.Worship without sacrifice.Politics without principle.”

•
"The death toll from a magnitude 7.8 earthquake and its aftershocks, which struck the southeastern region of Turkey along the border with Syria, has continued to rise.”

•
"Bucharest has denied that Russian missiles flew over Romania, countering Kyiv’s claims that two of Moscow’s rockets crossed the NATO member’s airspace on their way to Ukraine.”

•
"Reader, suppose you were an idiot. And suppose you were a member of Congress. But I repeat myself."



Clustering

Clustering via ChatGPT

CHATGPT
The phrases can be grouped as follows:
Group 1:"The Seven Social Sins are:Wealth without work.Pleasure without conscience.Knowledge without character.Commerce without morality.Science without humanity.Worship without sacrifice.Politics without principle.”
"Reader, suppose you were an idiot. And suppose you were a member of Congress. But I repeat myself.”
Group 2:"The death toll from a magnitude 7.8 earthquake and its aftershocks, which struck the southeastern region of Turkey along the border with Syria, has continued to rise.”
"Bucharest has denied that Russian missiles flew over Romania, countering Kyiv’s claims that two of Moscow’s rockets crossed the NATO member’s airspace on their way to Ukraine."


Can you explain to me what criteria did you use to perform this grouping
ChatGPT:The criteria I used to perform this grouping was based on the content and tone of the phrases. For Group 1, both phrases are more philosophical in nature and contain reflections on social and political issues. For Group 2, both phrases are factual and present information about current events and incidents. This distinction between philosophical and factual content was the main criteria I used to group these phrases into two categories.

Introduced to 








Problem Statement







Standard Algorithm


Step1: initialize 3 centroids


Step2: associating samples with the nearest centroids.


Step 3: Themeanof the3clusters becomes the new centroids.


Step 4: Steps 2 and 3 are repeated until convergence reached.





Convergence Criteria





Example











Example




























centroids move to the mean of the clusters


Example

Step 2 (2nd iteration): Assignment step: re-assign cluster membership for each point
















convergence not met because of cluster membership update



Example

Step 3 (2nd iteration): update step: Re-estimate centroids



















Example

Step 2 (3rd iteration): Assignment step: Re-assign cluster membership for each point



















Example

Step 3 (3rd iteration): update step Re-estimate centroids



















Example

Step 2 (4th iteration): Re-assign cluster membership for each point
Since there is no change in cluster membership, algorithm convergences.

Affects the speed of convergence

Introduced to 


Mini-batch k-Meansk-Mediansk-Medoid Clustering(Vertical Bullet List)






k-means does not model the data distribution and is better suited when: 
•
Clusters are near symmetrical in shape (distance to cluster centroid is relatively same)

•
No overlapping between clusters



k-means is a hard clustering method, which does not provide the probability (degree) of cluster membership

Desired Clustering


Overlapping dataset


•
The Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) is a probabilistic model for representing several normally distributed data within an overall data distribution.



GMM Clustering

Gaussian Mixture Models

A graph of a light in a purple background

Description automatically generated with medium confidence




A graph of a function

Description automatically generated



GMM assumes that data points are generated from a mixture of Gaussian distributions, each representing a cluster, with its own mean and covariance.
Expectation-Maximization (EM) Algorithm:
• Expectation (E-Step): Calculate the probability (responsibility) that each data point belongs to each Gaussian component based on the current parameters.
• Maximization (M-Step): Update the parameters (means, covariances, and mixture weights) of the Gaussian components to maximize the likelihood of the observed data.
Iteration: The E-step and M-step are repeated iteratively until convergence, typically when the change in likelihood or parameters falls below a threshold.
Cluster Assignment: Data points are assigned to the Gaussian component to which they have the highest probability of belonging.

GMM Clustering 


Mixture of 3 Gaussians in 1-d

Introduced to 


•
Clustering performance evaluation, or cluster validation, is an integral part of the process of developing a clustering model.

•
Not as trivial as counting the number of errors or the precision and recall of a supervised classification algorithm. 

•
An evaluation metric could measure if the results produced by the clustering algorithm: •
Are similar to some ground truth set of classes, or

•
Satisfy some assumption such that members belong to the same class are more similar than members of different classes according to some similarity metric.




•
Ground truth may not always be available to allow for a solid evaluation metric

•
The evaluation metric should be independent of the absolute values of the labels, i.e., a permutation of the class or cluster label values will not change the evaluation scores in any way.



Evaluating Clustering Performance


•Internal Evaluation•
Clustering result is evaluated based on the data that was clustered itself

•
These methods usually assign the best score to the algorithm that produces clusters with high similarity within a cluster and low similarity between clusters.

•
High scores on an internal measure do not necessarily result in effective information retrieval applications

•
Biased towards algorithms that use the same cluster model, i.e. distance based algorithms such as k-means will score high with a distance-based internal evaluation measure.

•
Example measures: Davies-Bouldin Index, Dunn Index, Silhouette Coefficient






Evaluating Clustering Performance

Internal vs External 


•External Evaluation•
Clustering results are evaluated based on data that was not used for clustering, such as known class labels and external benchmarks

•
Such benchmarks consist of a set of pre-classified items, and these sets are often created by (expert) humans.

•
Benchmark sets are referred to as “gold standard” or “ground truth”

•
These evaluation methods measure how close the generated clustering is to the predetermined benchmark classes.

•
Example measures: - Purity - Rand measure





 - F-measure - Jaccard index
 - Dice index - Mutual Information score

Evaluating Clustering Performance

Internal vs External

Clustering Metrics

Internal
Davies-Boudlin – 
•
Similarity between each cluster and its most similar one.

•
(Lower means more dissimilar to all other clusters)


Dunn – 
•
Measures density of cluster

•
Small variance within a cluster

•
Distance of means between clusters


Silhouette – 
•
Contrasts the average distance to elements in the same cluster with the average distance to elements in other clusters



External
Rand-Index -
•
Agreement between object pairs and ground-truth


Mutual Information Score – 
•
Measure of overlap between computed an ground-truth cluster on basis of information theory


Cluster Purity –
•
Extent to which clusters contain a single class

•
The brain has approximately 100 billion neurons

•
Neurons communicate through electro-chemical signals. Neurons are connected through junctions called synapses. 

•
Each neuron receives thousands of connections with other neurons, constantly receiving incoming signals.

•
If the resulting sum of the signals surpasses a certain voltage threshold, a response is sent through the axon. This is a non linear relation and motivates the use of activation

The SLP Perceptron Learning Algorithm


The XOR, or “exclusive or”, problem is a classic problem in ANN research. It is the problem of using a neural network to predict the outputs of XOR logic gates given two binary inputs.

Layer-wise organization: MLP consists of fully-connected layers in which neurons between two adjacent layers are fully pairwise connected, while neurons within a single layer share no connections.
Sizing MLP: the number of neurons or the number of parameters (more commonly)
•
The network has 4 + 4 + 2=10 neurons, [3 x 4] + [4 x 4] + [4 x 2] = 12 + 16 + 8 = 36 weights and 4 + 4 + 2 = 10 biases, for a total of 46 learnable parameters.

Review: Artificial Neural Networks

Common Activation Functions






•
Sigmoids saturate and kill gradients. When the neuron's activation saturates at either tail of 0 or 1, the gradient at these regions is almost zero. Recall that during backpropagation, this (local) gradient will be multiplied to the gradient of this gate's output for the whole objective. Therefore, if the local gradient is very small, it will effectively "kill" the gradient and almost no signal will flow through the neuron to its weights and recursively to its data. Additionally, one must pay extra caution when initializing the weights of sigmoid neurons to prevent saturation. For example, if the initial weights are too large then most neurons would become saturated and the network will barely learn. 



Review: Activation Functions 

Sigmoid and Tanh 

Sigmoid is used for its neuron’s firing and non-firing interpretation 

•
Sigmoid outputs are not zero-centered. This is undesirable since neurons in later layers of processing in a Neural Network would be receiving data that is not zero-centered. This has implications on the dynamics during gradient descent, because if the data coming into a neuron is always positive, then the gradient on the weights will, during backpropagation, become either all positive, or all negative. This could introduce undesirable zig-zagging dynamics in the gradient updates for the weights. However, notice that once these gradients are added up across a batch of data the final update for the weights can have variable signs, somewhat mitigating this issue. Therefore, this is an inconvenience, but it has less severe consequences compared to the saturated activation problem.




Tanh: Like the sigmoid neuron, its activations saturate, but unlike the sigmoid neuron its output is zero-centered. Therefore, in practice the tanh non-linearity is always preferred to the sigmoid nonlinearity. 



•
It was found to greatly accelerate the convergence of stochastic gradient descent compared to the sigmoid/tanh functions. It is argued that this is due to its linear, non-saturating form.

•
Compared to tanh/sigmoid neurons that involve expensive operations (exponentials, etc.), the ReLU can be implemented by simply thresholding a matrix of activations at zero.



Review: Activation Functions 

ReLU

•
Unfortunately, ReLU units can be fragile during training and can "die". For example, a large gradient flowing through a ReLU neuron could cause the weights to update in such a way that the neuron will never activate on any datapoint again. If this happens, then the gradient flowing through the unit will forever be zero from that point on. That is, the ReLU units can irreversibly die during training since they can get knocked off the data manifold. For example, you may find that as much as 40% of your network can be "dead" (i.e. neurons that never activate across the entire training dataset) if the learning rate is set too high. With a proper setting of the learning rate this is less frequently an issue.

•
Leaky ReLU. Leaky ReLUs are one attempt to fix the "dying ReLU" problem. Instead of the function being zero, a leaky ReLU will instead have a small negative slope (of 0.01, or so). Some people report success with this form of activation function, but the results are not always consistent.

What is PyTorch?•
A Python-based scientific computing package for executing dynamic computational graphs over Tensor objects that behave similarly as Numpy arrays. 




•
Why PyTorch?•
It comes with a powerful automatic differentiation engine that removes the need for manual back-propagation






PyTorch

Basics



PyTorch

AutoGrad: Automatic Differentiation




import torch
x1 = torch.randn(2, 2).requires_grad_(True)
x2 = torch.randn(2, 2).requires_grad_(True)
y = torch.sum((x1-x2)**2)
y.backward()
print(2*(x1-x2))
print(x1.grad)
# ======= output ========
# tensor([[ 0.1411, 3.9669], [-1.2993, 2.1217]])
# tensor([[ 0.1411, 3.9669], [-1.2993, 2.1217]] , grad_fn=<MulBackward0>)

for i in range(max_iter):
  Y_hat = X@W.T + o@b.T 
  # MSE loss  
  loss = np.sum((Y_hat-Y)**2)/num_samples #FrobeniusNorm
W_gradients= (2/num_samples)*(Y_hat.T-Y.T)@X
b_gradients= (2/num_samples)*(Y_hat.T-Y.T)@o 
  # follow the negative gradients
  W = W - alpha*W_gradients   # update the weight matrix
  b = b - alpha*b_gradients   # update the biases matrix
  Y_hat = X@W.T + o@b.T         # predicted "probabilities
#W gradients: [ 0.06089246 -0.01254302] 
#b gradients: -0.34077861476403615

for i in range(max_iter): 
  y_hat = X_tensor@W_tensor.t() + o_tensor@b_tensor.t()
  # MSE loss
  loss = F.mse_loss(y_hat, y_tensor)
  loss.backward()
W_gradient= W_tensor.grad
b_gradient= b_tensor.grad
  # updating the weights 
  with torch.no_grad():
    W_tensor.data = W_tensor.data - alpha*W_gradient
    b_tensor.data = b_tensor.data - alpha*b_gradient
  y_hat = X_tensor@W_tensor.t() + o_tensor@b_tensor.t()     # predicted "probabilities
#W gradients: [ 0.06089246 -0.01254302]

Given example, we can compare the gradients computed manually with NumPy and PyTorch autograd (see highlighted lines)

for i in range(max_iter):
  H = X@W.T + o@b.T 
  Y_hat = 1. / (1. + np.exp(-H))
  # BCE loss  
  loss = -1. * np.sum(Y * np.log(Y_hat) + (1-Y) * np.log(1-Y_hat)) / num_samples 
W_gradients= (1./num_samples)*(Y_hat.T-Y.T)@X
b_gradients= (1./num_samples)*(Y_hat.T-Y.T)@o
  # follow the negative gradients
  W = W - alpha*W_gradients   # update the weight matrix
  b = b - alpha*b_gradients   # update the biases matrix
#W gradients: [0.07499208 -0.05085141] 
#b gradients: -0.16729619094308953 
Iteration:120 | Loss = 0.3512 | Train Acc = 0.90

for i in range(max_iter): 
  h_hat = X_tensor@W_tensor.t() + o_tensor@b_tensor.t()
  y_hat = torch.sigmoid(h_hat)  
  # BCE loss
  loss = F.binary_cross_entropy(y_hat, y_tensor)
  loss.backward()
W_gradient= W_tensor.grad
b_gradient= b_tensor.grad
  # updating the weights 
  with torch.no_grad():
    W_tensor.data = W_tensor.data - alpha*W_gradient
    b_tensor.data = b_tensor.data - alpha*b_gradient

Basic Modules in PyTorch:
nn.ModuleAPI: defines arbitrary network architectures, while automatically tracking every learnable parameters.
torch.optimpackage: implements all the common optimizers, such as SGD, RMSProp, Adagrad, and Adam, etc.

PyTorch

Basic Modules


# Give your network class an intuitive name like `SNC(Sigle Neuron Classifier)`.
class SNC(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self):
        super(SNC, self).__init__()
        # define a single neuron
        self.single_neuron= nn.Linear(in_features=2, out_features=1, bias=True) # the layer will learn an additive bias
        self.non_linearity = nn.Sigmoid()
            def forward(self, x):
        x = self.single_neuron(x)
        x = self.non_linearity(x)
        return x

PyTorch example – single neuron classifier

To use the Module API, follow the steps below:

1.
Subclassnn.Module



2. In the constructor __init__() define all the layers and components you need as class attributes


3. In theforward()method, define theconnectivityof your network

# Sigle Neuron Nonlinear Classifier
class SNC(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self): # define the layer(s) and network components          
        super(SNC, self).__init__()
        self.single_neuron= nn.Linear(in_features=num_features, out_features=1, bias=True) # the layer will learn an additive bias.
        self.non_linearity = nn.Sigmoid()
    def forward(self, x): # here we define the forward pass for network 
        x = self.single_neuron(x)
        x = self.non_linearity(x)
        return x
network = SNC()
optimizer = optim.SGD(network.parameters(), lr=2) #Stochastic gradient descend 
criterion = nn.BCELoss() # using BCE loss for training 
for i in range(max_iter): 
    network.zero_grad() # clearning all gradients in the network 
    y_hat_tensor = network(X_tensor) #forward pass 
    loss = criterion(y_hat_tensor, y_tensor)
    loss.backward()
    optimizer.step()

# Multi-Layer non-linear Classifier
class MLP(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, num_classes): # define the layer(s) and network components 
        super(MLP, self).__init__()
        self.layer1= nn.Linear(in_features=num_features, out_features=4, bias=True)
        self.out= nn.Linear(in_features=4, out_features=1, bias=True)
        self.non_linearity = nn.Sigmoid()
    def forward(self, x): # here we define the forward pass for network 
        y = self.layer1(x)
        y = self.non_linearity(y)
        y = self.out(y)
        y = self.non_linearity(y)
        return y
network = MLP(num_classes=1)
optimizer = optim.SGD(network.parameters(), lr=1.2) criterion = nn.BCELoss()
for i in range(max_iter): 
    network.zero_grad() # clearning all gradients in the network 
    y_train_hat = network(X_train_tensor) #forward pass     
    loss = criterion(y_train_hat, y_train_tensor)
    loss.backward()
    optimizer.step()

Artificial Neural Networks: 
•
number of parameters scales quadratically with the size of the input 

•
does not leverage stationarity


Convolutional layers: 
•
connect each neuron to a patch of the input

•
share the weight across spatial locations

Convolutional Layer

2D Discrete Convolution


A 3x3 kernel slides over the input features, performing an element-wise multiplication with the current patch of the input, summing up the results into a single output at every location it slides over.

input features (5×5 matrix )

output features (3×3 matrix)

Convolutional Layer

Kernel Intuition


The Convolutional layer is the core building block of a Convolutional Network that does most of the computational heavy lifting.
The properties of convolutional layer:
•
Local Connectivity

•
Parameter sharing

•
Spatial arrangement

Convolutional Neural networks

Introduction


•
Fully-connected networks:•
Output of each neuron depends on the entire input




•
Convolutional networks：•
Output of a neuron only depends on a local region of the input. This region in the input is the receptive field for that neuron.






Luo, Wenjie, et al. "Understanding the effective receptive field in deep convolutional neural networks."NeurIPS 2016

(5x5 region in the input)

CNN Architectures

VGG (2014)


Novelty:
•
Introduced residual learning (Residual blocks)•
Shortcut connections with identity mapping 

•
i.e., skip connections, gradients flow through a network without passing through activations 




•
20 and 8 times deeper than AlexNet and VGG, respectively with less computational complexity and without compromising generalization power

CNN architectures of (a) AlexNet, (b) VGG-16, (c) VGG-19, (d) ResNet-50, (e) ResNet-101 and (f) ResNet-152. In this work, we represent convolution layers as "Convreceptive field size-number of channels". For example, Conv5-256 in (a) represents a convolution layer with a receptive field size of 5 x 5 and 256 channels. FC: Fully Connected layer. FC-2 is abbreviation for fully connected layer with two outputs. Max pool (3 x 3) is a short notation for max pooling operation with 3 x 3 kernel. Avg Pool: average pooling. Similar analogies apply to other operations. Readers are recommended to read the original papers for more detailed information about each layer.

CNN Architectures

VGG (2014)


Similar to ANNs, weights and biases in CNNs can be learned via backpropagation and gradient update w.r.t. a loss function
In general, the loss landscape of CNNs are non-convex with local minima
Other issues in optimization:
•
Noisy gradient estimation

•
many saddle points

•
Ill-conditioned loss surface



Introduction to Loss Functions
Finding Optimal Parameters

Gradient Descent




Finding Optimal Parameters

Gradient Descent




Finding Optimal Parameters

Gradient Descent


•
Loss surface geometries are difficult for optimization with local minima and saddle points

•
At saddle points:o
Gradients are zero  

o
Orthogonal directions disagree 

o
Gradient descent gets stuck




•
Saddle points are common in high dimension



Dauphin et al, “Identifying and attacking the saddle point problem in high-dimensional non-convex optimization”, NeurIPS 2014




PDF] Identifying and attacking the saddle point problem in high-dimensional  non-convex optimization | Semantic Scholar
(a) 5x2-y2 and (b) x3-3xy2

Image patches in the input that cause the maximum activations of certain filters




•
Obtaining Maximally Activating Patches:•
Pick activations in a layer, e.g., conv5 (128 x 13 x 13), pick one of the channels 17/128

•
Feed forward many images through the network,  record values of the chosen channel

•

•
Maximally activating  patches share similar visual patterns
