Training and Best Practices 


•
There is no one-size-fits-all solution here

•
Use the architecture best suited to the application

•
Broadly speaking, CNN architectures may be divided into two kinds: 1.
Those used for classification

2.
Those used for dense prediction tasks e.g., segmentation, reconstruction … etc.




•
After having determined the application and the corresponding architecture type to use with it, search for baseline models made available to the public by people who worked on the problem before you

•
Some places to look for: GitHub, PyTorch and Tensorflow tutorials … etc.

•
Chances are you will already have a dozen solutions tackling the problem or a similar one; no need to re-invent the wheel!


    
Which Architectures to Use?


•
CNN architectures for classification follow a typical pipeline processing input images via multiple 2D kernels across multiple, stacked layers to result in increasingly smaller activations

•
Output of the last convolution layer unrolled and vectorized, passed to fully connected layers and finally a softmax layer to output pseudo-probabilities for the input belonging to different target classes

•
Size of this logit vector equal to number of target classes

•
Number of kernels should be steadily increased starting from the first convolution layer

•
Experiment with different pooling options 

•
Experiment with different activation functions

•
Experiment with dropout 

•
Experiment with skip connections (e.g., as in Resnet described in earlier lectures)




See the source image
Classification Architectures

Which Architectures to Use?


•
Many applications require prediction of dense outputs  e.g., image super-resolution, denoising, semantic segmentation etc.

•
Suboptimal to use classification architectures since pooling operations would reduce input resolution and result in deteriorated output quality

•
Hence use fully convolutional architectures 

•
Typically follow an encoder-decoder configuration

•
Encoder similar to a classification type architecture without fully connected layers

•
Decoder works to progressively upsample encoder output to desired output resolution via transposed/deconvolution layers 




See the source image
Dense Prediction Architectures – next 2 lectures

Which Architectures to Use?


•
The choice of the loss function to use with the architecture should be dictated by the nature of the problem

•
For classification tasks, a typical cross-entropy based loss would be the obvious choice

•
For segmentation tasks, the cross-entropy loss would happen on per pixel basis for each pixel of the output

•
For super-resolution, denoising problems, mean square error loss function or L1 loss would be a good choice

•
Special considerations for when there is class imbalance  (more on next slide)



Loss Functions

Which Architectures to Use?


•
Regular cross entropy loss function fails in situations where one of the classes occurs much more frequently than other(s)

•
Observe the picture to the right: the network is much more likely to predict no-fault pixels on the seismic volume compared to fault pixels

•
Same situation with most anomaly, aberrant-event detection problems

•
Regular cross-entropy loss would make the network predict the majority class just by virtue of its overwhelming presence in the training dataset



See the source image
Class Imbalance


Modified Cross-entropy Loss

Class Imbalance




Focal Loss

Class Imbalance




•
The performance metrics one uses should be appropriate for the application being faced

•
Using the last example of anomaly/aberrant event detection, accuracy is a bad metric

•
A model that is hard-coded to predict the majority class despite the input can still achieve very high accuracy (because it gets most predictions correct just by virtue of the sheer numbers of the majority class)



See the source image
Performance Metrics


Precision and Recall

Performance Metrics


•
Mean Square Error (MSE) and Peak Signal to Noise Ratio (PSNR) can at times be bad predictors of performance in certain dense prediction tasks like image denoising, super-resolution etc.

•
Does not account for the spatial and structural characteristics of the image  

•
Structural Similarity-based Image quality Measure (SSIM) helps to navigate these situations much better 

•
Decomposes the image into luminance, contrast, and structure components and separately compares reference and query images for all three modalities before pooling the results into a single number



SSIM for Image Reconstruction

Performance Metrics



SSIM for Image Reconstruction

Performance Metrics





•
A common occurrence with deep learning models is where they fail to deliver expected performances on training and/or test sets

•
Sometimes, the poor performance might just be a consequence of the hard nature of the problem and indicate more research to be done in designing effective models, capturing better data etc.

•
However, many times, causes of such problems stem from common issues that the practitioner failed to account for earlier

•
Here we look at some of the most common issues that crop up in such situations and some of the ways to tackle them



Model Debugging


•
Model does not have the representational capacity to learn the mapping from input to output data 

•
Typical symptom includes underperformance on both training and test data (situation known as underfitting)

•
Solution is to increase model capacity by increasing the depth of the model, number of learnable kernels etc.

•
Make changes in small steps and monitor test and training performances after each change



Low Model Capacity

Model Debugging


•
When the model has very high representational capacity, it tends to perform well on training data, but poorly on test set—a situation commonly referred to as overfitting

•
Solution is to steadily decrease model capacity (by either reducing the depth and/or the number of learnable kernels) and monitor training and validation error after each change



High Model Capacity

Model Debugging


•
Sometimes, model underperformance (specifically on test set) may be caused by having insufficient data

•
In this case, look for various data augmentation strategies allowing one to perform simple image transformations to multiply the quantity of available training data

•
Common transformations include random cropping, horizontal and vertical flipping, random rotations, adding white gaussian noise, or any combination of these

•
Transfer Learning from models pretrained on large datasets may also sometimes help

•
It may also help to annotate new data if training data does not provide the complete picture of data distribution



Insufficient Data

Model Debugging


See the source image
Original Image

Rotation

Horizontal flip

Noise 

Blur 

Vertical Flip 

Crop

Too Few Data: Data Augmentations

Model Debugging


•
It is known deep networks tend to learn common representations for various tasks in their earlier layers

•
Can be exploited to transfer representations from networks trained on large datasets on one task (i.e., Image Classification on ImageNet) called the source to a different task called the target where quantity of labeled training data is much smaller 

•
Usually done by taking large pretrained network and then finetuning last layer (with all other layers frozen) on target dataset 

•
Helps avoid overfitting and trains a deployable network much faster

•
Pretrained frozen backbone acts as a feature extractor while finetuned last layer acts to project the representations into the decision boundary for the target task

•
Utility depends on how closely related the source and target datasets and/or tasks are 



Transfer Learning

AlexNet


Transfer Learning


•
Sometimes, numerical accuracy metrics can give a false sense of security regarding model performance

•
It helps to visualize network outputs to qualitatively judge performance

•
For image-based tasks, this involves looking at images superimposed with network predictions, visualizing sampled images (in case of dense predictions via VAEs, GANSs, Diffusion models) and reconstructions (e.g., image denoising applications)

•
Visualizing the worst predictions made by the model sometimes reveals important trends e.g., model always makes mistakes on a particular kind of input

•
Helps resolve issues by changing model/training data accordingly



Visualizations



Numerical Gradient Analysis




•
Visualize histograms of network activations and gradients to reveal dead neurons and to reveal any underlying problem with optimization

•
The pre-activation value of a neuron can tell us if the units saturate and how often they do.

•
In a deep network, gradients may quickly grow or vanish; can hamper optimization

•
Monitor magnitudes of parameter updates and compare that to the magnitudes of the parameter themselves

•
Former should be something like 1% of the latter.

•
Too large or too small would indicate problems with optimization



Visualize Activation Histograms


•
Top: Hyperbolic tangent nonlinearity

•
Bottom: Softplus nonlineraity

•
For the hyperbolic tangent, activation values are saturated in the lower layers

•
For softplus, the values are less saturated, specifically around (-0.6, -0.8) and (0.6, 0.8)



Visualize Activation Histograms

Glorot, Xavier, and Yoshua Bengio. "Understanding the difficulty of training deep feedforward neural networks."Proceedings of the thirteenth international conference on artificial intelligence and statistics. JMLR Workshop and Conference Proceedings, 2010.



•
Xavier initialization ensures that the activation and gradient variances remain consistent when forward propagation with activations and backward propagation with gradients

•
More recently: Kaiming initialization for ReLU nonlinearities



Weight initialization: Xavier Initialization

Visualize Activation Histograms

Glorot, Xavier, and Yoshua Bengio. "Understanding the difficulty of training deep feedforward neural networks."Proceedings of the thirteenth international conference on artificial intelligence and statistics. JMLR Workshop and Conference Proceedings, 2010.



•
Tuning hyperparameters like learning rate, batch size (for batch gradient descent), hidden layers, kernels etc., can at times lead to significant gains in network  performance

•
While one can always manually tune hyperparameters, there are more effective strategies available that automate the process: grid search and random search



Tuning Hyperparameters


•
User defines a finite set of values to take for each hyperparameter

•
Software constructs a grid containing all possible configurations of hyperparameter values defined by the user

•
Network training and inference procedure ran for each configuration to search for the best set of values

•
Con: Is computationally expensive!






Learning rate

Batch size

















Grid Search

Tuning Hyperparameters


•
User defines a probability distribution for each hyperparameter setting

•
Software randomly samples a configuration of hyperparameters each time a network is trained 

•
Tends to converge faster than grid search and is less computationally expensive






Learning rate

Batch size






Random Search

Tuning Hyperparameters

What is Explainability?
The ability of an entity to explain or justify its decisions or predictions in human-understandable terms

Explainability

Definition


The ability of an entity to explain or justify its decisions or predictions in human-understandable terms

Explanations

What is Explainability?

Interpretability: Goal of Interpretability research is to understand the inner workings of the model
Explainability: Goal of Explainability research is to explain the network decisions to humans


Interpretability


Explainability


Explainability matters: 
establish trust in deep learning systems by developing transparent models that can explain why they predict what they predict to humans
Explainability is useful in:
•
Medical: help doctors diagnose

•
Seismic: help interpreters label seismic data

•
Autonomous Systems: build appropriate trust and confidence 



classscores



Algorithm

Data

Output 

Deep models act as algorithms that take data and output something without being able to explain their methodology

•
Maximally Activating Patches:•
Image patches in the input that cause the maximum activations of certain filters




•
Obtaining Maximally Activating Patches:•
Pick activations in a layer, e.g., conv5 (128 x 13 x 13), pick one of the channels 17/128

•
Feed forward many images through the network,  record values of the chosen channel

•
Visualize image patches that correspond  to maximal activation




•
Maximally activating  patches share similar visual patterns




Springenberg et al, “Striving for Simplicity: The All Convolutional Net”, ICLR Workshop 2015

•

Saliency Methods

Uncovering Biases


•
Saliency map by vanilla backprop can be noisy

•
We want cleaner saliency maps:•
Modifying (rectifying) the backpropagation empirically produce better visualizations









Backwardpass: Computegradients


Saliency map by vanilla backprop 

Gradient-based Explanations

Vanilla Backpropagation


Going backward performing all the operations of the network (Unpooling, Filtering...), and for ReLU non-linearities,only pass gradients to regions of positive activations

Why is the ReLU function not differentiable at x=0?










































positive activationsin the previous layer


Gradients from the later layer


Saliency map by vanilla backprop 

Gradient-based Explanations

Vanilla Backpropagation


•
The way DeconvNet Backpropagation handle the ReLU non-linearities is different as they propose toonly propagate positive gradient

•
Rectifying the backpropagation empirically produce better saliency visualizations



Simonyan et al., Deep Inside Convolutional Networks: Visualising Image Classification Models and Saliency Maps, 2014





















Saliency map by Deconv backprop 

positive gradient in the later layer









Gradients from the later layer

Why is the ReLU function not differentiable at x=0?
We can think of Deconvnet as 
rectified gradients propagation

Cleaner saliency map

Gradient-based Explanations

Deconvnet Backpropagation


•
Guided backpropagation propose to propagate positive gradient and rectified by positive activations

•
Non-intuitive approach but empirically produce better saliency visualizations



Springenberg, Dosovitskiy, et al., Striving for Simplicity: The all convolutional net, 2015

Gradient-based Explanations

Summary


•
To find the important activations that are responsible for a particular class 

•
We want the activations:•
Class-discriminative to reflect decision-making

•
Preserve spatial information to ensure spatial coverage of important regions







Selvaraju, Ramprasaath R., et al. "Grad-cam: Visual explanations from deep networks via gradient-based localization."
Proceedings of the IEEE international conference on computer vision. 2017.

Gradient-based Class Activation Map (Grad-CAM)

Motivation


•
To find the important activations that are responsible for a particular class 

•
We want the activations:•
Class-discriminative

•
Preserve spatial information




•
Which layer to perturb:•
Higher layer capture class specific information

•
Spatial information is lost in fully-connected layers

•
Last convolutional layer forms a best compromise between high-level  semantics and detailed spatial resolution









Low-level features captured by lower-layers

High-level features captured by higher-layers


Last convolutional layer 

Selvaraju, Ramprasaath R., et al. "Grad-cam: Visual explanations from deep networks via gradient-based localization."
Proceedings of the IEEE international conference on computer vision. 2017.

Gradient-based Class Activation Map (Grad-CAM)

Method


Grad-CAM uses the gradient information flowing into the last convolutional layer of the CNN to assign importance values to each activation for a particular decision of interest.

Explanatory Evaluation Taxonomy

Tasks : Humans directly evaluate explanations. 

Tasks : Any task intersecting with explainability that does not require humans for evaluation.  Ex : Robustness of neural nets.

Tasks : Any task that requires humans-in-the-evaluation-loop without directly having humans measure explainability.

Transformer

Attention is All You Need


Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".


Original task of Transformer architectures is translation between languages.


Transformer

Encoding and Decoding

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".


Basic Structure of Transformer is 6 stacks of each encoder with the output connected to 6 stacks of decoders.



Transformer

Encoding Block

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".


•
Encoder has input that flows into self-attention layer

•
Output of self attention is fed into a feed-forward neural network.•
This network is a MLP network.







Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

•
Decoder block is same at encoder block with an additional attention layer.

•
Attention layer helps decoder focus on relevant parts of input sequence.



Decoding Block



Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

•
Data must first be encoded into a tensor that can be interpreted by a computational model.

•
These tensors can be generated by any embedding algorithm.


•
The tensors are fed into the bottom encoder layer.

•
Note that each word flows through its own path.



Tensors




Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

•
Vectors passed into self attention to produce new vectors.

•
These vectors are passed through neural networks to produce new set of vectors.

•
These vectors are then passed into next encoding block.



Tensor Flow to Next Encoder



Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

•
Goal of all language models is for a model to learn relevant associations between different parts of a sequence.

•
What does “it” refer to in the sentence?

•
Attention is about learning associations to better encode dependencies in data.



Self Attention




Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Self Attention QKV


•
Take into vector and create:•
Query Vector

•
Key Vector

•
Value Vector




•
These vectors are used in the calculation of a score to assess how much one input vector is relevant for another input vector.




Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Self Attention QKV

•
Want to create scores between all pairs of word in input sequence.

•
This is calculated by taking the dot product (cosine similarity) between every query and key vector.

•
Note that it should be highest for a word with itself.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Self Attention QKV

•
Divide scores by dimensionality normalization.

•
Pass result through softmax.

•
This score shows how relevant all other words are to the word at the current position.

•
In ML, a lot of operations are done to enable stable gradient flow.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Self Attention QKV

•
Multiply each value vector by the softmax score

•
Sum up the weighted value vectors.

•
This weighted sum vector is the output of the self attention layer for a specific word.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Self Attention Matrix Perspective

•
Can also collect all vectors at once into a single matrix, by multiplying by the weight matrices that generate Q,K, and V.

•
Faster implementation time.






Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Multi-Headed Attention

•
Use many self attention layers in parallel and combine all their output vectors.

•
Expands ability to focus on different positions





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Multi-Headed Attention

•
Creates many different representation subspaces all for the same input sequence.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Bring it all Together

Results after Training



Different attention heads can arrive at different weighting strategies.


Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

How do we choose this pattern?

•
There are many different potential ways.

•
The major point is that the time embedding vectors of points closer to each other should be more similar in some way.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

How do we ensure stable training?

•
Need information to flow across different layers through residual connections.

•
Add additional normalization layers to restrict range of possible values.

How do we account for the order of words?


•
We need to add a time vector to every word that provides information about where the vector belongs in a sequence.




Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Decoding Details

•
Output of top encoder is is made into attention vectors K and V.

•
These vectors are passed into each decoder block.

•
This processed is repeated for each output.

•
Output of each step is fed into bottom decoder.

•
Continues until end character reached.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Final Layer and SoftMax

•
Linear layer is simply a layer of neurons.

•
Maps the vectors into the probability of each of the words in the vocabulary.

•
Each cell in this probability vector can represent words, characters, punctuation, and other defined language constructs.





Transformer

Vaswani, A., Shazeer, N., Parmar, N., Uszkoreit, J., Jones, L., Gomez, A. N., ... & Polosukhin, I. (2017). Attention is all you need.Advances in neural information processing systems,30.
Alammar, J., & Grootendorst, M. (2024).Hands-on large language models: language understanding and generation. " O'Reilly Media, Inc.".

Loss Function for Training

•
Cross Entropy Loss between probability vector and output vector.






Image processing as a sequence: Vision Transformer

Vision Transformer

•
Each image patch is fed sequentially into an encoder model

•
Depending on application, the embedding is either fed into a classifier like MLP (classification) or a decoder (segmentation or dense prediction)

Layer Norm

Transformer Encoder

https://wandb.ai/wandb_fc/LayerNorm/reports/Layer-Normalization-in-Pytorch-With-Examples---VmlldzoxMjk5MTk1 

•
Batch normalization: •
Batch norm is not applicable in sequence modeling since it is unknown during training how many ‘words’ will be processed per batch




•
Layer norm •
Layer norm normalizes channel-wise for a batchsize of N = 1 at inference 

•
Similar to batch norm, beta and gamma are learnable parameters




•
Advantages over Batch norm:•
Layer norm can operate with any batchsize

•
Batch norm requires storage of mean and variance during training, to be used at inference

Attention

Transformer Encoder

•
RNNs•
Process inputs using U

•
Perform state updates using W

•
Perform output updates using V

•
All three MLPs are learned




•
Transformers•
Q = Query matrix

•
K = Key matrix

•
V = Value matrix

A black text with a square and a equal sign

Description automatically generated

Attention

Transformer Encoder

•
Self-attention mechanism•
Captures relationships between input patch embeddings

•
Computes a weighted sum of all patch embeddings, where the weights are determined by the relevance of each patch to the current one

•
Multi-head attention employs multiple sets of learnable parameters (attention heads) to capture different types of relationships.




•
Transformers are trained via self supervison

What is a “Sequential Decision Making Problem” (SMDP)? Examples 
Electric Uber Driver 
• 
As a driver of an electric Uber, you have three states: 

•

• 
Battery level medium 

• 
Battery level low 

• 
Stuck (bad, cost -200) 



• 
You also have two actions 

• 
Pick up rider (reward +20) 

• 
Charge (cost -1)

Formalism: value function, policy, and q-function 
Definitions: 
The value function vt(s) for an SMDP is 
vt(s)= cumulatve cost of starting in state s at stage t and then taking the optimal actions at stages t,t+1,...,T 1. 
We keep track of the optimal actions to take using the policy: 
⇡t(s)= the optimal action to take if we are in state s at stage t. 
The q function is an intermediary that ties together the value function and the policy. It is deﬁned in English as follows 
qt(s,a)= cumulative cost of starting in state s in stage t, taking action a,andthentakingthe optimalactions thereafter at stages t+1,...,T 1. 
19 ofx 
Relationships: 
vt(s)=minqt(s, a). 
a2A 
qt(s, a)=c(s, a)+vt+1(f(s, a)). 
⇡t(s)=argminqt(s, a). 
a2A 
Combining the above we have… 
vt(s)=min c(s, a)+vt+1(f(s, a)) 
a2A 
… this is the principle of optimality

q-learning
Data-driven solutions to sequential decision making problems 
q-learning is “model free”; it learns directly from data 
• We observe data consisting of single steps: 
𝑠,𝑎,𝑟,𝑠! , 𝑛=1,2,…,𝑁 𝑠! = starting state, 𝑎! = action taken, 𝑟! = reward received, 𝑠′! = next state 
•

•
Fully-connected autoencoders:•
Does not leverage stationary 

•
Redundancy in the parameters

•
Learn from every sample as an independent feature 




•
Convolutional autoencoders:•
Preserving spatial locality

•
Sharing weights across different locations. 

•
Reconstruction based on the local latent features 











convolutional layers



Autoencoders

Convolutional Autoencoders


Encoder: (dimension decreases)
•
Convolution (with stride)

•
or Convolution + Pooling


Decoder: (dimension increases)
•
Unpooling + Convolution

•
or Transposed Convolution with stride


(learning the “Unpooling + Convolution”)

















Autoencoders

Convolutional Autoencoders


Convolutional autoencoder: 

Conv 1
16x28x28 

Conv 2
8x14x14 





Maxpool
 16x14x14



ConvTranspose 3
16x14x14 


ConvTranspose 4
1x28x28 






Encoder:
•
Conv 1: 16 kernels of 3x3, padding=1 (ReLU)

•
Maxpooling: stride = 2

•
Conv 2: 8 kernels of 3x3, padding=1 (ReLU)

•
Maxpooling: with stride = 2



Decoder:
•
Transposed conv 3: 16 kernels of 2x2, stride=2 (ReLU)

•
Transposed conv 4: 1 kernels of 2x2, stride=2 (Sigmoid)

Anomaly Detection








[1] V. Chandola, A. Banerjee, V. Kumar. "Anomaly detection: A survey". ACM Comput. Surv. 41, 3, Article 15 (July 2009), 58 pages

Anomaly Detection

Definition











Problem Formulation

Anomaly Detection in Images

Normal data

Anomalies


Problem Formulation

Anomaly Detection in a Statistical Framework


•
Most algorithms are composed of: •
 A statistic that has a known response to normal data (e.g., the average, the sample variance, the log-likelihood, the confidence of a classifier, an “anomaly score”…) 

•
A decision rule to analyze the statistic (e.g., an adaptive threshold, a confidence region) 







Problem Formulation

Anomaly Detection in a Statistical Framework





Problem Formulation

Anomaly Detection in a Statistical Framework


Performance Measures

TPR/FPR








Performance Measures

TPR/FPR Tradeoff

Performance Measures

TPR/FPR Tradeoff


•
Comparing different methods might be tricky since we have to make sure that both have been configured in their best conditions 

•
Testing a large number of parameters lead to the ROC (receiver operating characteristic) curve 

•
The ideal detector would achieve:  FPR = 0%,  TPR = 100% 

•
Thus, the closer to (0,1) the better 

•
The larger the Area Under the Curve (AUC), the better

•
The optimal parameter is the one yielding the point closest to (0,1)




Performance Measures

Area Under Curve (AUC)

TPR


Fit a statistical model to the observation to describe dependence, apply anomaly detection on the independent residuals.
•
Detection is performed by using a model ℳ which can encode and reconstruct normal data:•
During training: learn the model ℳ from training set TR

•
During testing: 





− Encode and reconstruct each test signal 𝒔 through ℳ
− Assess err(𝒔), namely the residual between 𝒔 and its reconstruction through ℳ
•
The rationale is that ℳ can reconstruct only normal data, thus anomalies are expected to yield large reconstruction errors.

Dijkstra's Algorithm

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Not to be confused with Dykstra's projection algorithm.

Dijkstra's algorithm finds the shortest paths between nodes in a weighted graph, such as a road network. It picks the unvisited vertex with the lowest distance, calculates the distance through it to each unvisited neighbor, and updates the neighbor's distance if smaller. The vertex is marked visited when done with its neighbors.

Class: Search algorithm, Greedy algorithm, Dynamic programming
Data structure: Graph, usually used with priority queue or heap for optimization
Worst-case performance: Theta(|E| + |V| log |V|), where |E| is the number of edges and |V| is the number of vertices

The algorithm was conceived by computer scientist Edsger W. Dijkstra in 1956 and published three years later. It finds the shortest path from a given source node to every other node. It can also find the shortest path to a specific destination node by terminating once the destination's shortest path is determined. For example, if nodes represent cities and edge costs represent distances between cities connected by roads, the algorithm finds the shortest route from one city to all others. It is widely used in network routing protocols, such as IS-IS and OSPF, and as a subroutine in algorithms like Johnson's algorithm.

The algorithm uses a min-priority queue to select the shortest paths known so far. Originally, it ran in Theta(|V|^2) time, where |V| is the number of nodes. Using a Fibonacci heap priority queue, the running time was optimized to Theta(|E| + |V| log |V|), the fastest known single-source shortest-path algorithm for arbitrary directed graphs with unbounded non-negative weights. Specialized cases, like bounded or integer weights or directed acyclic graphs, can be further improved. With preprocessing, algorithms like contraction hierarchies can be significantly faster.

The algorithm is commonly used on graphs with positive integer or real number edge weights. It can be generalized to graphs with partially ordered edge weights, provided subsequent labels are monotonically non-decreasing.

In fields like artificial intelligence, Dijkstra's algorithm or its variants are used for uniform cost search, formulated as a best-first search.

Curse of Dimensionality

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Part of a series on Machine Learning and Data Mining

The curse of dimensionality refers to various phenomena that arise when analyzing and organizing data in high-dimensional spaces that do not occur in low-dimensional settings, such as the three-dimensional physical space of everyday experience. The expression was coined by Richard E. Bellman when considering problems in dynamic programming. The curse generally refers to issues that arise when the number of datapoints is small relative to the intrinsic dimension of the data.

Dimensionally cursed phenomena occur in domains such as numerical analysis, sampling, combinatorics, machine learning, data mining, and databases. The common theme of these problems is that when the dimensionality increases, the volume of the space increases so fast that the available data become sparse. To obtain a reliable result, the amount of data needed often grows exponentially with the dimensionality. Also, organizing and searching data often relies on detecting areas where objects form groups with similar properties; in high dimensional data, however, all objects appear to be sparse and dissimilar in many ways, which prevents common data organization strategies from being efficient.

Decision Tree

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

This article is about decision trees in decision analysis. For the use of the term in machine learning, see Decision tree learning.

Traditionally, decision trees have been created manually.

A decision tree is a decision support recursive partitioning structure that uses a tree-like model of decisions and their possible consequences, including chance event outcomes, resource costs, and utility. It is one way to display an algorithm that only contains conditional control statements.

Decision trees are commonly used in operations research, specifically in decision analysis, to help identify a strategy most likely to reach a goal, but are also a popular tool in machine learning.

Overview
A decision tree is a flowchart-like structure in which each internal node represents a test on an attribute (e.g., whether a coin flip comes up heads or tails), each branch represents the outcome of the test, and each leaf node represents a class label (decision taken after computing all attributes). The paths from root to leaf represent classification rules.

In decision analysis, a decision tree and the closely related influence diagram are used as a visual and analytical decision support tool, where the expected values (or expected utility) of competing alternatives are calculated.

A decision tree consists of three types of nodes:
- Decision nodes - typically represented by squares
- Chance nodes - typically represented by circles
- End nodes - typically represented by triangles

Decision trees are commonly used in operations research and operations management. If decisions must be made online with no recall under incomplete knowledge, a decision tree should be paired with a probability model as a best choice model or online selection model algorithm. Another use of decision trees is as a descriptive means for calculating conditional probabilities.

Decision trees, influence diagrams, utility functions, and other decision analysis tools and methods are taught to undergraduate students in schools of business, health economics, and public health, and are examples of operations research or management science methods. These tools are also used to predict decisions of householders in normal and emergency scenarios.

Entropy (Information Theory)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In information theory, the entropy of a random variable quantifies the average level of uncertainty or information associated with the variable's potential states or possible outcomes. This measures the expected amount of information needed to describe the state of the variable, considering the distribution of probabilities across all potential states. For a discrete random variable X with possible values in a set and distributed according to a probability function p(x), the entropy is defined as the negative sum of p(x) times the logarithm of p(x) over all possible values x. The choice of logarithm base varies: base 2 gives units of bits, base e gives natural units (nats), and base 10 gives units of dits, bans, or hartleys. An equivalent definition of entropy is the expected value of the self-information of a variable.

Two bits of entropy: In the case of two fair coin tosses, the information entropy in bits is the base-2 logarithm of the number of possible outcomes. With two coins, there are four possible outcomes, yielding two bits of entropy. Generally, information entropy is the average amount of information conveyed by an event, considering all possible outcomes.

The concept of information entropy was introduced by Claude Shannon in his 1948 paper "A Mathematical Theory of Communication" and is also referred to as Shannon entropy. Shannon's theory defines a data communication system with three elements: a source of data, a communication channel, and a receiver. The fundamental problem of communication, as expressed by Shannon, is for the receiver to identify what data was generated by the source based on the signal received through the channel. Shannon proved in his source coding theorem that entropy represents an absolute mathematical limit on how well data can be losslessly compressed onto a perfectly noiseless channel. He strengthened this result for noisy channels in his noisy-channel coding theorem.

Entropy in information theory is analogous to entropy in statistical thermodynamics. When the values of the random variable designate energies of microstates, Gibbs's formula for entropy is formally identical to Shannon's formula. Entropy is relevant to areas such as combinatorics and machine learning.

Introduction
The core idea of information theory is that the informational value of a message depends on how surprising its content is. A highly likely event carries little information, while a highly unlikely event is more informative. For example, knowing a particular number will not win a lottery provides little information, as most numbers are unlikely to win. However, knowing a number will win is highly informative due to its low probability.

The information content, or surprisal, of an event E is a function that increases as the probability p(E) decreases. When p(E) is close to 1, the surprisal is low; when close to 0, it is high. This is described by the function log(1/p(E)), where log is the logarithm, giving zero surprise when the probability is 1. Thus, the surprisal of an event E is defined as negative log(p(E)) or equivalently log(1/p(E)).

Entropy measures the expected amount of information conveyed by identifying the outcome of a random trial. Rolling a die has higher entropy than tossing a coin because each die outcome has a smaller probability (1/6) than each coin outcome (1/2).

For a coin with probability p of heads and 1-p of tails, the maximum surprise occurs when p = 1/2, where outcomes are equally likely, yielding one bit of entropy. When p = 0 or p = 1, there is no uncertainty, and entropy is zero bits. Other values of p give entropies between zero and one bits.

Example
Information theory calculates the smallest amount of information required to convey a message, as in data compression. For example, consider transmitting sequences of the 4 characters 'A', 'B', 'C', and 'D' over a binary channel. If all letters are equally likely (25%), each requires two bits to encode (e.g., 'A' as '00', 'B' as '01', 'C' as '10', 'D' as '11'). If probabilities are unequal, say 'A' at 70%, 'B' at 26%, 'C' and 'D' at 2% each, variable-length codes can be used: 'A' as '0', 'B' as '10', 'C' as '110', 'D' as '111'. This reduces the average bits needed, as 70% of the time only one bit is sent. The entropy, calculated as the sum of probability-weighted log probabilities, captures this effect.

English text has low entropy, being fairly predictable. For example, 'e' is more common than 'z', and 'qu' is more common than other 'q' combinations. English text has between 0.6 and 1.3 bits of entropy per character.

Definition
Shannon defined the entropy H of a discrete random variable X, taking values in a set and distributed according to a probability function p(x), as the expected value of the information content, or negative log p(X). Explicitly, it is the negative sum of p(x) times log base b of p(x) over all x, where b is typically 2 (bits), e (nats), or 10 (bans). When p(x) = 0, the term 0 log(0) is taken as 0, consistent with the limit as p approaches 0.

Conditional entropy of two variables X and Y is the remaining randomness in X given Y, defined as the negative sum over all pairs (x, y) of the joint probability p(x, y) times the log of the ratio p(x, y)/p(y).

Measure Theory
Entropy can be defined using measure theory. For a probability space, the surprisal of an event A is negative ln of its probability. The expected surprisal is the probability times the surprisal. The entropy of a partition is the sum of the expected surprisals of its events. The entropy of a sigma-algebra is the supremum of the entropies of all partitions within it. The entropy of the probability space is the entropy of the sigma-algebra of all measurable subsets.


Greedy Algorithm

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Greedy algorithms determine the minimum number of coins to give while making change. For example, to represent 36 cents using coins with values 1, 5, 10, and 20, one would choose the coin of the highest value less than the remaining change owed at each step. This is a local optimum. In general, the change-making problem requires dynamic programming for an optimal solution, but most currency systems allow the greedy strategy to find an optimal solution.

A greedy algorithm follows the heuristic of making the locally optimal choice at each stage. In many problems, a greedy strategy does not produce an optimal solution, but it can yield locally optimal solutions that approximate a globally optimal solution in a reasonable amount of time.

For example, a greedy strategy for the travelling salesman problem, which is computationally complex, is to visit the nearest unvisited city at each step. This heuristic terminates quickly but does not aim to find the best solution, as finding an optimal solution typically requires many steps.

In mathematical optimization, greedy algorithms optimally solve combinatorial problems with matroid properties and provide constant-factor approximations for optimization problems with submodular structure.

Specifics
Greedy algorithms produce good solutions for some problems but not others. Most problems where they work have two properties:

Greedy Choice Property
The best choice at a given moment can be made, and the remaining sub-problems can be solved recursively. The choice depends on past choices but not future ones or all subproblem solutions. A greedy algorithm iteratively makes one choice after another, reducing the problem without reconsidering choices. This differs from dynamic programming, which is exhaustive, considers all previous decisions, and may reconsider paths.

Optimal Substructure
A problem has optimal substructure if an optimal solution contains optimal solutions to its sub-problems.

Correctness Proofs
A common technique for proving the correctness of greedy algorithms uses an inductive exchange argument. This demonstrates that any solution different from the greedy solution can be transformed into the greedy solution without worsening quality. The proof typically follows these steps (by contradiction):

- Assume an optimal solution differs from the greedy solution.
- Identify the first point where they differ.
- Prove that exchanging the optimal choice for the greedy choice does not worsen the solution.
- Conclude by induction that an optimal solution identical to the greedy solution exists.
- Sometimes, prove that no optimal solution can strictly improve upon the greedy solution.


Graph (Abstract Data Type)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In computer science, a graph is an abstract data type that implements the undirected and directed graph concepts from graph theory in mathematics.

A graph data structure consists of a finite, possibly mutable set of vertices (also called nodes or points), together with a set of unordered pairs of these vertices for an undirected graph or a set of ordered pairs for a directed graph. These pairs are known as edges (also called links or lines), and for a directed graph, they are sometimes called arrows or arcs. Vertices may be part of the graph structure or external entities represented by integer indices or references.

A graph data structure may associate a value with each edge, such as a symbolic label or a numeric attribute (e.g., cost, capacity, length).

Operations
The basic operations provided by a graph data structure G usually include:
- adjacent(G, x, y): Tests whether there is an edge from vertex x to vertex y.
- neighbors(G, x): Lists all vertices y such that there is an edge from vertex x to vertex y.
- add_vertex(G, x): Adds vertex x, if it is not there.
- remove_vertex(G, x): Removes vertex x, if it is there.
- add_edge(G, x, y, z): Adds edge z from vertex x to vertex y, if it is not there.
- remove_edge(G, x, y): Removes the edge from vertex x to vertex y, if it is there.
- get_vertex_value(G, x): Returns the value associated with vertex x.
- set_vertex_value(G, x, v): Sets the value associated with vertex x to v.

Structures that associate values to edges usually also provide:
- get_edge_value(G, x, y): Returns the value associated with the edge (x, y).
- set_edge_value(G, x, y, v): Sets the value associated with the edge (x, y) to v.

Common Data Structures for Graph Representation
Adjacency List
Vertices are stored as records or objects, and every vertex stores a list of adjacent vertices. This allows storage of additional data on vertices. If edges are stored as objects, each vertex stores its incident edges, and each edge stores its incident vertices.

Adjacency Matrix
A two-dimensional matrix where rows represent source vertices and columns represent destination vertices. Data on edges and vertices must be stored externally. Only the cost for one edge can be stored between each pair of vertices.

Incidence Matrix
A two-dimensional matrix where rows represent vertices and columns represent edges. The entries indicate the incidence relation between the vertex at a row and the edge at a column.

Time Complexity of Operations
The following table shows the time complexity of performing various operations on graphs for different representations, where |V| is the number of vertices and |E| is the number of edges. In matrix representations, entries encode the cost of following an edge, with absent edges assumed to have infinite cost.

Operation                 Adjacency List        Adjacency Matrix      Incidence Matrix
Store graph              O(|V| + |E|)          O(|V|^2)              O(|V| * |E|)
Add vertex               O(1)                  O(|V|^2)              O(|V| * |E|)
Add edge                 O(1)                  O(1)                  O(|V| * |E|)
Remove vertex            O(|E|)                O(|V|^2)              O(|V| * |E|)
Remove edge              O(|V|)                O(1)                  O(|V| * |E|)
Are vertices x and y adjacent?  O(|V|)         O(1)                  O(|E|)
Remarks                  Slow to remove vertices and edges  Slow to add or remove vertices  Slow to add or remove vertices and edges

Adjacency lists are preferred for sparse graphs, while adjacency matrices are preferred for dense graphs (where the number of edges is close to the number of vertices squared) or when quick lookup of edge existence is needed.

More Efficient Representation of Adjacency Sets
The time complexity of operations in the adjacency list representation can be improved by using more efficient data structures, such as hash tables or balanced binary search trees (the latter requires vertices to be identified by elements of a linearly ordered set, like integers or strings). Using hash tables leads to an amortized average time complexity of O(1) to test adjacency of two vertices and remove an edge, and O(deg(x)) to remove a vertex x of degree deg(x). The time complexity of other operations and the asymptotic space requirement remain unchanged.

Cosine Similarity

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Part of a series on Machine Learning and Data Mining

In data analysis, cosine similarity is a measure of similarity between two non-zero vectors in an inner product space. It is the cosine of the angle between the vectors, calculated as the dot product of the vectors divided by the product of their lengths. Cosine similarity depends only on the angle between vectors, not their magnitudes, and ranges from -1 to +1. Proportional vectors have a similarity of +1, orthogonal vectors have a similarity of 0, and opposite vectors have a similarity of -1. In contexts where vector components are non-negative, such as text analysis, the similarity ranges from 0 to 1.

In information retrieval and text mining, each word is assigned a coordinate, and a document is represented by a vector of word occurrence counts. Cosine similarity measures how similar two documents are in terms of subject matter, independent of document length.

The technique is also used to measure cohesion within clusters in data mining.

One advantage of cosine similarity is its low complexity, especially for sparse vectors, as only non-zero coordinates are considered.

Other names for cosine similarity include Orchini similarity, Tucker coefficient of congruence, and Otsuka-Ochiai similarity, which applies cosine similarity to binary data.

Definition
The cosine of two non-zero vectors is derived using the Euclidean dot product formula: the dot product equals the product of the vectors' magnitudes times the cosine of the angle between them.

For two n-dimensional vectors A and B, the cosine similarity is the dot product of A and B divided by the product of their magnitudes. It is calculated as the sum of the products of corresponding components divided by the square root of the sum of squared components of A times the square root of the sum of squared components of B.

The similarity ranges from -1 (exactly opposite) to +1 (exactly the same), with 0 indicating orthogonality or decorrelation, and intermediate values indicating partial similarity or dissimilarity.

In text matching, vectors A and B are typically term frequency vectors of documents. Cosine similarity normalizes document length, ranging from 0 to 1 since term frequencies are non-negative, even with TF-IDF weights, as the angle between term frequency vectors cannot exceed 90 degrees.

If vectors are normalized by subtracting their means, the measure is called centered cosine similarity, equivalent to the Pearson correlation coefficient.

Cosine Distance
The cosine distance is often defined as 1 minus the cosine similarity. This is not a true distance metric, as it does not satisfy the triangle inequality or the coincidence axiom, being proportional to squared Euclidean distance. To satisfy the triangle inequality while maintaining the same ordering, one can use the square root of 2 times (1 minus cosine similarity) or the angular distance, defined as the arccosine of the cosine similarity.

Angular Distance and Similarity
The angular distance, a formal distance metric, is calculated from the cosine similarity. For vectors with positive or negative elements, it is the arccosine of the cosine similarity divided by pi. The angular similarity is 1 minus the angular distance.

For vectors with only positive elements, the angular distance is 2 times the arccosine of the cosine similarity divided by pi, and the angular similarity is 1 minus this value. Computing the arccosine is computationally expensive, making angular distance less efficient than the common cosine distance.

L2-Normalized Euclidean Distance
An alternative to cosine distance is L2 normalization of vectors followed by Euclidean distance. Each vector component is divided by the vector's magnitude to yield a unit-length vector. The Euclidean distance between these vectors is a proper metric, gives the same ordering as cosine distance, and avoids trigonometric operations. This allows use of standard Euclidean space techniques, such as dimensionality reduction.

Otsuka-Ochiai Coefficient
In biology, the Otsuka-Ochiai coefficient, named after Yanosuke Otsuka and Akira Ochiai, measures similarity between sets. It is the size of the intersection of sets A and B divided by the square root of the product of their sizes. For bit vectors, this is equivalent to cosine similarity. The coefficient is also known as the Ochiai-Barkman or Ochiai coefficient and is identical to a score introduced by Godfrey Thomson.

Euclidean Distance

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In mathematics, the Euclidean distance between two points in Euclidean space is the length of the line segment between them. It is calculated from the Cartesian coordinates of the points using the Pythagorean theorem, sometimes called the Pythagorean distance.

Named after the ancient Greek mathematicians Euclid and Pythagoras, the term reflects their contributions. In Euclid's geometry, distances were represented as equal-length line segments rather than numbers. The connection to the Pythagorean theorem for distance calculation was established in the 18th century.

The distance between non-point objects is typically the smallest distance between any pair of points from the two objects. Formulas exist for distances between various object types, such as from a point to a line. In advanced mathematics, distance concepts are generalized to abstract metric spaces, and other distances are studied. In some statistical and optimization applications, the square of the Euclidean distance is used.

Distance Formulas
One Dimension
The distance between two points on the real line is the absolute value of their coordinate difference. For points p and q, the distance is the absolute value of p minus q. Alternatively, it can be expressed as the square root of (p minus q) squared, which generalizes to higher dimensions and ensures positive distances.

Two Dimensions
In the Euclidean plane, for point p with coordinates (p1, p2) and point q with coordinates (q1, q2), the distance is the square root of (p1 minus q1) squared plus (p2 minus q2) squared. This follows from the Pythagorean theorem applied to a right triangle with the line segment from p to q as the hypotenuse. Using the hypot function, the distance can be written as hypot(p1 minus q1, p2 minus q2).

For points in polar coordinates, with p at (r, theta) and q at (s, psi), the distance is the square root of r squared plus s squared minus 2 times r times s times the cosine of (theta minus psi), derived from the law of cosines.

In the complex plane, for points p and q as complex numbers, the distance is the absolute value of p minus q, using the complex norm.

Higher Dimensions
In three dimensions, the distance between points p and q with Cartesian coordinates is the square root of (p1 minus q1) squared plus (p2 minus q2) squared plus (p3 minus q3) squared.

In n-dimensional Euclidean space, the distance is the square root of the sum of (pi minus qi) squared for i from 1 to n. This can be expressed compactly as the Euclidean norm of the vector difference p minus q.

Objects Other Than Points
For non-point objects, the distance is typically the smallest distance between any two points from the objects. More complex generalizations, like Hausdorff distance, are also used. Specific formulas include:
- Distance from a point to a line in the Euclidean plane.
- Distance from a point to a plane in three-dimensional space.
- Distance between two lines in three-dimensional space.
- Distance from a point to a curve, used to define parallel curves where all points are equidistant from the given curve.


Mahalanobis Distance

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

The Mahalanobis distance measures the distance between a point P and a probability distribution D, introduced by P. C. Mahalanobis in 1936. It first appeared mathematically in the Journal of The Asiatic Society of Bengal in 1936, motivated by identifying similarities in skull measurements from earlier studies in 1922 and 1927. R.C. Bose later derived its sampling distribution, assuming equal dispersion.

It generalizes the squared standard score, which measures how many standard deviations a point is from the mean of a distribution. The Mahalanobis distance is zero when the point is at the distribution's mean and increases as the point moves away along each principal component axis. If these axes are scaled to unit variance, the Mahalanobis distance equals the Euclidean distance in the transformed space. It is unitless, scale-invariant, and accounts for data correlations.

Definition
For a probability distribution Q in N-dimensional space with mean vector mu (mu1, mu2, ..., muN) and positive semi-definite covariance matrix Sigma, the Mahalanobis distance of a point x (x1, x2, ..., xN) from Q is the square root of (x minus mu) transpose times the inverse of Sigma times (x minus mu).

For two points x and y in N-dimensional space, the Mahalanobis distance between them with respect to Q is the square root of (x minus y) transpose times the inverse of Sigma times (x minus y). Thus, the distance from a point to the distribution's mean is equivalent to the distance between the point and the mean vector.

Since Sigma is positive semi-definite, its inverse is also positive semi-definite, ensuring the square roots are defined.

Using the spectral theorem, Sigma can be decomposed as S transpose times S, where S is a real N by N matrix, such as the symmetric square root of Sigma (the standard deviation matrix). This gives an equivalent definition: the Mahalanobis distance is the Euclidean norm of S inverse times (x minus y), representing the Euclidean distance after a whitening transformation. The matrix S is not unique, and different choices offer various theoretical and practical advantages.

In practice, Q is typically the sample distribution from independent and identically distributed samples, with mu as the sample mean and Sigma as the sample covariance matrix.

When the samples' affine span is not the entire N-dimensional space, the covariance matrix is not positive-definite, making the standard definition inapplicable. However, the Mahalanobis distance is preserved under any full-rank affine transformation of the samples' affine span. In such cases, samples can be orthogonally projected to an n-dimensional space, where n is the dimension of the affine span, and the Mahalanobis distance computed as usual.

Intuitive Explanation
To estimate the probability that a test point in N-dimensional Euclidean space belongs to a set, given sample points from that set, we first find the centroid or center of mass of the samples. Intuitively, a point closer to this center is more likely to belong to the set.

We also need to assess whether the set is spread over a large or small range to determine if a distance from the center is significant. A simple approach estimates the standard deviation of sample distances from the center. If a test point is within one standard deviation, it is likely part of the set; further distances suggest it is not.

This approach assumes a spherical distribution, but if the distribution is non-spherical, like an ellipsoid, the probability depends on both distance and direction. Points must be closer along short ellipsoid axes and can be farther along long axes.

The Mahalanobis distance quantifies this by using the covariance matrix to model the ellipsoid representing the set's distribution. It measures the distance from the test point to the center of mass, adjusted by the ellipsoid's width in the test point's direction.

Mutual Information

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In probability theory and information theory, mutual information (MI) measures the mutual dependence between two random variables. It quantifies the amount of information (in units like bits, nats, or hartleys) gained about one random variable by observing the other. Closely tied to entropy, which measures a random variable's expected information, MI is more general than the correlation coefficient, capturing non-linear dependencies and differences between the joint distribution of the pair (X, Y) and the product of their marginal distributions. MI is the expected value of pointwise mutual information (PMI).

Defined by Claude Shannon in his 1948 paper "A Mathematical Theory of Communication," the term "mutual information" was later coined by Robert Fano. It is also known as information gain.

Definition
For a pair of random variables (X, Y) with values over the space X times Y, with joint distribution P(X,Y) and marginal distributions PX and PY, mutual information is the Kullback-Leibler divergence of P(X,Y) from the product distribution PX times PY.

Expressed using entropy H and conditional entropy H( | ), mutual information is the entropy of X minus the conditional entropy of X given Y, or equivalently, the entropy of Y minus the conditional entropy of Y given X.

MI is zero when the joint distribution equals the product of the marginals, meaning X and Y are independent, and observing Y provides no information about X. MI is non-negative, measuring the cost of encoding (X, Y) as independent when they are not.

With the natural logarithm, MI is in nats; with base-2 logarithm, it is in bits (shannons); with base-10 logarithm, it is in hartleys (bans or dits).

In Terms of PMFs for Discrete Distributions
For jointly discrete random variables X and Y, mutual information is a double sum over all x in X and y in Y of the joint probability mass function P(X,Y)(x,y) times the logarithm of P(X,Y)(x,y) divided by PX(x) times PY(y).

In Terms of PDFs for Continuous Distributions
For jointly continuous random variables, the double sum becomes a double integral over X and Y of the joint probability density function P(X,Y)(x,y) times the logarithm of P(X,Y)(x,y) divided by PX(x) times PY(y), with respect to dx and dy.

Motivation
Mutual information measures the information shared by X and Y, indicating how much knowing one variable reduces uncertainty about the other. If X and Y are independent, knowing X provides no information about Y, and MI is zero. If X is a deterministic function of Y and vice versa, MI equals the entropy of X or Y, as knowing one fully determines the other. When X and Y are the same, MI is the variable's entropy.

MI measures the dependence in the joint distribution of X and Y relative to their marginal distributions assuming independence. MI is zero if and only if X and Y are independent, as the joint probability equals the product of marginal probabilities, making the logarithm term zero. MI is non-negative and symmetric, meaning I(X;Y) equals I(Y;X).


Kullback-Leibler Divergence

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In mathematical statistics, the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, also called relative entropy or I-divergence, measures how much a model probability distribution Q differs from a true probability distribution P. It is defined as the sum over all x in the sample space of P(x) times the logarithm of P(x) divided by Q(x).

KL divergence represents the expected excess surprise when using Q instead of P when the true distribution is P. Although it measures the difference between distributions, it is not a true metric, as it is not symmetric and does not satisfy the triangle inequality. In information geometry, it is a divergence, generalizing squared distance, and for some distributions, like exponential families, it satisfies a generalized Pythagorean theorem. It is always non-negative, equaling zero only when P and Q are identical.

KL divergence has theoretical applications, such as characterizing relative entropy in information systems, randomness in time-series, and information gain in statistical models, and practical uses in applied statistics, fluid mechanics, neuroscience, bioinformatics, and machine learning.

Introduction and Context
For probability distributions P and Q, P typically represents data or observations, while Q is a model or approximation. KL divergence is the average extra bits needed to encode samples of P using a code optimized for Q instead of P. The roles of P and Q can be reversed in computations like the expectation-maximization algorithm or evidence lower bound.

Etymology
Introduced by Solomon Kullback and Richard Leibler in 1951 as the mean information for discrimination between two hypotheses, it was initially denoted I(1:2). They defined the symmetrized divergence J(1,2) as I(1:2) plus I(2:1), previously used by Harold Jeffreys in 1948. Kullback later called the symmetric form "divergence" and the asymmetric forms "directed divergences," preferring "discrimination information." The term "divergence" distinguishes it from a metric, as it does not satisfy the triangle inequality.

Definition
For discrete probability distributions P and Q on the same sample space, KL divergence from Q to P is the sum over all x of P(x) times the logarithm of P(x) divided by Q(x), or equivalently, the negative sum of P(x) times the logarithm of Q(x) divided by P(x). It is the expectation of the logarithmic difference between P and Q, taken with respect to P.

KL divergence is defined only if Q(x) equals zero implies P(x) equals zero (absolute continuity); otherwise, it is often set to infinity. When P(x) is zero, the term contributes zero, as the limit of x times log(x) as x approaches zero is zero.

For continuous random variables, KL divergence is the integral from negative to positive infinity of p(x) times the logarithm of p(x) divided by q(x), where p and q are the probability densities functions of P and Q.

For general probability measures P and Q on a measurable space, with P absolutely continuous with respect to Q, KL divergence is the integral of the logarithm of the Radon-Nikodym derivative of P with respect to Q, weighted by P. This derivative is the function r such that P(dx) equals r(x) times Q(dx). Alternatively, it is the integral of r times the logarithm of r times Q(dx). If P and Q have densities p and q with respect to a measure mu, KL divergence is the integral of p(x) times the logarithm of p(x) divided by q(x) with respect to mu.

The logarithm is typically base 2 for bits or base e for nats, but formulas hold regardless of the base. A measure mu always exists, such as the average of P and Q, though context-specific measures like counting or Lebesgue measure are often used.

KL divergence is often called the divergence from Q to P, reflecting Bayesian inference's asymmetry from prior Q to posterior P. It is also referred to as the relative entropy of P with respect to Q or the information gain from P over Q.


Cross-Entropy

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In information theory, cross-entropy between two probability distributions p and q over the same set of events measures the average number of bits needed to identify an event drawn from the set when using a coding scheme optimized for an estimated distribution q, rather than the true distribution p.

Definition
The cross-entropy of distribution q relative to distribution p is the negative expected value of the logarithm of q, taken with respect to p.

It can be expressed using the Kullback-Leibler divergence DKL(p || q), which measures the divergence of p from q, as the entropy of p plus DKL(p || q).

For discrete probability distributions p and q with the same support, cross-entropy is the negative sum over all x of p(x) times the logarithm of q(x).

For continuous distributions, assuming p and q are absolutely continuous with respect to a reference measure r, with probability density functions P and Q, cross-entropy is the negative integral over the support of P(x) times the logarithm of Q(x) with respect to dx.

Motivation
The Kraft-McMillan theorem shows that any decodable coding scheme for a set of possibilities {x1, ..., xn} implies a probability distribution q(xi) = (1/2)^li, where li is the code length for xi in bits. Cross-entropy is the expected message length per datum when assuming the wrong distribution q while the data follows p. The expectation is taken over p, reflecting the true distribution.

Estimation
When the true distribution p is unknown, as in language modeling, cross-entropy is estimated. A model is trained on a set T, and its cross-entropy is measured on a test set to assess prediction accuracy. Here, p is the true word distribution, and q is the model's predicted distribution. Since p is unknown, cross-entropy is estimated as the negative sum over N test set words of (1/N) times the base-2 logarithm of q(xi), where q(xi) is the model's probability estimate for the i-th word xi. This is a Monte Carlo estimate, treating the test set as samples from p.


Data Structure

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In computer science, a data structure is a data organization and storage format chosen for efficient access to data. It is a collection of data values, their relationships, and the functions or operations that can be applied to the data, forming an algebraic structure about data.

Usage
Data structures are the basis for abstract data types (ADT), where the ADT defines the logical form of the data type, and the data structure implements its physical form.

Different data structures suit different applications, with some specialized for specific tasks. For example, relational databases often use B-tree indexes for data retrieval, while compilers typically use hash tables for identifier lookup.

Data structures enable efficient management of large datasets, such as in databases and internet indexing services. Efficient data structures are crucial for designing efficient algorithms. Some design methods and programming languages prioritize data structures over algorithms as the key organizing factor in software design. Data structures organize storage and retrieval in both main and secondary memory.

Implementation
Data structures can be implemented in various programming languages, all aiming to efficiently organize and store data. They rely on a computer's ability to fetch and store data at any memory location, specified by a pointer—a bit string representing a memory address that can be stored and manipulated. Arrays and records use arithmetic operations to compute data item addresses, while linked data structures store addresses within the structure itself. This impacts algorithm efficiency and scalability, with contiguous array memory enabling fast access and modification for sequential processing.

Implementing a data structure involves writing procedures to create and manipulate its instances. The efficiency of a data structure is tied to these operations, motivating the concept of an abstract data type, defined by its operations and their mathematical properties, including space and time costs.



Basis (Linear Algebra)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In mathematics, a set B of elements in a vector space V is called a basis if every element of V can be uniquely written as a finite linear combination of elements of B. The coefficients of this combination are the components or coordinates of the vector with respect to B, and the elements of B are called basis vectors.

Equivalently, B is a basis if its elements are linearly independent and span V, meaning every element of V is a linear combination of B's elements. A vector space may have multiple bases, but all bases have the same number of elements, known as the dimension of the vector space.

This article focuses on finite-dimensional vector spaces, though many principles apply to infinite-dimensional spaces. Basis vectors are used in studying crystal structures and frames of reference.

Definition
A basis B of a vector space V over a field F (e.g., real numbers or complex numbers) is a linearly independent subset that spans V. It satisfies:

Linear Independence
For any finite subset {v1, ..., vm} of B, if c1 times v1 plus ... plus cm times vm equals the zero vector for some coefficients c1, ..., cm in F, then c1, ..., cm must all be zero.

Spanning Property
For any vector v in V, there exist coefficients a1, ..., an in F and vectors v1, ..., vn in B such that v equals a1 times v1 plus ... plus an times vn.

The coefficients ai are the coordinates of v with respect to B, uniquely determined by the linear independence property.

A vector space with a finite basis is finite-dimensional, where B itself can be checked for linear independence.

An ordering on basis vectors is often needed, such as for orientation or associating coefficients without explicitly naming basis elements. This requires numbering the basis elements, forming an ordered basis, which is a sequence or indexed family rather than an unstructured set.

Fourier Transform

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In mathematics, the Fourier transform (FT) is an integral transform that takes a function and outputs another function describing the presence of various frequencies in the original function. The output is a complex-valued function of frequency. The term Fourier transform refers to both this function and the operation. The output is sometimes called the frequency domain representation of the original function, analogous to decomposing a musical chord into the intensities of its pitches.

Functions localized in time have Fourier transforms spread across frequency, and vice versa, a phenomenon known as the uncertainty principle.
