The Gaussian function, critical in probability and statistics, is a key case: its Fourier transform is another Gaussian. Joseph Fourier introduced sine and cosine transforms, components of the modern Fourier transform, in his heat transfer studies, where Gaussians solve the heat equation.

The Fourier transform is defined as an improper Riemann integral, but many applications require advanced integration theory, especially with the Dirac delta function. It generalizes to multi-variable functions, transforming position space to momentum space, vital in wave studies and quantum mechanics. Further generalizations apply to functions on groups, including discrete-time Fourier transform, discrete Fourier transform, and Fourier series for periodic functions. The fast Fourier transform (FFT) is an algorithm for computing the discrete Fourier transform.

Definition
The Fourier transform of a complex-valued, Lebesgue integrable function f(x) on the real line is a complex-valued function of frequency xi, defined by the integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i times 2 pi xi x, integrated from negative infinity to infinity, for all real xi.

This produces the frequency-domain function, which is continuous and tends to zero at infinity if f(x) decays with all derivatives. The Fourier inversion theorem gives the inverse transform: f(x) equals the integral of the transform times e to the power i times 2 pi xi x, integrated from negative infinity to infinity, for all real x. These form a Fourier transform pair.

The Fourier series is analogous, decomposing a periodic function on a finite interval into a discrete sum of complex exponentials, with coefficients given by an integral. As the interval grows to infinity, this becomes the Fourier transform.

Lebesgue Integrable Functions
A function f from real numbers to complex numbers is Lebesgue integrable if the integral of its absolute value is finite. If f is integrable, its Fourier transform is well-defined, bounded, uniformly continuous, and zero at infinity. In the space of integrable functions (L1), the Fourier transform is one-to-one, but its image is hard to characterize.

The transform is not defined for square-integrable functions (L2) like 1 over the square root of (1 plus x squared), which is in L2 but not L1. However, it extends uniquely to a unitary operator on L2 via the dense subspace of functions in both L1 and L2. This extension uses regularization and limits, often treating integrals as improper rather than Lebesgue integrals.

Gaussians are dense in this subspace, and their Fourier transform properties (e.g., a Gaussian transforms to a Gaussian) help prove the transform's unitarity. The L1 Fourier transform is a homomorphism of Banach algebras under convolution, mapping to continuous functions with the supremum norm.

Angular Frequency
When x represents time, xi represents frequency in hertz. The transform can use angular frequency omega (radians per second), where omega equals 2 pi xi. Substituting xi equals omega over 2 pi, the transform becomes the integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i omega x, with the inverse using a factor of 1 over 2 pi. Another convention splits the 2 pi factor evenly, making the transform unitary.

Summary of Popular Forms of the Fourier Transform, One-Dimensional
Ordinary Frequency xi (Hz), Unitary:
Transform: Integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i 2 pi xi x from negative to positive infinity.
Inverse: Integral of the transform times e to the power i 2 pi x xi from negative to positive infinity.

Angular Frequency omega (rad/s), Unitary:
Transform: 1 over square root of 2 pi times the integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i omega x.
Inverse: 1 over square root of 2 pi times the integral of the transform times e to the power i omega x.

Angular Frequency omega (rad/s), Non-Unitary:
Transform: Integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i omega x.
Inverse: 1 over 2 pi times the integral of the transform times e to the power i omega x.

Generalization for n-Dimensional Functions
Ordinary Frequency xi (Hz), Unitary:
Transform: Integral over n-dimensional real space of f(x) times e to the power negative i 2 pi xi dot x.
Inverse: Integral over n-dimensional real space of the transform times e to the power i 2 pi xi dot x.

Angular Frequency omega (rad/s), Unitary:
Transform: 1 over (2 pi) to the power n/2 times the integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i omega dot x.
Inverse: 1 over (2 pi) to the power n/2 times the integral of the transform times e to the power i omega dot x.

Angular Frequency omega (rad/s), Non-Unitary:
Transform: Integral of f(x) times e to the power negative i omega dot x.
Inverse: 1 over (2 pi) to the power n times the integral of the transform times e to the power i omega dot x.


Dimensionality Reduction

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Dimensionality reduction transforms data from a high-dimensional space to a low-dimensional space, retaining meaningful properties close to the intrinsic dimension. High-dimensional spaces are problematic due to data sparsity from the curse of dimensionality and computational intractability. It is common in fields like signal processing, speech recognition, neuroinformatics, and bioinformatics, used for noise reduction, visualization, cluster analysis, or as a preprocessing step.

Methods are divided into linear and nonlinear approaches, with linear approaches split into feature selection and feature extraction.

Feature Selection
Feature selection identifies a subset of input variables suitable for the task. Strategies include:
- Filter strategy, using measures like information gain.
- Wrapper strategy, guided by accuracy.
- Embedded strategy, adding or removing features during model building based on prediction errors.

Analysis like regression or classification can be more accurate in the reduced space than the original.

Feature Projection
Feature projection, or extraction, transforms data to fewer dimensions, using linear methods like principal component analysis (PCA) or nonlinear techniques.

Principal Component Analysis (PCA)
PCA linearly maps data to a lower-dimensional space to maximize variance. It constructs the covariance or correlation matrix, computes eigenvectors, and uses those with the largest eigenvalues (principal components) to reconstruct most of the original data's variance. The first few eigenvectors often capture large-scale system behavior, though this varies by system. PCA reduces the original space to one spanned by a few eigenvectors, with some data loss but retaining key variance.

Non-Negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)
NMF decomposes a non-negative matrix into two non-negative matrices, useful for non-negative signals like in astronomy. Developments include handling uncertainties, missing data, parallel computation, and sequential construction for stability and linearity. Sequential NMF preserves flux in imaging circumstellar structures, aiding exoplanet detection. Unlike PCA, NMF does not remove the matrix mean, preserving more information, especially for non-negative fluxes.

Kernel PCA
Kernel PCA applies PCA nonlinearly using the kernel trick, constructing mappings that maximize data variance.

Graph-Based Kernel PCA
Nonlinear techniques like Isomap, locally linear embedding (LLE), Hessian LLE, Laplacian eigenmaps, and tangent space analysis retain local data properties, defining a graph-based kernel for Kernel PCA. Maximum variance unfolding (MVU) preserves pairwise distances between nearest neighbors while maximizing distances to others. Other methods minimize differences in input-output space distances, including classical multidimensional scaling (identical to PCA), Isomap, diffusion maps, t-SNE, and curvilinear component analysis.

Autoencoders
Autoencoders, feedforward neural networks with a bottleneck hidden layer, learn nonlinear dimension reduction and codings. Training often uses greedy layer-wise pre-training with restricted Boltzmann machines, followed by backpropagation finetuning.

Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA)
LDA finds linear combinations of features to separate classes, generalizing Fisher's linear discriminant for statistics, pattern recognition, and machine learning.

Generalized Discriminant Analysis (GDA)
GDA uses kernel functions for nonlinear discriminant analysis, mapping vectors to high-dimensional feature space like support-vector machines, maximizing the ratio of between-class to within-class scatter.

t-SNE
t-Distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding (t-SNE) is a nonlinear technique for visualizing high-dimensional datasets, not ideal for clustering or outlier detection due to poor preservation of density or distances.

UMAP
Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection (UMAP) is a nonlinear technique similar to t-SNE, assuming data is uniformly distributed on a locally connected Riemannian manifold with a locally constant metric.

Dimension Reduction
For high-dimensional datasets, dimension reduction precedes k-nearest neighbors (k-NN) algorithms to mitigate the curse of dimensionality. Feature extraction and reduction can combine using PCA, LDA, canonical correlation analysis (CCA), or NMF, followed by k-NN clustering in reduced space, known as low-dimensional embedding. For very high-dimensional data like video streams or DNA, fast approximate k-NN searches using locality-sensitive hashing, random projection, or sketches are often necessary.

Applications
In neuroscience, maximally informative dimensions reduce data dimensions while preserving as much information as possible about the original dataset.


Singular Value Decomposition

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In linear algebra, singular value decomposition (SVD) factorizes a real or complex matrix into a rotation, a scaling, and another rotation. It generalizes eigendecomposition of a square normal matrix with an orthonormal eigenbasis to any m by n matrix, related to polar decomposition.

For an m by n complex matrix M, SVD is M equals U times Sigma times V conjugate transpose, where U is an m by m complex unitary matrix, Sigma is an m by n rectangular diagonal matrix with non-negative real numbers on the diagonal, V is an n by n complex unitary matrix, and V conjugate transpose is V's conjugate transpose. This decomposition exists for any complex matrix. For real M, U and V can be real orthogonal matrices, often written as U times Sigma times V transpose.

The diagonal entries of Sigma, called singular values, are uniquely determined by M. The number of non-zero singular values equals M's rank. U's columns are left-singular vectors, and V's columns are right-singular vectors, forming orthonormal bases u1 to um and v1 to vn. If sorted so zero singular values are in the highest-numbered columns, SVD can be written as the sum from i=1 to r of sigma_i times u_i times v_i conjugate transpose, where r is at most the minimum of m and n, the rank of M.

SVD is not unique, but singular values can be ordered descending, uniquely determining Sigma. Compact SVD uses a square r by r Sigma with only non-zero singular values, with U as m by r and V as n by r semi-unitary matrices.

SVD is used for computing pseudoinverses, matrix approximation, and determining rank, range, and null space, with applications in signal processing, least squares fitting, and process control.

Intuitive Interpretations

Rotation, Coordinate Scaling, and Reflection
For an m by m real square matrix M, U and V conjugate transpose are orthogonal matrices, representing rotations or reflections. Sigma scales coordinates by singular values. SVD decomposes the transformation into a rotation or reflection (V conjugate transpose), coordinate scaling (Sigma), and another rotation or reflection (U). For positive determinant, U and V can both be rotations or both include reflections; for negative, one includes a reflection; for zero, either type is possible. For non-square m by n matrices, U and V are rotations/reflections of m- and n-dimensional spaces, and Sigma scales and adjusts dimensions.

Singular Values as Semiaxes
Singular values represent the semiaxes' magnitudes of an ellipsoid in n-dimensional space for an n by n matrix, or an n-dimensional ellipsoid in m-dimensional space for an m by n matrix, with singular vectors encoding directions.

Orthonormal Bases
U and V's columns are orthonormal bases for m- and n-dimensional spaces. M maps V's i-th basis vector to sigma_i times U's i-th basis vector, with zero mappings for i beyond min(m,n).

Relation to Fundamental Subspaces
U's first r columns are a basis for M's column space, the last m-r for M conjugate transpose's null space. V's first r columns are a basis for M conjugate transpose's column space (M's row space for real M), the last n-r for M's null space.

Geometric Meaning
For a linear map T from n- to m-dimensional space, SVD provides orthonormal bases where T maps the i-th n-dimensional basis vector to a non-negative multiple of the i-th m-dimensional basis vector, sending others to zero, represented by a diagonal matrix. Geometrically, T maps a unit sphere to an ellipsoid, with singular values as semiaxes lengths. SVD decomposes T into an isometry aligning directions to axes, a diagonal scaling, and another isometry to the final ellipsoid.

Automated Reasoning

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In computer science, particularly in knowledge representation, reasoning, and metalogic, automated reasoning focuses on understanding reasoning aspects. It aims to develop computer programs that reason automatically or nearly automatically. A sub-field of artificial intelligence, it connects to theoretical computer science and philosophy.

Key subareas include automated theorem proving, interactive theorem proving, and automated proof checking, ensuring correct reasoning under fixed assumptions. Extensive work covers reasoning by analogy, induction, and abduction. Reasoning under uncertainty and non-monotonic reasoning are significant, with argumentation applying minimality and consistency constraints, as in John Pollock's OSCAR system, a specific automated argumentation system.

Tools include classical logics, fuzzy logic, Bayesian inference, maximal entropy reasoning, and ad hoc techniques.

Early Years
Formal logic significantly influenced automated reasoning, contributing to artificial intelligence. A formal proof verifies every inference back to mathematical axioms, supplying all logical steps without intuition, reducing errors. The 1957 Cornell Summer meeting, uniting logicians and computer scientists, is considered a starting point, though some trace origins to the 1955 Logic Theorist or Martin Davis' 1954 Presburger decision procedure implementation.

Automated reasoning faced an AI winter in the 1980s and early 1990s but revived. By 2005, Microsoft integrated verification technology internally, planning a logical specification language for Visual C 2012.

Significant Contributions
Principia Mathematica, by Alfred North Whitehead and Bertrand Russell, published in 1910-1913, derived mathematical expressions in symbolic logic. Logic Theorist, developed in 1956 by Allen Newell, Cliff Shaw, and Herbert Simon, mimicked human reasoning, proving 38 of 52 theorems from Principia Mathematica's chapter two, including a more elegant proof for one theorem. Their 1958 report predicted machines would soon match human problem-solving capabilities.

Examples of Formal Proofs
Year  Theorem                       Proof System  Formalizer          Traditional Proof
1986  First Incompleteness         Boyer-Moore   Shankar             Godel
1990  Quadratic Reciprocity        Boyer-Moore   Russinoff           Eisenstein
1996  Fundamental of Calculus      HOL Light     Harrison            Henstock
2000  Fundamental of Algebra       Mizar         Milewski            Brynski
2000  Fundamental of Algebra       Coq           Geuvers et al.      Kneser
2004  Four Color                   Coq           Gonthier            Robertson et al.
2004  Prime Number                 Isabelle      Avigad et al.       Selberg-Erdos
2005  Jordan Curve                 HOL Light     Hales               Thomassen
2005  Brouwer Fixed Point          HOL Light     Harrison            Kuhn
2006  Flyspeck 1                   Isabelle      Bauer-Nipkow        Hales
2007  Cauchy Residue               HOL Light     Harrison            Classical
2008  Prime Number                 HOL Light     Harrison            Analytic proof
2012  Feit-Thompson                Coq           Gonthier et al.     Bender, Glauberman, Peterfalvi
2016  Boolean Pythagorean Triples  SAT           Heule et al.        None

Proof Systems
Boyer-Moore Theorem Prover (NQTHM)
Started in 1971 at Edinburgh, NQTHM was a fully automatic theorem prover in Pure Lisp, using Lisp as logic, recursive function definitions, rewriting, symbolic evaluation, and induction heuristics.

HOL Light
Written in OCaml, HOL Light has a simple logical foundation and clean implementation for classical higher-order logic.

Coq
Developed in France, Coq is a proof assistant extracting executable programs from specifications in Objective CAML or Haskell, using the Calculus of Inductive Constructions.

Applications
Automated reasoning builds theorem provers, often requiring human guidance as proof assistants. Logic Theorist found a more efficient proof for a Principia Mathematica theorem. Automated reasoning solves problems in formal logic, mathematics, computer science, logic programming, software and hardware verification, circuit design, and more. The TPTP library, regularly updated, provides test problems, with competitions at the CADE conference selecting problems from this library.

Explainable Artificial Intelligence

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Explainable AI (XAI), also known as interpretable AI or explainable machine learning (XML), is a research field within artificial intelligence (AI) focused on methods that enable human oversight of AI algorithms. It emphasizes the reasoning behind AI decisions or predictions, making them understandable and transparent. XAI addresses the "black box" nature of machine learning, where even designers cannot explain specific decisions, ensuring safety and scrutiny in automated decision-making.

XAI aims to enhance user effectiveness by improving understanding of AI reasoning, supporting the social right to explanation or enhancing trust in AI decisions. It explains past, current, and future actions, revealing the information basis for these actions, enabling confirmation, challenge, or generation of knowledge.

Machine learning (ML) algorithms are categorized as white-box (understandable to experts) or black-box (hard to explain, even for experts). XAI algorithms adhere to transparency, interpretability, and explainability:
- Transparency: Processes for extracting model parameters and generating labels are describable.
- Interpretability: The model can be comprehended and presented understandably to humans.
- Explainability: Features contributing to a decision are identified, though a consensus definition is pending.

Interpretability focuses on understanding model outputs, while transparency includes simulatability, decomposability, and algorithmic transparency. Model functionality provides textual descriptions, visualizations, and local explanations for specific outputs. These enhance AI comprehensibility and usability, justifying decisions, tracking, verifying, improving algorithms, and discovering new facts.

White-box ML algorithms, like Concept Bottleneck Models using concept-level abstractions, can achieve high accuracy with interpretable structures, crucial in medicine, defense, finance, and law for trust and understanding. Symbolic regression, searching mathematical expressions for optimal models, is proposed for supervised learning.

AI systems optimize mathematically specified goals, but may learn inappropriate rules (e.g., associating "Daniel Day-Lewis" with positive reviews). XAI allows auditing to assess generalization to new data.

Goals
Trust is essential for human-AI cooperation. Transparency, interpretability, and explainability are steps toward comprehensive trust criteria, especially in medicine, where clinical decision support systems require understandable machine decisions. XAI prevents AI from learning undesirable tricks, like cheating by recognizing irrelevant patterns (e.g., copyright tags for horse images or false grasping in virtual environments).

The DARPA XAI program develops "glass box" models, explainable to humans without sacrificing performance, enabling trust assessment. XAI extracts knowledge from black-box models, compares models, and ensures ethical and legal compliance in monitoring systems, contrasting with opaque "black box" systems.

Explainability and Interpretability Techniques

Explainability vs. Interpretability
Term             Definition
Interpretability Level of understanding how the AI technology works
Explainability   Level of understanding how the AI system produced a result

Explainability ensures decisions avoid unfair criteria, often treating models as black boxes and analyzing input changes' effects. Techniques for classification and regression include:
- Partial dependency plots: Show a feature's marginal effect on predictions.
- SHAP: Visualizes feature contributions using Shapley values.
- Feature importance: Estimates feature significance via permutation importance.
- LIME: Approximates model outputs with a simpler interpretable model.
- Multitask learning: Provides additional outputs to deduce model learning.
- Saliency maps: Highlight influential image parts for results.

Expert systems encode domain knowledge as production rules, allowing users to query and understand reasoning. For language models like generative pretrained transformers, explanations may be unreliable. Techniques include attention analysis, probing methods, causal tracing, and circuit discovery.

Interpretability
Interpretability, or mechanistic interpretability, reverse-engineers neural networks to understand decision-making, crucial for AI safety and alignment. It studies phenomena like grokking, where models transition from overfitting to generalization. For generative pretrained transformers, it seeks automated feature identification, using compute-intensive dictionary learning to enhance safety. For convolutional neural networks, DeepDream visualizes neuron activations to reveal learned patterns.


AI Safety

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

AI safety is an interdisciplinary field dedicated to preventing accidents, misuse, or harmful consequences from artificial intelligence (AI) systems. It includes machine ethics and AI alignment to ensure AI systems are moral and beneficial, alongside monitoring risks and improving reliability. The field is particularly focused on existential risks from advanced AI models.

Beyond technical research, AI safety involves developing norms and policies to promote safety. It gained significant attention in 2023 due to rapid advances in generative AI and public concerns from researchers and CEOs about potential dangers. In 2023, the United States and United Kingdom established their own AI Safety Institutes during the AI Safety Summit, though concerns remain that safety measures lag behind AI capability development.

Motivations
Scholars address current risks like critical system failures, bias, and AI-enabled surveillance, as well as emerging risks such as technological unemployment, digital manipulation, weaponization, AI-enabled cyberattacks, and bioterrorism. Speculative risks include losing control of future artificial general intelligence (AGI) agents or AI enabling stable dictatorships.

Existential Safety
Some, like Andrew Ng in 2015, have downplayed AGI concerns, comparing them to worrying about Mars overpopulation before reaching the planet. Conversely, Stuart J. Russell advocates caution, emphasizing the need to anticipate human ingenuity.

AI researchers differ on the severity and sources of AI risks, but surveys indicate serious consideration of high-consequence risks. In surveys, median AI researchers were optimistic but assigned a 5 percent probability to extremely bad outcomes, like human extinction, from advanced AI. A 2022 survey of the natural language processing community found 37 percent agreed or weakly agreed that AI decisions could lead to a catastrophe as severe as nuclear war.


Linear Regression

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In statistics, linear regression estimates the relationship between a scalar response (dependent variable) and one or more explanatory variables (independent variables). Simple linear regression uses one explanatory variable; multiple linear regression uses two or more. This differs from multivariate linear regression, which predicts multiple correlated dependent variables.

Linear regression models relationships using linear predictor functions, with parameters estimated from data. Typically, the conditional mean of the response given the explanatory variables is an affine function, though sometimes the median or another quantile is used. It focuses on the conditional probability distribution of the response given predictors, not the joint distribution, which is multivariate analysis.

As a supervised machine learning algorithm, linear regression learns from labeled datasets to map data points to optimized linear functions for prediction. It was the first rigorously studied regression analysis, widely used due to easier parameter fitting and determinable statistical properties compared to non-linear models.

Linear regression has two main applications:
- Prediction or forecasting: Fitting a model to observed data to predict the response for new explanatory variable values.
- Explaining variation: Quantifying relationships between the response and explanatory variables, identifying variables with no linear relationship or redundant information.

Models are often fitted using least squares, but other methods include minimizing lack of fit in different norms (e.g., least absolute deviations) or penalized least squares (e.g., ridge regression with L2-norm penalty, lasso with L1-norm penalty). For datasets with large outliers, robust cost functions are preferred over Mean Squared Error (MSE), which overfits outliers. Least squares can fit non-linear models, so "least squares" and "linear model" are related but not identical.

Formulation
Given a dataset of n units with response yi and explanatory variables xi1 to xip, linear regression assumes a linear relationship between y and the vector x, modeled with an error term epsilon, an unobserved random variable adding noise. The model is yi equals beta0 plus beta1 times xi1 plus ... plus betap times xip plus epsilon_i, or in matrix form, y equals X times beta plus epsilon, where y is the response vector, X is the design matrix, beta is the parameter vector, and epsilon is the error vector.

Notation and Terminology
- y is the vector of observed response values yi, also called the regressand, endogenous, target, or dependent variable.
- X is the design matrix of explanatory variables, also called regressors, covariates, or independent variables, treated as fixed or random.
- A constant (intercept) is often included as xi0 equals 1, with beta0 as the intercept.
- Regressors can include non-linear functions of other regressors or data, as in polynomial regression, if linear in beta.
- beta is the parameter vector, with elements as regression coefficients or effects, interpreted as partial derivatives of y with respect to x.
- epsilon is the error term, capturing influences on y not from x, critical for estimation method choice.

Fitting minimizes the error term, often using the sum of squared errors.

Example
For a ball tossed upward, measuring heights hi at times ti, physics suggests hi equals beta1 times ti plus beta2 times ti squared plus epsilon_i, where beta1 is initial velocity, beta2 relates to gravity, and epsilon_i is measurement error. Linear regression estimates beta1 and beta2, linear in parameters despite non-linearity in time, expressed as hi equals xi transpose times beta plus epsilon_i, with xi as (ti, ti squared).

Assumptions
Standard linear regression assumes:
- Weak exogeneity: Predictor variables are fixed, error-free values.
- Linearity: The response mean is a linear combination of parameters and predictors, allowing flexible predictor transformations.
- Homoscedasticity: Error variance is constant across predictor values; heteroscedasticity (non-constant variance) requires methods like weighted least squares.
- Independence of errors: Errors are uncorrelated; correlated errors need methods like generalized least squares.
- No perfect multicollinearity: The design matrix X has full column rank, avoiding linear dependencies among predictors.

Violations lead to biased estimates, incorrect standard errors, or unreliable tests. Data properties, like error-predictor relationships and predictor distribution, affect estimation.

Interpretation
A fitted model identifies the relationship between a predictor xj and response y, holding other predictors fixed, with beta_j as the expected change in y per unit change in xj. This unique effect differs from the marginal effect, assessed via correlation or simple regression. Care is needed with variables like dummy variables or intercepts, and "held fixed" may reflect study design or data subset selection. Unique effects may be small despite large marginal effects if other covariates capture xj's information, or large if covariates reduce unrelated variability.

Extensions
Extensions relax assumptions:
- Simple and Multiple Linear Regression: Simple uses one predictor; multiple uses several, distinct from multivariate regression with vector responses.
- General Linear Models: Handle vector responses with a matrix of coefficients, using multivariate OLS or GLS.
- Heteroscedastic Models: Allow varying error variances, using weighted least squares or heteroscedasticity-consistent standard errors.
- Generalized Linear Models (GLMs): Model bounded or discrete responses (e.g., Poisson for counts, logistic for binary data) with a link function.
- Hierarchical Linear Models: Organize data hierarchically, regressing variables at different levels.
- Errors-in-Variables: Account for predictor measurement errors, reducing bias toward zero.


Gradient Descent

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Gradient descent is a first-order iterative algorithm for minimizing a differentiable multivariate function, widely used in machine learning to minimize cost or loss functions. It takes repeated steps in the opposite direction of the function's gradient at the current point, the direction of steepest descent. Conversely, gradient ascent maximizes the function by following the gradient. It should not be confused with local search algorithms, though both are iterative optimization methods.

Attributed to Augustin-Louis Cauchy in 1847, with Jacques Hadamard proposing a similar method in 1907, its convergence for non-linear problems was studied by Haskell Curry in 1944, gaining prominence thereafter. Stochastic gradient descent, a simple extension, is fundamental for training deep neural networks.

Description
Gradient descent leverages the fact that a differentiable multi-variable function F(x) decreases fastest in the direction of its negative gradient at a point a. Thus, updating a_n+1 equals a_n minus gamma times the gradient of F at a_n, with a small step size gamma (learning rate), ensures F(a_n) is at least F(a_n+1). Starting with an initial guess x_0, the sequence x_0, x_1, x_2, ... is generated by x_n+1 equals x_n minus gamma_n times the gradient of F at x_n, forming a monotonic sequence F(x_0) at least F(x_1) at least F(x_2) ..., converging to a local minimum. The step size gamma_n may vary per iteration.

Convergence to a local minimum is guaranteed under assumptions like convexity of F and Lipschitz continuity of its gradient, using step sizes like the Barzilai-Borwein method or those satisfying Wolfe conditions via line search. For convex functions, local minima are global, ensuring global convergence.

An Analogy
Imagine people stuck in foggy mountains, aiming to descend to the global minimum. With low visibility, they use gradient descent by measuring the hill's steepness at their position and moving downhill in the steepest direction. Measuring steepness requires a time-consuming instrument, so they must balance measurement frequency to descend before sunset. The people represent the algorithm, their path the parameter sequence, steepness the gradient, and measurement the differentiation.

Choosing Step Size and Direction
A step size gamma too small slows convergence; too large causes overshoot. Optimal step size and direction balance the angle between the negative gradient and descent direction with gradient changes along the path. Methods include:
- Using the gradient as the direction and line search (e.g., backtracking) for step size.
- Estimating gradient changes with the Hessian for twice-differentiable functions.
- Bounding gradient changes with a Lipschitz constant.
- Modeling gradient behavior to optimize step size and direction.

For convex functions, these ensure convergence to the global minimum.

Solution of a Linear System
Gradient descent solves linear systems Ax equals b, reformulated as minimizing a quadratic function F(x) equals x transpose times A times x minus 2 times x transpose times b for symmetric positive-definite A, where the gradient is 2 times (Ax minus b). For general A, linear least squares minimize F(x) equals the squared norm of Ax minus b, with gradient 2 times A transpose times (Ax minus b). An algorithm iterates: compute residual r equals b minus Ax, set step size gamma as r transpose times r divided by r transpose times A times r, update x as x plus gamma times r, and repeat until r transpose times r is small. An optimized version updates r directly, avoiding extra A multiplications.

Conjugate gradient methods are preferred, converging faster based on the square root of A's condition number, unlike gradient descent's linear dependence. Preconditioning can improve both.

Geometric Behavior
In steepest descent for Ax equals b with symmetric positive-definite A, residual vectors are orthogonal across iterations, causing a zig-zag path due to alternating extreme axes of elongated level sets, especially with high condition numbers, slowing convergence.

Solution of a Non-Linear System
Gradient descent solves non-linear systems, e.g., 3x1 minus cos(x2 x3) minus 3/2 equals 0, 4x1 squared minus 625x2 squared plus 2x2 minus 1 equals 0, exp(-x1 x2) plus 20x3 plus (10 pi minus 3)/3 equals 0. Define G(x) as the vector of these equations and minimize F(x) equals half G transpose times G. Starting at x_0 equals [0, 0, 0], one iteration computes the Jacobian J_G and updates x_1 equals x_0 minus gamma_0 times J_G transpose times G, reducing F from 58.456 to 23.306 with gamma_0 equals 0.001, continuing until convergence.

Comments
Gradient descent operates in any dimension, including infinite-dimensional function spaces using Frechet derivatives, due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality maximizing dot products when vectors are colinear. It may require many iterations if curvature varies, but preconditioning can reshape level sets for faster convergence. Momentum methods (e.g., Adam, DiffGrad) enhance performance. Newton-based methods like BFGS converge faster but are costlier per iteration, while L-BFGS suits large problems. Gradient descent differs from local search, relying on gradients, and can be viewed as Euler's method for a gradient flow.

Modifications
Fast Gradient Methods
Nesterov's fast gradient method accelerates convex problem convergence to O(1/k^2) versus gradient descent's O(1/k), optimal for first-order methods. The optimized gradient method further reduces the constant factor.

Momentum Method
The momentum or heavy ball method adds a momentum term, combining the gradient with prior updates to reduce zig-zagging, used in stochastic gradient descent and neural network training.

Extensions
Gradient descent handles constraints via projection onto constraint sets, converging if projections are efficient, as a case of the forward-backward algorithm. It is a special case of mirror descent with squared Euclidean distance.

Theoretical Properties
Convergence depends on function properties and method variants. For strongly convex, Lipschitz-smooth functions, gradient descent converges linearly with fixed step size; weaker assumptions require sophisticated step size selection for weaker guarantees.

Regularization (Mathematics)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In mathematics, statistics, finance, and computer science, particularly machine learning and inverse problems, regularization simplifies solutions to problems, often used for ill-posed problems or to prevent overfitting. It includes:

Explicit Regularization: Adding a term (prior, penalty, or constraint) to the optimization problem to ensure a unique solution, common in ill-posed problems.
Implicit Regularization: Other methods like early stopping, robust loss functions, or discarding outliers, ubiquitous in modern machine learning, including stochastic gradient descent and ensemble methods.

Explicit regularization combines a data term (likelihood) and a regularization term (prior) using Bayesian statistics to compute a posterior, stabilizing estimation. Balancing these terms aligns the solution with data or enforces regularization to avoid overfitting. In machine learning, the data term is training data, and regularization involves model choice or algorithm modifications to reduce generalization error on testing data, not training data. Tikhonov regularization (ridge regression) is an early example tied to least squares.

Regularization in Machine Learning
Machine learning aims for accurate predictions on unseen data, avoiding overfitting where models memorize training data but fail to generalize. Regularization encourages learning broad patterns, preventing overfitting and underfitting, and improving generalization using techniques like:

Early Stopping: Halts training when validation performance worsens, preventing memorization.
L1 Regularization (LASSO): Adds a penalty on the absolute value of coefficients, promoting sparse models.
L2 Regularization (Ridge Regression): Adds a penalty on squared coefficients, favoring smaller, evenly distributed weights.
Dropout: In neural networks, randomly ignores neuron subsets during training, simulating multiple architectures to enhance generalization.

Classification
Learning classifiers from finite data is underdetermined, inferring a function from examples x1, x2, ..., xn. A regularizer R(f) is added to the loss function: minimize over f the sum from i=1 to n of V(f(xi), yi) plus lambda times R(f), where V is the loss (e.g., square or hinge loss), and lambda controls regularization strength. R(f) penalizes model complexity, like smoothness or norm bounds, applying Occam's razor or Bayesian priors to favor simpler models, enhancing generalization.

Regularization purposes include simpler, sparse models or introducing group structures. Tikhonov regularization, a simple form, trades fitting data with reducing solution norms, while non-linear methods like total variation are now common.

Generalization
Regularization improves model generalizability, minimizing expected error over all inputs and labels: integral over X times Y of V(f_n(x), y) times rho(x, y) dx dy. With only noisy samples available, empirical error is used: 1/n times sum from i=1 to N of V(f_n(x_i), y_i). Without complexity bounds, models overfit noisy data. Regularization penalizes certain function space regions, improving generalization.

Tikhonov Regularization (Ridge Regression)
For a linear function f(x) equals w dot x, Tikhonov regularization adds the L2-norm of w to the loss: minimize over w the sum from i=1 to n of V(x_i dot w, y_i) plus lambda times the squared L2-norm of w. For general functions, it minimizes the sum from i=1 to n of V(f(x_i), y_i) plus lambda times the squared norm of f in its reproducing kernel Hilbert space. The L2-norm's differentiability allows gradient descent optimization.

Tikhonov-Regularized Least Squares
For least squares with Tikhonov regularization, the solution is analytical: minimize 1/n times (Xw minus Y) transpose times (Xw minus Y) plus lambda times the squared L2-norm of w. The gradient is 2/n times X transpose times (Xw minus Y) plus 2 lambda w, set to zero, yielding w equals (X transpose X plus lambda n I) inverse times X transpose Y. This takes O(d^3 + n d^2) time for training and O(n d) for testing.

Early Stopping
Early stopping regularizes by time, halting training when validation performance stops improving, using separate training, validation, and test sets. It controls model complexity, enhancing generalization.

Theoretical Motivation in Least Squares
Early stopping approximates the unregularized least squares solution using a Neumann series for an invertible matrix A with norm of I minus A less than 1. Limiting iterations T regularizes the problem, improving generalization, equivalent to restricting gradient descent iterations.

Regularizers for Sparsity
For a function f(x) equals sum from j=1 to p of phi_j(x) times w_j, sparsity in w simplifies models. The L0-norm (non-zero elements) is NP-hard, so the L1-norm approximates it via convex relaxation, inducing sparsity (e.g., LASSO): minimize 1/n times the squared norm of Xw minus Y plus lambda times the L1-norm of w.

Elastic Net Regularization
To address L1's non-unique solutions, elastic net combines L1 and L2: minimize 1/n times the squared norm of Xw minus Y plus lambda times (alpha times the L1-norm of w plus (1 minus alpha) times the squared L2-norm of w), promoting sparsity and grouping correlated features.

Proximal Methods
For L1 regularization, non-differentiability at zero is handled by proximal methods, iterating w_k+1 equals the proximal operator of gamma, R applied to w_k minus gamma times the gradient of F. For L1, this is soft-thresholding, efficiently computable.

Group Sparsity Without Overlaps
For non-overlapping groups, regularize with R(w) equals sum from g=1 to G of the L2-norm of w_g, combining L2 within groups and L1 across groups, solved by block-wise soft-thresholding.

Group Sparsity With Overlaps
For overlapping groups, define R(w) as the infimum of sum from g=1 to G of the L2-norm of w_g where w equals the sum of w_bar_g, duplicating elements in multiple groups. The proximal operator requires iterative computation.

Regularizers for Semi-Supervised Learning
In semi-supervised learning, a regularizer uses a symmetric weight matrix W: R(f) equals sum over i,j of w_ij times (f(x_i) minus f(x_j)) squared, encouraging similar outputs for similar inputs. This equals f transpose times L times f, where L is the graph Laplacian. The solution respects supervised constraints and minimizes the regularizer for unsupervised samples.

Regularizers for Multitask Learning
For T tasks, learn a T by D matrix W. Regularizers include:
- Sparse Regularizer on Columns: Sum from i=1 to D of the L2,1-norm of W.
- Nuclear Norm: L1-norm of W's singular values.
- Mean-Constrained: Sum from t=1 to T of the squared norm of f_t minus the average f_s.
- Clustered Mean-Constrained: Similar, but within task clusters.
- Graph-Based Similarity: Sum over t,s of M_ts times the squared norm of f_t minus f_s, using a similarity matrix M.

Other Uses
Bayesian methods use priors favoring simpler models, while model selection uses AIC, BIC, or cross-validation. Regularization applications include:

Model              Fit Measure                     Entropy Measure
AIC/BIC           Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Norm of beta (L0)
Lasso             Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Norm of beta (L1)
Ridge             Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Norm of beta (L2)
Basis Pursuit     Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Lambda times norm of beta (L1)
TV                Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Lambda times norm of gradient beta (L1)
Potts             Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Lambda times norm of gradient beta (L0)
RLAD              Norm of Y minus X beta (L1)     Norm of beta (L1)
Dantzig Selector  Infinity norm of X transpose (Y minus X beta)  Norm of beta (L1)
SLOPE             Norm of Y minus X beta (L2)     Sum from i=1 to p of lambda_i times absolute beta_(i)

Support Vector Machine

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Support vector machines (SVMs), also known as support vector networks, are supervised max-margin models used in machine learning for classification and regression. Developed at AT&T Bell Laboratories, SVMs are grounded in statistical learning frameworks like VC theory by Vapnik and Chervonenkis. They perform linear classification and non-linear classification via the kernel trick, which maps data into a higher-dimensional feature space using pairwise similarity comparisons through a kernel function. SVMs are resilient to noisy data and can handle regression with an epsilon-sensitive objective. Support vector clustering, an unsupervised approach, categorizes unlabeled data into natural groups.

SVMs are popular due to their theoretical analyzability and versatility across tasks like structured prediction. Their predictive performance may not surpass other linear models like logistic regression.

Motivation
SVMs classify data points, each a p-dimensional vector, into one of two classes using a (p-1)-dimensional hyperplane. The optimal hyperplane maximizes the margin, the distance to the nearest point from either class, called the maximum-margin hyperplane, reducing generalization error and overfitting.

SVMs construct hyperplanes in high or infinite-dimensional spaces for classification, regression, or outlier detection. A larger margin typically lowers generalization error.

Kernel Machine
When data is not linearly separable, SVMs map it to a higher-dimensional space via the kernel trick, where linear separation is easier. The kernel function k(x, y) computes dot products efficiently, defining hyperplanes as points with constant dot products to a vector. This allows complex, non-convex separations in the original space.

Applications
SVMs are used in:
- Text and hypertext categorization, reducing labeled training needs.
- Image classification and segmentation, achieving high accuracy.
- Satellite data classification, like SAR data.
- Hand-written character recognition.
- Biological sciences, classifying proteins with up to 90% accuracy, with SVM weights aiding model interpretation.

History
Invented by Vapnik and Chervonenkis in 1964, the non-linear kernel trick was proposed by Boser, Guyon, and Vapnik in 1992. The soft-margin SVM, common in software, was introduced by Cortes and Vapnik in 1993, published in 1995.

Linear SVM
Given n points (x_1, y_1), ..., (x_n, y_n), where y_i is 1 or -1 indicating class and x_i is a p-dimensional vector, SVMs find the maximum-margin hyperplane dividing classes, defined by w transpose x minus b equals 0, where w is the normal vector and b/||w|| is the offset. The goal is to minimize ||w|| to maximize the margin 2/||w||.

Hard-Margin
For linearly separable data, two hyperplanes w transpose x minus b equals 1 and w transpose x minus b equals -1 define the margin. Data points must satisfy y_i (w transpose x_i minus b) at least 1. The optimization minimizes 1/2 ||w||^2 subject to these constraints. The hyperplane is determined by support vectors, the nearest points to it.

Soft-Margin
For non-separable data, the hinge loss max(0, 1 - y_i (w transpose x_i minus b)) measures margin violations. The optimization minimizes ||w||^2 plus C times the average hinge loss, where C balances margin size and correct classification. This is reformulated as minimizing ||w||^2/2 plus C times sum of slack variables zeta_i, subject to y_i (w transpose x_i minus b) at least 1 minus zeta_i and zeta_i at least 0.

Nonlinear Kernels
The kernel trick enables non-linear classification by mapping data to a high-dimensional space via phi(x_i), where the kernel k(x_i, x_j) equals phi(x_i) dot phi(x_j). Common kernels include:
- Polynomial (homogeneous): (x_i dot x_j)^d.
- Polynomial (inhomogeneous): (x_i dot x_j plus r)^d.
- Gaussian radial basis: exp(-gamma ||x_i minus x_j||^2).
- Sigmoid: tanh(kappa x_i dot x_j plus c).

The classifier is w equals sum c_i y_i phi(x_i), computed via k(x_i, x_j).

Computing the SVM Classifier
The soft-margin SVM minimizes the average hinge loss plus lambda times ||w||^2. The primal problem introduces slack variables zeta_i, minimizing 1/n sum zeta_i plus lambda ||w||^2 subject to margin constraints. The dual problem maximizes sum c_i minus 1/2 sum y_i c_i (x_i dot x_j) y_j c_j, subject to sum c_i y_i equals 0 and 0 at most c_i at most 1/(2n lambda), solved via quadratic programming. Support vectors have non-zero c_i.

Kernel Trick
For non-linear rules, the dual uses k(x_i, x_j) for phi(x_i) dot phi(x_j), solving the same optimization with kernel-substituted dot products. The offset b is computed from a boundary point, and the classifier is sgn(sum c_i y_i k(x_i, z) minus b).

Modern Methods
Sub-gradient descent minimizes the soft-margin expression directly, using sub-gradients for the non-differentiable hinge loss, efficient for large datasets. Coordinate descent adjusts each c_i along the partial derivative, projecting onto constraints, fast for high-dimensional spaces.

Empirical Risk Minimization
Soft-margin SVM is empirical risk minimization (ERM) with hinge loss max(0, 1 - y z), akin to regularized least-squares (square loss) and logistic regression (log-loss). ERM minimizes empirical risk 1/n sum l(y_k, f(X_k)), approximating expected risk. Tikhonov regularization adds a penalty lambda ||f|| to control complexity.

SVM and Hinge Loss
SVM minimizes hinge loss plus lambda ||w||^2, equivalent to ERM with Tikhonov regularization. The hinge loss targets the optimal classifier sgn(p_x - 1/2), minimizing complexity in rich hypothesis spaces, unlike square or log-loss, which provide distributional details.

Properties
SVMs are generalized linear classifiers, extending perceptrons with maximum margin minimization, a form of Tikhonov regularization. They balance empirical error and margin size.

Parameter Selection
SVM performance depends on kernel, kernel parameters (e.g., gamma for Gaussian), and lambda. Grid search with cross-validation tests combinations like lambda in {2^-5, 2^-3, ..., 2^15} and gamma in {2^-15, 2^-13, ..., 2^3}, or Bayesian optimization for efficiency. The final model trains on the full dataset with optimal parameters.

Issues
SVM drawbacks include:
- Requiring full data labeling.
- Uncalibrated probability estimates.
- Limited to binary tasks, needing multi-class extensions.
- Hard-to-interpret model parameters.

Margin Classifier

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In machine learning, a margin classifier provides a distance from the decision boundary for each data sample. For a linear classifier, this distance, typically Euclidean, is the sample's margin, measured from the separating hyperplane.

Margins are crucial in classification algorithms, enabling bounds on generalization error using VC dimension. These bounds are prominent in boosting algorithms and support vector machines.

Margin for Boosting Algorithms
For a boosting algorithm with a two-class dataset, given a sample pair (x, y) where x is in the domain space X and y is in {-1, +1}, the algorithm selects a classifier h_j from a set C at iteration j, predicting real values. Each classifier is weighted by alpha_j, a real number. At iteration t, the margin of sample x is y times the sum from j=1 to t of alpha_j times h_j(x), divided by the sum of absolute alpha_j values. A positive margin indicates correct labeling; a negative margin indicates incorrect labeling. This definition is one approach, chosen for its theoretical appeal.

Examples of Margin-Based Algorithms
Many classifiers provide margins, but only some use margin information during learning. Boosting algorithms like AdaBoost and LogitBoost use convex loss, weighting low-margin samples more heavily. Non-convex algorithms like BrownBoost also use margins, but weighting is non-monotone. These algorithms adjust sample weights based on margins to focus on difficult cases.

Generalization Error Bounds
Margin classifiers allow bounding generalization error using algorithm parameters and margin terms. For AdaBoost with m data points sampled from distribution D, base classifier VC-dimension d, and m at least d at least 1, with probability 1 minus delta, the error probability that y times the sum from j=1 to t of alpha_j times h_j(x) divided by the sum of absolute alpha_j values is at most 0 is bounded by the sample probability that this margin is at most theta, plus a term proportional to 1 over the square root of m times the square root of d times log squared (m/d) over theta squared plus log(1/delta), for all theta greater than 0.

Kernel Method

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In machine learning, kernel machines are algorithms for pattern analysis, with support-vector machines (SVMs) as the most prominent example. They use linear classifiers to solve non-linear problems by operating in a high-dimensional, implicit feature space without computing data coordinates, using only a similarity function (kernel) over pairs of data points via inner products. This kernel trick makes computations efficient, though slow for datasets over a few thousand examples without parallel processing.

Kernel methods analyze relations like clusters, rankings, principal components, correlations, or classifications in datasets. Unlike algorithms requiring explicit feature vector transformations, kernel methods need only a user-specified kernel, leveraging the representer theorem to use a finite-dimensional matrix. Kernel functions apply to sequences, graphs, text, images, and vectors.

Algorithms using kernels include kernel perceptron, SVMs, Gaussian processes, principal components analysis (PCA), canonical correlation analysis, ridge regression, spectral clustering, and linear adaptive filters. Most rely on convex optimization or eigenproblems, with statistical properties analyzed via statistical learning theory, such as Rademacher complexity.

Motivation and Informal Explanation
Kernel methods are instance-based learners, storing training examples (x_i, y_i) and learning weights w_i instead of fixed parameters. For an unlabeled input x', prediction uses a kernel function k measuring similarity to training inputs x_i. A kernelized binary classifier computes a weighted sum of similarities: y_hat equals the sign of the sum from i=1 to n of w_i times y_i times k(x_i, x'), where y_hat is in {-1, +1}, k maps pairs from the domain X to real numbers, y_i is in {-1, +1}, w_i are learned weights, and sgn is the sign function.

Kernel classifiers, introduced with the kernel perceptron in the 1960s, gained prominence in the 1990s with SVMs, competing with neural networks in tasks like handwriting recognition.

Cluster Analysis

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Cluster analysis, or clustering, is a data analysis technique that groups objects so those in the same group (cluster) are more similar, based on criteria defined by the analyst, than those in other groups. It is a key method in exploratory data analysis and statistical data analysis, applied in fields like pattern recognition, image analysis, information retrieval, bioinformatics, data compression, computer graphics, and machine learning.

Cluster analysis encompasses a family of algorithms and tasks, not a single algorithm, varying in their definition of a cluster and how to find them. Common cluster definitions include groups with small distances between members, dense data regions, intervals, or specific statistical distributions. Clustering is a multi-objective optimization problem, with algorithm and parameter choices (e.g., distance function, density threshold, expected cluster number) depending on the dataset and intended use. It is an iterative process of knowledge discovery, requiring trial and error, data preprocessing, and parameter adjustments to achieve desired results.

Related terms include automatic classification, numerical taxonomy, botryology (from Greek 'grape'), typological analysis, and community detection. In data mining, clusters are the focus, while in automatic classification, discriminative power is key.

Originating in anthropology by Driver and Kroeber in 1932, cluster analysis was introduced to psychology by Zubin in 1938 and Tryon in 1939, and notably used by Cattell in 1943 for trait theory in personality psychology.

Definition
A cluster's definition varies, contributing to the diversity of clustering algorithms. Common cluster models include:
- Connectivity models: Hierarchical clustering uses distance connectivity.
- Centroid models: K-means represents clusters by a mean vector.
- Distribution models: Expectation-maximization uses statistical distributions like multivariate normal.
- Density models: DBSCAN and OPTICS define clusters as dense regions.
- Subspace models: Biclustering models clusters with both members and relevant attributes.
- Group models: Provide grouping without refined models.
- Graph-based models: Cliques or quasi-cliques in graphs form clusters.
- Signed graph models: Use balance theory for bifurcated or multi-cluster graphs.
- Neural models: Self-organizing maps resemble other models, including subspace models via PCA or ICA.

A clustering is a set of clusters, often covering all data objects, possibly with hierarchical relationships. Clusterings include:
- Hard clustering: Each object belongs to one cluster or none.
- Soft clustering: Objects belong to clusters with varying degrees (e.g., likelihood).
- Strict partitioning: Each object is in exactly one cluster.
- Strict partitioning with outliers: Objects may be outliers, belonging to no cluster.
- Overlapping clustering: Objects may belong to multiple clusters.
- Hierarchical clustering: Child cluster objects belong to parent clusters.
- Subspace clustering: Non-overlapping clusters within unique subspaces.

Algorithms
Clustering algorithms vary by cluster model, with over 100 published methods. Prominent examples include:
- Hierarchical clustering: Connectivity-based, forming nested clusters.
- K-means: Centroid-based, using mean vectors.
- Expectation-maximization: Distribution-based, modeling statistical distributions.
- DBSCAN and OPTICS: Density-based, identifying dense regions.
- HCS clustering: Graph-based, using quasi-cliques.

No single algorithm is objectively correct; clustering is subjective. An axiomatic approach shows no method can simultaneously satisfy scale invariance, richness, and consistency. The best algorithm depends on the dataset and problem, often chosen experimentally. Algorithms suited for one model (e.g., k-means for convex clusters) fail on others (e.g., non-convex clusters). Most traditional methods assume spherical, elliptical, or convex cluster shapes.

k-means Clustering

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

k-means clustering, originating from signal processing, is a vector quantization method that partitions n observations into k clusters, where each observation belongs to the cluster with the nearest mean (centroid), acting as the cluster's prototype. This creates Voronoi cells in the data space. It minimizes within-cluster variances (squared Euclidean distances), not regular Euclidean distances, unlike the Weber problem solved by k-medians or k-medoids. The mean minimizes squared errors, while the geometric median minimizes Euclidean distances.

The problem is NP-hard, but efficient heuristic algorithms quickly converge to a local optimum, similar to the expectation-maximization algorithm for Gaussian mixture models. Both use cluster centers, but k-means finds clusters of similar spatial extent, while Gaussian mixtures allow varied shapes.

k-means, an unsupervised algorithm, relates loosely to the supervised k-nearest neighbor classifier. Applying the 1-nearest neighbor classifier to k-means cluster centers assigns new data to existing clusters, known as the nearest centroid classifier or Rocchio algorithm.

Description
Given n observations (x_1, x_2, ..., x_n), each a d-dimensional real vector, k-means clustering partitions them into k (at most n) sets S = {S_1, S_2, ..., S_k} to minimize the within-cluster sum of squares (WCSS), or variance. The objective is to find the set S that minimizes the sum from i=1 to k of the sum over x in S_i of the squared norm of x minus mu_i, where mu_i is the mean of points in S_i, defined as 1 over the size of S_i times the sum over x in S_i of x. This is equivalent to minimizing the sum from i=1 to k of the size of S_i times the variance of S_i.

This also minimizes the pairwise squared deviations within clusters: minimize over S the sum from i=1 to k of 1 over the size of S_i times the sum over x, y in S_i of the squared norm of x minus y. Equivalence follows from the identity: the size of S_i times the sum over x in S_i of the squared norm of x minus mu_i equals 1/2 times the sum over x, y in S_i of the squared norm of x minus y. Since total variance is constant, this maximizes the between-cluster sum of squares (BCSS), related to the law of total variance.

Mixture Model

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In statistics, a mixture model is a probabilistic model representing subpopulations within a population without identifying which subpopulation each observation belongs to. It corresponds to a mixture distribution, modeling the overall population's probability distribution. Unlike mixture distribution problems, which derive overall population properties from subpopulations, mixture models infer subpopulation properties from pooled population observations without subpopulation labels. They are used for model-based clustering and density estimation.

Mixture models differ from compositional data models, where components sum to a constant (e.g., 1 or 100%). However, compositional models can be viewed as mixture models with random sampling, and mixture models as compositional models normalized to a total size of 1.

Structure
General Mixture Model
A finite-dimensional mixture model is a hierarchical model with:
- N observed random variables, each following a mixture of K components from the same parametric family (e.g., normal, Zipfian) with different parameters.
- N latent random variables indicating each observation's component, following a K-dimensional categorical distribution.
- K mixture weights, probabilities summing to 1.
- K parameters, each specifying a component's distribution (e.g., mean and variance for Gaussian, or V probabilities for categorical distributions).

In Bayesian settings, weights and parameters are random variables with priors, typically a K-dimensional Dirichlet distribution for weights and conjugate priors for parameters.

Mathematically:
- K: number of components
- N: number of observations
- theta_i (i=1 to K): parameter of component i's distribution
- phi_i (i=1 to K): mixture weight, prior probability of component i
- phi: K-dimensional vector of phi_1 to phi_K, summing to 1
- z_i (i=1 to N): component of observation i
- x_i (i=1 to N): observation i
- F(x | theta): probability distribution of an observation, parameterized by theta
- z_i (i=1 to N) ~ Categorical(phi)
- x_i (i=1 to N) given z_i ~ F(theta_z_i)

In Bayesian settings:
- alpha: hyperparameter for component parameters
- beta: hyperparameter for mixture weights
- H(theta | alpha): prior distribution of component parameters
- theta_i (i=1 to K) ~ H(theta | alpha)
- phi ~ Symmetric-Dirichlet_K(beta)
- z_i (i=1 to N) given phi ~ Categorical(phi)
- x_i (i=1 to N) given z_i, theta_i ~ F(theta_z_i)

Common distributions for F include:
- Gaussian: for real-valued data
- Categorical: for discrete data
- Binomial: for successes in fixed trials
- Multinomial: for multi-way counts
- Negative binomial: for failures before successes
- Poisson: for event counts in time
- Exponential: for time to next event
- Log-normal: for exponentially growing positive values
- Multivariate normal: for correlated Gaussian vectors
- Multivariate Student's t: for heavy-tailed correlated vectors

Neural Network (Machine Learning)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In machine learning, a neural network, also known as an artificial neural network or neural net, is a computational model inspired by the structure and functions of biological neural networks.

A neural network consists of connected units called artificial neurons, modeling brain neurons. These are linked by edges, representing synapses. Each neuron receives signals from connected neurons, processes them, and sends a signal to others. The signal is a real number, and the neuron's output is computed by a non-linear activation function applied to the sum of its inputs. Connection strengths are determined by weights, adjusted during learning.

Neurons are organized into layers, with different layers performing distinct transformations. Signals flow from the input layer to the output layer, possibly through multiple hidden layers. A network with at least two hidden layers is called a deep neural network.

Neural networks are used for tasks like predictive modeling, adaptive control, and solving artificial intelligence problems. They learn from experience, deriving conclusions from complex, seemingly unrelated data.

Training
Neural networks are trained through empirical risk minimization, optimizing parameters to minimize the difference between predicted and actual target values in a dataset. Gradient-based methods like backpropagation estimate parameters. During training, networks learn from labeled data by iteratively updating parameters to minimize a loss function, enabling generalization to unseen data.

Backpropagation

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In machine learning, backpropagation is a gradient estimation method commonly used to train neural networks by computing parameter updates. It efficiently applies the chain rule to compute the gradient of a loss function with respect to network weights for a single input-output example, processing one layer at a time, backward from the last layer, using dynamic programming to avoid redundant calculations.

Strictly, backpropagation refers to the gradient computation algorithm, not its use (e.g., in stochastic gradient descent or complex optimizers like Adaptive Moment Estimation). However, it is often used loosely to denote the entire learning algorithm. Disadvantages include local minimum convergence, exploding or vanishing gradients, and weak learning rate control. Hessian and quasi-Hessian optimizers address only local minimum issues, and backpropagation can be slow. Hybrid and fractional optimization algorithms have been developed to mitigate these issues.

Also known as reverse mode of automatic differentiation or reverse accumulation, backpropagation has a complex history with multiple discoveries.

Overview
Backpropagation computes the gradient in weight space of a feedforward neural network relative to a loss function. Key notations:
- x: input vector of features
- y: target output (e.g., class probabilities like (0.1, 0.7, 0.2) for classification, with target as a one-hot vector like (0, 1, 0))
- C: loss function (e.g., cross-entropy for classification, squared error for regression)
- L: number of layers
- W^l: weight matrix between layers l-1 and l, where w_jk^l is the weight from the k-th node in layer l-1 to the j-th node in layer l
- f^l: activation function at layer l (e.g., logistic or softmax for the last layer in classification, ReLU for hidden layers)
- a_j^l: activation of the j-th node in layer l

The network combines function composition and matrix multiplication: g(x) equals f^L(W^L f^(L-1)(W^(L-1) ... f^1(W^1 x) ... )). For a training set of input-output pairs (x_i, y_i), the loss is C(y_i, g(x_i)), measuring the difference between predicted and target outputs.

Backpropagation computes the gradient for a fixed input-output pair (x_i, y_i), with weights w_jk^l varying. It calculates partial derivatives of C with respect to w_jk^l efficiently by computing the gradient of each layer's weighted input (delta^l) backward, avoiding duplicate calculations and unnecessary intermediate values. This leverages the fact that a weight in W^l affects the loss only through the next layer linearly, allowing recursive computation of delta^(l-1) from delta^l. This prevents redundant derivative calculations for later layers and avoids computing derivatives of hidden layer values with respect to weights.

Backpropagation can be expressed as matrix multiplication for simple feedforward networks or via the adjoint graph.

Convolutional Neural Network

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

A convolutional neural network (CNN) is a feedforward neural network that learns features through filter (or kernel) optimization, widely used in deep learning for processing text, images, and audio. CNNs are the standard for computer vision and image processing, though newer architectures like transformers have begun to replace them in some cases.

CNNs prevent vanishing and exploding gradients during backpropagation through regularization from shared weights and fewer connections. For example, a fully-connected layer for a 100x100 pixel image requires 10,000 weights per neuron, while a 5x5 convolutional layer needs only 25 weights. Higher-layer features capture wider context compared to lower-layer features.

Applications include:
- Image and video recognition
- Recommender systems
- Image classification and segmentation
- Medical image analysis
- Natural language processing
- Brain-computer interfaces
- Financial time series analysis

CNNs, also called shift-invariant or space-invariant neural networks, use shared-weight convolution kernels that slide along inputs, producing translation-equivariant feature maps. However, most CNNs are not fully translation-invariant due to downsampling.

Fully connected feedforward networks, where each neuron connects to all neurons in the next layer, are prone to overfitting. CNNs mitigate this through regularization techniques like weight decay, skipped connections, dropout, and robust datasets to learn generalized principles.

Inspired by biological processes, CNNs mimic the animal visual cortex, where neurons respond to restricted receptive fields, partially overlapping to cover the visual field. CNNs require minimal preprocessing compared to other image classification algorithms, learning to optimize filters automatically, enhancing efficiency and scalability.

Architecture
A CNN comprises an input layer, hidden layers, and an output layer. Hidden layers include convolutional layers performing dot products (often Frobenius inner products) with kernels, typically using ReLU activation. Convolution generates feature maps for the next layer, followed by pooling, fully connected, or normalization layers.

Convolutional Layers
The input is a tensor with shape: (number of inputs) times (input height) times (input width) times (input channels). After convolution, it becomes a feature map with shape: (number of inputs) times (feature map height) times (feature map width) times (feature map channels). Each neuron processes its receptive field, reducing parameters (e.g., 25 weights for a 5x5 kernel versus 10,000 for a fully connected layer), allowing deeper networks and avoiding gradient issues.

Depthwise separable convolutional layers, with spatial depthwise convolution per channel and pointwise convolution using 1x1 kernels, speed up processing.

Pooling Layers
Pooling layers reduce data dimensions by combining neuron cluster outputs into a single neuron. Local pooling uses small clusters (e.g., 2x2 tiles), while global pooling acts on all feature map neurons. Common types are max pooling (maximum value) and average pooling (average value).

Fully Connected Layers
Fully connected layers connect every neuron in one layer to every neuron in the next, like a multilayer perceptron, often used for final classification after flattening the feature map.

Receptive Field
Each neuron's receptive field is a restricted area in the previous layer. In convolutional layers, this is typically a square (e.g., 5x5). Fully connected layers use the entire previous layer. Convolution increases the input area considered, incorporating surrounding pixels. Dilated (atrous) convolution expands receptive fields without more parameters, and multi-dilation filters vary receptive field sizes.

Weights
Neurons compute outputs using a function of input values, weights, and biases. Filters (weights and biases) represent features (e.g., shapes) and are shared across receptive fields, reducing memory compared to unique weights per field.

Deconvolutional
A deconvolutional neural network reverses a CNN, using deconvolutional and unpooling layers. A deconvolutional layer transposes a convolutional layer's matrix multiplication. Unpooling (e.g., max-unpooling) expands layers by copying entries (e.g., a single value becomes a 2x2 block of identical values). Used in image generators, deconvolution may produce checkerboard artifacts, mitigated by upscale-then-convolve methods.


Explainable Artificial Intelligence

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Explainable AI (XAI), also known as interpretable AI or explainable machine learning, is a research field within artificial intelligence focused on methods enabling human oversight of AI algorithms. It emphasizes understanding the reasoning behind AI decisions or predictions, making them transparent and comprehensible. XAI addresses the "black box" nature of machine learning, where even designers cannot explain specific decisions, ensuring safety and scrutiny in automated decision-making.

XAI aims to enhance user effectiveness by improving understanding of AI reasoning, potentially fulfilling the social right to explanation or fostering trust in AI decisions. It explains past, current, and future actions, revealing their informational basis, enabling confirmation, challenge, or generation of knowledge.

Machine learning algorithms are categorized as white-box (understandable to experts) or black-box (hard to explain, even for experts). XAI algorithms adhere to transparency, interpretability, and explainability:
- Transparency: Processes for extracting model parameters and generating labels are describable.
- Interpretability: The model can be comprehended and presented understandably to humans.
- Explainability: Features contributing to a decision are identified, though a consensus definition is pending.

Interpretability focuses on understanding model outputs, while transparency includes simulatability, decomposability, and algorithmic transparency. Model functionality provides textual descriptions, visualizations, and local explanations for specific outputs. These enhance AI comprehensibility, justifying decisions, tracking, verifying, improving algorithms, and discovering new facts.

White-box algorithms, like Concept Bottleneck Models using concept-level abstractions, can achieve high accuracy with interpretable structures, crucial in medicine, defense, finance, and law for trust and understanding. Symbolic regression, searching mathematical expressions for optimal models, is proposed for supervised learning.

AI systems optimize mathematically specified goals, but may learn inappropriate rules (e.g., associating "Daniel Day-Lewis" with positive reviews). XAI allows auditing to assess generalization to new data.

Goals
Trust is essential for human-AI cooperation. Transparency, interpretability, and explainability are steps toward comprehensive trust criteria, especially in medicine, where clinical decision support systems require understandable decisions. XAI prevents AI from learning undesirable tricks, like cheating by recognizing irrelevant patterns (e.g., copyright tags for horse images or false grasping in virtual environments).

The DARPA XAI program develops "glass box" models, explainable to humans without sacrificing performance, enabling trust assessment. XAI extracts knowledge from black-box models, compares models, and ensures ethical and legal compliance, contrasting with opaque "black box" systems.

Explainability and Interpretability Techniques

Explainability vs. Interpretability
Term             Definition
Interpretability Level of understanding how the AI technology works
Explainability   Level of understanding how the AI system produced a result

Explainability ensures decisions avoid unfair criteria, often treating models as black boxes and analyzing input changes' effects. Techniques for classification and regression include:
- Partial dependency plots: Show a feature's marginal effect on predictions.
- SHAP: Visualizes feature contributions using Shapley values.
- Feature importance: Estimates feature significance via permutation importance.
- LIME: Approximates model outputs with a simpler interpretable model.
- Multitask learning: Provides additional outputs to deduce model learning.
- Saliency maps: Highlight influential image parts for results.

Expert systems encode domain knowledge as production rules, allowing users to query and understand reasoning. For language models like generative pretrained transformers, explanations may be unreliable. Techniques include attention analysis, probing methods, causal tracing, and circuit discovery.

Interpretability
Interpretability, or mechanistic interpretability, reverse-engineers neural networks to understand decision-making, crucial for AI safety and alignment. It studies phenomena like grokking, where models transition from overfitting to generalization. For generative pretrained transformers, it seeks automated feature identification, using compute-intensive dictionary learning to enhance safety. For convolutional neural networks, DeepDream visualizes neuron activations to reveal learned patterns.

Debugging

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In engineering, debugging is the process of identifying the root cause, workarounds, and possible fixes for bugs.

For software, debugging tactics include interactive debugging, control flow analysis, log file analysis, monitoring at application or system levels, memory dumps, and profiling. Many programming languages and tools offer debuggers to aid debugging.

Etymology
The term "bug" for a defect dates to 1878, when Thomas Edison described "little faults and difficulties" in inventions as bugs.
