A 1940s story from Admiral Grace Hopper recounts a moth stuck in a Mark II computer relay at Harvard, logged as the "first actual case of a bug being found." Though likely a joke, it shows the term's use in computing then. The term "debugging" appeared in aeronautics before computing, noted in a 1944 letter by J. Robert Oppenheimer and a 1945 Royal Aeronautical Society article. The 1951 article by Gill is the earliest detailed discussion of programming errors, though it avoids "bug" or "debugging." By 1952, "debugging" appeared in ACM papers, and by 1963, it was common in the CTSS manual.

Scope
As systems grow complex, debugging techniques expand to detect anomalies, assess impact, and schedule patches or updates. Neutral terms like "anomaly" or "discrepancy" avoid implying all issues must be fixed. Impact assessments determine if fixing an anomaly is cost-effective or if a new release makes changes unnecessary. Not all issues are safety- or mission-critical, and some changes may disrupt users more than the problem itself. Avoiding a "zero-defects" mandate prevents hiding issues, and broader techniques assess anomaly frequency to gauge system impact.

Tools
Debugging ranges from fixing simple errors to complex data collection and analysis. Programmer skill and system complexity affect debugging difficulty, varying by programming language and tools like debuggers. Debuggers monitor program execution, allowing stopping, restarting, setting breakpoints, and modifying memory values.

High-level languages like Java simplify debugging with features like exception handling and type checking, making erratic behavior easier to spot. Low-level languages like C or assembly may cause silent issues like memory corruption, requiring memory debuggers. Static code analysis tools, specific to languages, detect semantic issues (e.g., unassigned variable dereference) in large codebases, complementing compilers. These tools, like the Unix lint program, may produce false positives but are valuable for codebases too large for manual review.

For hardware, low-level software, or firmware, tools like oscilloscopes, logic analyzers, or in-circuit emulators perform debugger-like tasks.

Loss Function

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In mathematical optimization and decision theory, a loss function, or cost function, maps an event or variable values to a real number representing a "cost." Optimization problems aim to minimize a loss function. An objective function is either a loss function or its opposite (e.g., reward, profit, utility, or fitness function), which is maximized. Loss functions may combine terms from multiple hierarchical levels.

In statistics, loss functions are used for parameter estimation, measuring the difference between estimated and true values. Introduced by Laplace and reintroduced by Abraham Wald in the 20th century, they are applied in economics (cost or regret), classification (penalty for incorrect classification), actuarial science (modeling benefits over premiums), optimal control (penalty for missing desired values), and financial risk management (monetary loss).

Examples
Regret
Leonard J. Savage proposed that non-Bayesian methods like minimax should use a loss function based on regret, the difference between the best possible decision's outcome, given known circumstances, and the actual decision made.

Quadratic Loss Function
Common in least squares, the quadratic loss function is mathematically tractable and symmetric, assigning equal loss to errors above or below the target. For a target t, it is lambda(x) equals C times (t minus x) squared, where C is a constant (often set to 1). Also known as squared error loss, it underpins t-tests, regression models, and experimental design in linear regression theory. In linear-quadratic optimal control, it forms quadratic deviations from desired values, yielding linear conditions. The expected quadratic form is used in stochastic control. Its square nature emphasizes outliers, leading to alternatives like Huber, Log-Cosh, and SMAE losses for data with large outliers.

0-1 Loss Function
In statistics and decision theory, the 0-1 loss function L(y_hat, y) equals 1 if y_hat is not equal to y, and 0 otherwise, penalizing incorrect classifications.

Constructing Loss and Objective Functions
Objective functions are determined by problem formulation or decision maker preferences, often as scalar-valued utility functions, as Ragnar Frisch noted. Methods for constructing objective functions, especially quadratic and additive ones, use indifference points from ordinal or cardinal data elicited through interviews. Andranik Tangian applied this to budget distribution for Westfalian universities and European subsidies for German regions.

Expected Loss
When the loss function's value is random due to a random variable X, its expected value is used in decision-making.

Statistics
Frequentist and Bayesian theories use the expected loss differently.

Frequentist Expected Loss
The expected loss, or risk function, is the expected value of the loss with respect to the data's probability distribution P_theta: R(theta, delta) equals the integral over X of L(theta, delta(x)) dP_theta(x), where theta is a state of nature, X is observed data, delta is the decision rule, and E_theta is the expectation over X.

Bayes Risk
In Bayesian theory, the expectation uses the prior distribution pi_star of theta: rho(pi_star, a) equals the double integral over Theta and X of L(theta, a(x)) dP(x | theta) d pi_star(theta), or equivalently, over X and Theta of L(theta, a(x)) d pi_star(theta | x) d M(x), where M(x) is the predictive likelihood, and pi_star(theta | x) is the posterior. The Bayes decision rule a_star minimizes this Bayes Risk by optimizing the posterior risk.

Examples in Statistics
For a scalar parameter theta, with a decision function theta_hat estimating theta, and quadratic loss L(theta, theta_hat) equals (theta minus theta_hat) squared, the risk is the mean squared error: R(theta, theta_hat) equals E_theta[(theta minus theta_hat) squared]. Minimizing this estimates the posterior mean.

In density estimation, with the parameter as the probability density, the loss is a norm (e.g., L2 norm: L(f, f_hat) equals the squared L2 norm of f minus f_hat), and the risk is the mean integrated squared error: R(f, f_hat) equals E[the squared norm of f minus f_hat].

Economic Choice Under Uncertainty
In economics, decision-making under uncertainty uses the von Neumann-Morgenstern utility function of an uncertain variable (e.g., wealth), maximizing the expected utility.

Transfer Learning

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Transfer learning is a machine learning technique where knowledge from one task is reused to improve performance on a related task. For example, knowledge from recognizing cars can aid in recognizing trucks. Related to the psychological concept of transfer of learning, it enhances learning efficiency by reusing information from previously learned tasks, though practical connections to psychology are limited.

Since transfer learning involves multiple objective functions, it relates to cost-sensitive machine learning and multi-objective optimization.

History
In 1976, Bozinovski and Fulgosi published a paper on transfer learning in neural network training, providing a mathematical and geometrical model. In 1981, a report demonstrated transfer learning on a letter image dataset, showing positive and negative transfer. In 1992, Lorien Pratt developed the discriminability-based transfer algorithm. By 1998, multi-task learning and formal theoretical foundations emerged, with key works including the 1998 book Learning to Learn, a 2009 survey, and a 2019 survey. In 2016, Andrew Ng predicted transfer learning would drive machine learning commercial success. A 2020 paper by Zoph et al. suggested pre-training could reduce accuracy, advocating self-training instead.

Definition
Transfer learning is defined using domains and tasks. A domain D consists of a feature space X and a marginal probability distribution P(X), where X is a set of instances x_1 to x_n in X. A task T, given domain D = {X, P(X)}, includes a label space Y and a predictive function f: X to Y, predicting the label f(x) for a new instance x. The task T = {Y, f(x)} is learned from training pairs (x_i, y_i), where x_i is in X and y_i is in Y.

Given a source domain D_S and task T_S, and a target domain D_T and task T_T, where D_S is not equal to D_T or T_S is not equal to T_T, transfer learning improves the target predictive function f_T in D_T using knowledge from D_S and T_S.

Applications
Transfer learning algorithms apply to Markov logic networks, Bayesian networks, cancer subtype discovery, building utilization, general game playing, text classification, digit recognition, medical imaging, and spam filtering.

In 2020, transfer learning was shown between electromyographic (EMG) muscle signals and electroencephalographic (EEG) brainwave classification, from gesture to mental state recognition, and vice versa. This improved neural and convolutional neural network accuracy, both initially (versus random weights) and asymptotically, with benefits from cross-domain exposure. End-users can also modify fully-connected layers of pre-trained models to enhance performance.

Hyperparameter Optimization

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

In machine learning, hyperparameter optimization, or tuning, is the process of selecting optimal hyperparameters for a learning algorithm. Hyperparameters are parameters set before training to control the learning process. Optimization finds the hyperparameter set that minimizes a predefined loss function on a dataset, using an objective function that maps hyperparameters to loss. Cross-validation often estimates generalization performance to select hyperparameters maximizing it.

Approaches

Grid Search
Grid search, or parameter sweep, exhaustively searches a manually specified subset of the hyperparameter space, guided by a performance metric from cross-validation or a hold-out validation set. For continuous or unbounded parameters, bounds and discretization are needed. For example, for a soft-margin SVM with an RBF kernel, tuning regularization constant C and kernel parameter gamma involves selecting finite values (e.g., C in {10, 100, 1000}, gamma in {0.1, 0.2, 0.5, 1.0}), training an SVM for each pair in their Cartesian product, and evaluating performance to select the best settings. Grid search suffers from the curse of dimensionality but is embarrassingly parallel as evaluations are independent.

Random Search
Random search selects hyperparameter combinations randomly, applicable to discrete, continuous, or mixed spaces. It explores more values for continuous hyperparameters than grid search and can outperform it when few hyperparameters significantly impact performance (low intrinsic dimensionality). It is also embarrassingly parallel and allows prior knowledge via sampling distributions, remaining a key baseline for new methods.

Bayesian Optimization
Bayesian optimization is a global optimization method for noisy black-box functions, building a probabilistic model of the hyperparameter-to-objective mapping. It iteratively evaluates promising configurations, updating the model to balance exploration (uncertain hyperparameters) and exploitation (near-optimal hyperparameters). It achieves better results with fewer evaluations than grid or random search by reasoning about experiment quality beforehand.

Gradient-Based Optimization
For certain algorithms, gradients with respect to hyperparameters can be computed and optimized via gradient descent, initially used for neural networks and extended to support vector machines and logistic regression. Another approach differentiates iterative optimization steps using automatic differentiation. A recent method uses the implicit function theorem for stable hypergradient approximation, scaling to millions of hyperparameters with constant memory. Hypernetworks approximate best response functions, handling discrete hyperparameters, with self-tuning networks and Delta-STN improving efficiency and approximation. Continuous relaxation of discrete parameters aids neural architecture search.

Evolutionary Optimization
Evolutionary optimization uses evolutionary algorithms to search hyperparameter spaces, creating a population of random hyperparameter tuples, evaluating their fitness (e.g., cross-validation accuracy), ranking them, replacing poor performers with new tuples via crossover and mutation, and repeating until satisfactory performance. It is used for statistical machine learning, automated machine learning, neural network architecture search, and weight training.

Population-Based
Population-Based Training learns hyperparameters and weights simultaneously, with independent processes using different hyperparameters. Poor performers are replaced with models adopting modified hyperparameters and weights from better performers, allowing hyperparameters to evolve without manual tuning. Unlike non-adaptive methods, it updates hyperparameters during training.

Early Stopping-Based
Early stopping-based methods suit large search spaces, especially when evaluation is costly. Iterated racing (Irace) focuses on promising configurations, discarding poor ones via statistical tests. Successive halving starts with random search, periodically pruning low performers to focus resources. Asynchronous successive halving improves resource use by asynchronous pruning. Hyperband invokes successive halving multiple times with varying pruning aggressiveness for broader applicability.

Others
Radial basis function and spectral approaches have also been developed.

Issues with Hyperparameter Optimization
Hyperparameter optimization often fits hyperparameters on a training set and selects them based on validation set performance, risking overfitting to the validation set. Thus, validation set performance cannot estimate final model generalization. An independent test set or nested cross-validation, evaluating performance on a separate set, provides an unbiased estimate, accounting for optimization bias.


Transformer (Deep Learning Architecture)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

The transformer is a deep learning architecture introduced in 2017 by Google researchers in the paper "Attention Is All You Need," based on the multi-head attention mechanism. Text is converted to tokens, mapped to vectors via word embeddings, and contextualized within a context window using parallel multi-head attention, amplifying key tokens and diminishing less important ones. Unlike recurrent neural networks like LSTM, transformers lack recurrent units, reducing training time, and have been widely adopted for large language models, computer vision, reinforcement learning, audio, multimodal tasks, robotics, and chess.

History
Sequence modeling initially used recurrent neural networks (RNNs) like the Elman network (1990). LSTM (1995) addressed vanishing gradients with multiplicative units, becoming the standard until transformers. RNNs process tokens sequentially, limiting parallelization. Fast weight controllers (1992) used dynamic links, later shown equivalent to linear transformers. Seq2seq models (2014) introduced encoder-decoder architectures, with attention mechanisms (RNNsearch, 2015) improving long-sequence handling. Google Neural Machine Translation (2016) used bidirectional LSTM. Decomposable attention (2016) applied self-attention to feedforward networks, enabling parallelization. The 2017 transformer replaced recurrence with multi-head attention, enhancing parallelization and performance.

AI Boom Era
Transformers fueled the AI boom, with decoder-only models generating text (e.g., 2017 Wikipedia articles). ELMo (2018) and BERT (2018, encoder-only) advanced contextual embeddings, with BERT used in Google Search (2019). OpenAI's GPT series (2018-2022) set NLP benchmarks, with ChatGPT (2022) sparking widespread LLM adoption. Transformers extended to vision (Vision Transformer, 2020), speech (Conformer, Whisper), robotics, and multimodal tasks (DALL-E, Stable Diffusion, Sora).

Training
Transformers require stabilization methods like learning rate warmup (original paper) or pre-layer normalization (2020) for convergence. They are pretrained on large, unlabeled corpora (e.g., The Pile) using self-supervised tasks like language modeling, then fine-tuned on task-specific datasets for tasks like translation, question answering, and sentiment analysis.

Tasks
Transformers support masked (predicting masked tokens, e.g., BERT), autoregressive (predicting next tokens, e.g., GPT), and prefixLM (predicting tokens after a context) tasks. Loss functions typically sum log-perplexities of predicted tokens.

Architecture
Transformers consist of tokenizers, embedding layers, transformer layers (encoder/decoder), and un-embedding layers.

Tokenization
Tokenizers convert text to integer tokens using methods like byte pair encoding, with a vocabulary size n_vocabulary. Unknown tokens use a special "[UNK]" token.

Embedding
Tokens are mapped to d_emb-dimensional vectors via a lookup table, added to positional encodings for sequence order.

Un-embedding
The final layer converts vectors to token probability distributions using a linear-softmax layer, sometimes sharing weights with the embedding layer.

Positional Encoding
Positional encodings provide token position information, typically using sinusoidal functions: for position t and dimension 2k, f(t)_2k = sin(t / N^(2k/d)), f(t)_(2k+1) = cos(t / N^(2k/d)), where N = 10000, d is even. This enables linear transformations for position shifts.

Encoder-Decoder Overview
Encoders process input tokens in parallel, producing contextualized representations via self-attention. Decoders iteratively generate output tokens, using masked self-attention and cross-attention to encoder outputs.

Feedforward Network
Each transformer layer includes a two-layer multilayer perceptron: FFN(x) = phi(x W^(1) + b^(1)) W^(2) + b^(2), with ReLU activation and intermediate size typically 4 times d_emb.

Scaled Dot-Product Attention
Attention units use query (Q), key (K), and value (V) matrices: Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q K^T / sqrt(d_k)) V, where d_k is the key dimension. Queries and keys determine attention weights, scaled for stability, and values form weighted outputs.

Multiheaded Attention
Multiple attention heads (n_heads) process inputs independently, concatenated and projected: MultiheadedAttention(Q, K, V) = Concat_i(Attention(Q W_i^Q, K W_i^K, V W_i^V)) W^O. Heads capture different relevance relations, enabling parallel computation.

Masked Attention
Decoder self-attention uses causal masking to prevent attending to future tokens: MaskedAttention(Q, K, V) = softmax(M + Q K^T / sqrt(d_k)) V, with M_causal setting future entries to negative infinity.

Encoder
Encoders stack layers with self-attention and feedforward sublayers, processing all tokens simultaneously: EncoderLayer(H) = FFN(MultiheadedAttention(H, H, H)).

Decoder
Decoders stack layers with masked self-attention, cross-attention to encoder outputs, and feedforward sublayers: DecoderLayer(H) = FFN(MultiheadedAttention(MaskedMultiheadedAttention(H, H, H), H^E, H^E)).

Adapted Architectures
- Encoder-only (e.g., BERT): Maps inputs to representations for embedding tasks.
- Decoder-only (e.g., GPT): Uses masked self-attention and feedforward layers for generation.
- Encoder-decoder (e.g., T5): Full architecture for seq2seq tasks.
- PrefixLM: Decoder-only with prefix masking, resembling encoder-decoder.

Sublayers
Encoder layers have two sublayers (self-attention, feedforward); decoder layers have three (masked self-attention, cross-attention, feedforward). Residual connections (y = F(x) + x) and layer normalization (pre-LN: x + Sublayer(LayerNorm(x)) or post-LN: LayerNorm(x + Sublayer(x))) ensure stability. Pre-LN aids convergence without warmup.

Pseudocode
Input: Encoder input t_e, Decoder input t_d
Output: Probability distributions (decoder vocabulary size x length(decoder output))

/* Encoder */
z_e = encoder.tokenizer(t_e)
for t in 1 to length(z_e):
    z_e[t] = encoder.embedding(z_e[t]) + encoder.positional_embedding(t)
for l in 1 to length(encoder.layers):
    layer = encoder.layers[l]
    z_e_copy = copy(z_e)
    for t in 1 to length(z_e):
        z_e[t] = layer.layer_norm(z_e[t])
    z_e = layer.multiheaded_attention(z_e, z_e, z_e)
    for t in 1 to length(z_e):
        z_e[t] = z_e[t] + z_e_copy[t]
    z_e_copy = copy(z_e)
    for t in 1 to length(z_e):
        z_e[t] = layer.layer_norm(z_e[t])
    z_e = layer.feedforward(z_e)
    for t in 1 to length(z_e):
        z_e[t] = z_e[t] + z_e_copy[t]
for t in 1 to length(z_e):
    z_e[t] = encoder.final_layer_norm(z_e[t])

/* Decoder */
z_d = decoder.tokenizer(t_d)
for t in 1 to length(z_d):
    z_d[t] = decoder.embedding(z_d[t]) + decoder.positional_embedding(t)
for l in 1 to length(decoder.layers):
    layer = decoder.layers[l]
    z_d_copy = copy(z_d)
    for t in 1 to length(z_d):
        z_d[t] = layer.layer_norm(z_d[t])
    z_d = layer.masked_multiheaded_attention(z_d, z_d, z_d)
    for t in 1 to length(z_d):
        z_d[t] = z_d[t] + z_d_copy[t]
    z_d_copy = copy(z_d)
    for t in 1 to length(z_d):
        z_d[t] = layer.layer_norm(z_d[t])
    z_d = layer.multiheaded_attention(z_d, z_e, z_e)
    for t in 1 to length(z_d):
        z_d[t] = z_d[t] + z_d_copy[t]
    z_d_copy = copy(z_d)
    for t in 1 to length(z_d):
        z_d[t] = layer.layer_norm(z_d[t])
    z_d = layer.feedforward(z_d)
    for t in 1 to length(z_d):
        z_d[t] = z_d[t] + z_d_copy[t]
z_d = decoder.final_layer_norm(z_d)

output_distributions = []
for t in 1 to length(z_d):
    output_distributions.append(decoder.unembed(z_d[t]))
return output_distributions

Subsequent Work
Alternative Activation Functions
Transformers may use SwiGLU (Llama, PaLM), GELU (GPT-1, BERT), or Gated Linear Units instead of ReLU.

Alternative Normalizations
RMSNorm (Llama), CapsuleNorm, ScaleNorm, or FixNorm may replace LayerNorm.

Alternative Positional Encodings
Learned encodings or RoPE (rotary positional embedding) may replace sinusoidal encodings. RoPE encodes pairs of coordinates as rotations, preserving relative position dot products. ALiBi (Attention with Linear Biases) adds a linear bias matrix to attention, enabling length extrapolation. Relative Position Encodings use Toeplitz matrices for attention biases.

Efficient Implementation
Transformers are implemented in TensorFlow, PyTorch, and Hugging Face's Transformers library. KV caching stores key/value vectors to speed up autoregressive inference. FlashAttention optimizes attention on GPUs via block-based matrix operations, with FlashAttention-2 enhancing parallelism. Multi-Query Attention shares key/value matrices across heads, boosting inference speed. Multihead Latent Attention reduces KV cache size via low-rank projections.

Speculative Decoding
Speculative decoding accelerates token generation by predicting tokens with a smaller model, verifying them with a larger model, discarding incorrect predictions, and recomputing, reducing overall computation time.

Multi-Token Prediction
Predicts multiple future tokens in one pass, trading accuracy for speed by processing embeddings through additional transformer blocks.

Sub-Quadratic Transformers
Reformer uses locality-sensitive hashing for O(N log N) complexity. Sparse attention (e.g., BigBird) scales linearly. Attention-free transformers reduce memory to O(N). Random Feature Attention approximates attention linearly using Fourier features.

Multimodality
Transformers handle non-text modalities by tokenizing inputs (e.g., Vision Transformer patches images, Conformer processes spectrograms). Multimodal models like DALL-E, Parti, and Muse generate images/videos from text prompts, using transformer-based token processing.

Applications
Transformers excel in:
- Natural language processing: machine translation, summarization, generation, named entity recognition, coding
- Time series prediction
- Biological sequence analysis
- Video understanding
- Protein folding (AlphaFold)
- Chess evaluation (grandmaster-level static evaluation)


Attention (Machine Learning)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Attention is a machine learning method that assigns relative importance to components in a sequence, represented by "soft" weights that change per input step, unlike "hard" weights computed during training. In natural language processing, weights are assigned to words in a sentence. Attention encodes token embeddings across sequences, from tens to millions of tokens. Initially used in recurrent neural networks (RNNs) for translation, it was pivotal in the transformer architecture, which replaced slow sequential RNNs with parallel attention for efficiency.

Inspired by human attention, attention addresses RNN limitations, where recent tokens dominate due to vanishing gradients. Attention allows direct access to any sentence part, improving context capture.

History
1950s-1960s: Psychological studies on attention (e.g., cocktail party effect, filter model, saccade control).
1965: Group Method of Data Handling used multiplicative units.
1980s: Sigma-pi and higher-order neural networks; Neocognitron variants.
1990s: Fast weight controllers with dynamic links, resembling transformer keys/values.
2014: RNN with attention improved translation of long sentences.
2015: Attention applied to images.
2016: Decomposable attention used self-attention in feedforward networks.
2017: Transformers introduced parallel multi-head attention, removing RNN recurrence.

Overview
Attention enhances encoder-decoder models by adding an attention mechanism, calculating correlations between words (e.g., "that" with others in "See that girl run") to form context vectors. Unlike RNNs, attention processes all tokens in parallel, using softmax-scaled dot products to prevent single-word dominance.

Interpreting Attention Weights
In translation (e.g., "I love you" to "je t'aime"), attention aligns words via soft weights in an alignment matrix, capturing nuanced relationships (e.g., 94% weight on "I" for "je"). Soft weights allow weighted sums of hidden vectors, unlike hard weights selecting one vector.

Variants
- Fast Weight Controllers (1992): Slow network generates fast weights via outer products.
- Bahdanau Attention (2014): Additive attention with learnable weights.
- Luong Attention (2015): Multiplicative attention with a learnable matrix.
- Decomposable Attention (2016): Parallelizable self-attention.
- Positional/Factorized Positional Attention: Encodes position information.
- Spatial/Channel Attention: For convolutional networks, operating on spatial or channel dimensions.

Optimizations
Flash Attention: Reduces memory for large attention matrices by partitioning computations into GPU cache-fitting blocks, maintaining accuracy.

Self-Attention and Transformers
Self-attention, where query, key, and value vectors come from the same input, enables transformers to capture global dependencies, replacing RNNs in models like BERT, GPT, and T5.

Applications
- Natural Language Processing: Question answering, summarization.
- Computer Vision: Object detection, image captioning.
- Speech Recognition: Context-aware processing.

Mathematical Representation
Standard Scaled Dot-Product Attention
For query (Q), key (K), and value (V) matrices with dimensions Q in R^(m x d_k), K in R^(n x d_k), V in R^(n x d_v), attention is:
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q K^T / sqrt(d_k)) V
where softmax normalizes rows, and scaling by sqrt(d_k) stabilizes gradients. Outputs are convex combinations of V rows.

Permutation properties: For permutation matrices A, B, attention is equivariant to query reordering and invariant to key-value reordering:
Attention(A Q, B K, B V) = A Attention(Q, K, V)

Masked Attention
In autoregressive decoders, masked attention uses a strictly upper triangular mask M (zeros on/below diagonal, negative infinity above) to prevent attending to future tokens:
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q K^T / sqrt(d_k) + M) V
This breaks permutation invariance/equivariance.

Multi-Head Attention
Multi-head attention concatenates multiple attention heads:
MultiHead(Q, K, V) = Concat(head_1, ..., head_h) W^O
where head_i = Attention(Q W_i^Q, K W_i^K, V W_i^V), and W_i^Q, W_i^K, W_i^V, W^O are learnable matrices. It inherits permutation properties.

Bahdanau (Additive) Attention
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(tanh(W_Q Q + W_K K) V)
with learnable W_Q, W_K.

Luong (General) Attention
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q W K^T) V
with learnable W.

Self-Attention
Self-attention sets Q = K = V = H W^Q, H W^K, H W^V, computing:
H' = Attention(H W^Q, H W^K, H W^V)
Encoder self-attention is all-to-all; decoder self-attention uses causal masking to exclude future tokens.

Masking
Causal masking in decoders sets attention weights w_ij = 0 for i < j, ensuring tokens only attend to prior positions.


Vision Transformer

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

A vision transformer (ViT) is a transformer designed for computer vision, decomposing an input image into patches, serializing each into a vector, and mapping it to a smaller dimension via matrix multiplication. These vector embeddings are processed by a transformer encoder, similar to token embeddings in natural language processing.

ViTs are alternatives to convolutional neural networks (CNNs) in computer vision, with different inductive biases, training stability, and data efficiency. Less data-efficient but higher-capacity than CNNs, ViTs include some of the largest vision models, like one with 22 billion parameters. Variants combine ViT and CNN features, applied in image recognition, segmentation, weather prediction, and autonomous driving.

History
Transformers, introduced in 2017 for natural language processing, inspired a 2019 study replacing ResNet's convolutional kernels with transformer self-attention, improving performance but not yet a ViT. In 2020, an encoder-only transformer was adapted for computer vision, creating the ViT, which achieved state-of-the-art image classification, surpassing CNN dominance. The 2022 masked autoencoder extended ViT to unsupervised training, stimulating new CNN developments. In 2021, variants like the Swin Transformer, using sliding window attention and pyramid processing, improved efficiency and accuracy, excelling on datasets like COCO.

Overview
The ViT architecture, based on a 2020 paper, resembles a BERT-like encoder-only transformer. An input image, with dimensions height H, width W, and channels C, is split into square patches of size P x P x C. Each patch is linearly mapped to a vector (patch embedding), combined with a position encoding vector, and processed by transformer encoders.

The attention mechanism transforms patch representations, capturing semantic relations, analogous to word relations in NLP. For downstream tasks like classification, a shallow multilayer perceptron (e.g., linear-GeLU-linear-softmax) is added to output class probabilities.


Sequential Decision Making

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Sequential decision making, a concept in control theory and operations research, involves making a series of decisions over time to optimize an objective function, such as maximizing cumulative rewards or minimizing costs. Each decision influences subsequent choices and system outcomes, considering the current state, available actions, and probabilistic state transitions. It models and regulates dynamic systems under uncertainty, commonly using Markov decision processes and dynamic programming.

Markov Decision Process

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

A Markov decision process (MDP), also known as a stochastic dynamic program or stochastic control problem, is a model for sequential decision making under uncertainty. Originating in 1950s operations research, MDPs are used in ecology, economics, healthcare, telecommunications, and reinforcement learning. In reinforcement learning, MDPs model agent-environment interactions via states, actions, and rewards, capturing cause-and-effect, uncertainty, and goal pursuit. Named after Andrey Markov, MDPs extend Markov chains by incorporating decision-making, with state transitions following the Markov property.

Definition
An MDP is a 4-tuple (S, A, P_a, R_a), where:
- S: State space, discrete or continuous (e.g., real numbers).
- A: Action space, discrete or continuous, with A_s as actions available in state s.
- P_a(s, s'): Probability of transitioning from state s to s' via action a at time t, defined as the probability s_(t+1) in S' given s_t = s, a_t = a, equaling the integral over S' of P_a(s, s') ds'. For discrete states, this is P_a(s, s') = Pr(s_(t+1) = s' | s_t = s, a_t = a); for continuous states, it uses Lebesgue measure.
- R_a(s, s'): Immediate or expected reward after transitioning from s to s' via action a.
- pi: Policy function, mapping states to actions, potentially probabilistic.

Optimization Objective
The goal is to find a policy pi specifying action pi(s) in state s to maximize a cumulative reward function, typically the expected discounted sum over an infinite horizon: E[sum from t=0 to infinity of gamma^t R_a_t(s_t, s_(t+1))], where a_t = pi(s_t), s_(t+1) ~ P_a_t(s_t, s_(t+1)), and gamma (0 to 1) is the discount factor, often near 1 (e.g., gamma = 1/(1+r) for discount rate r). A lower gamma prioritizes early actions.

Alternatively, the H-step return maximizes E[sum from t=0 to H-1 of R_a_t(s_t, s_(t+1))], focusing on the first H steps with equal weights, common in learning theory.

An optimal policy pi^* maximizes the objective. Multiple optimal policies may exist, and the Markov property ensures the optimal policy depends only on the current state.

Simulator Models
When transition probabilities P_a(s, s') are hard to specify, simulators model MDPs implicitly. An episodic environment simulator starts from an initial state, yielding a state and reward per action, producing state-action-reward trajectories (episodes). A generative model samples next states and rewards for any state-action pair, offering flexibility over episodic simulators. Explicit models yield generative models via sampling, and generative models yield episodic simulators via repetition. Only approximate models can be learned in reverse. Model type affects algorithm choice: dynamic programming needs explicit models, Monte Carlo tree search requires generative or episodic simulators, and reinforcement learning often uses episodic simulators.

Q-learning

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Q-learning is a model-free reinforcement learning algorithm that trains an agent to assign values to actions based on the current state, handling stochastic transitions and rewards without adaptation. For example, in a grid maze, an agent learns to reach a rewarding exit by valuing actions (e.g., moving right over left) through trial and error. For finite Markov decision processes, Q-learning finds an optimal policy maximizing expected total reward from any state, given infinite exploration and a partly random policy. The "Q" refers to the quality function, computing expected rewards for state-action pairs.

Reinforcement Learning
Reinforcement learning involves an agent, states S, and actions A per state. Actions transition states, yielding rewards. The agent maximizes total reward by considering current and future rewards, weighted by expected future values.

Example: Boarding a train, where reward is the negative of boarding time. Entering immediately (0s wait, 15s fight) versus waiting (5s wait, 0s fight) shows waiting yields a higher reward, learned through exploration.

Algorithm
Q-learning uses a function Q: S x A to R, initialized arbitrarily. At time t, the agent selects action A_t, observes reward R_(t+1), enters state S_(t+1), and updates Q via the Bellman equation:
Q_new(S_t, A_t) = (1 - alpha) Q(S_t, A_t) + alpha (R_(t+1) + gamma max_a Q(S_(t+1), a))
where alpha (0 < alpha <= 1) is the learning rate, R_(t+1) is the reward, gamma (0 <= gamma <= 1) is the discount factor, and max_a Q(S_(t+1), a) estimates optimal future value. Episodes end at terminal states, but Q-learning can learn in non-episodic tasks if gamma < 1. Terminal state Q-values are fixed (often zero).

Influence of Variables
Learning Rate
Alpha determines how new information overrides old. Alpha = 0 exploits prior knowledge; alpha = 1 uses only recent data. In deterministic settings, alpha = 1 is optimal; in stochastic settings, alpha must decrease to zero, though constant alpha (e.g., 0.1) is common.

Discount Factor
Gamma weights future rewards. Gamma = 0 considers only current rewards; gamma near 1 prioritizes long-term rewards. If gamma >= 1, values may diverge, especially without terminal states. Lower gamma with neural networks prevents instability.

Initial Conditions (Q0)
High initial Q-values encourage exploration, as updates lower untried actions' values. Rewards can reset initial conditions, enabling immediate learning for deterministic rewards. Resetting initial conditions better predicts human behavior in choice experiments.

Implementation
Q-learning stores Q-values in tables, infeasible for large state/action spaces due to low visitation probability.

Function Approximation
Function approximation, using neural networks or sparse fuzzy rule-bases, enables Q-learning for continuous spaces, generalizing experiences to unseen states.

Quantization
Quantizing state/action values (e.g., "Near" vs. "Far" for position) reduces space size, as in balancing a stick, where continuous variables (position, velocity, angle) are bucketed.

History
Introduced by Chris Watkins in 1989, with convergence proven by Watkins and Peter Dayan in 1992. Bozinovski's 1981 Crossbar Adaptive Array predated Q-learning, using a similar memory matrix and state evaluation. In 2014, Google DeepMind patented deep Q-learning for Atari games.

Variants
Deep Q-learning
DeepMind's deep Q-learning uses convolutional neural networks, facing instability from observation correlations and policy changes. Experience replay samples prior actions randomly, reducing correlations and smoothing data distribution changes.

Double Q-learning
Double Q-learning counters Q-learning's overestimation in noisy environments by using two value functions Q^A and Q^B, trained symmetrically. Updates use one function for action selection and the other for evaluation, e.g.:
Q_(t+1)^A(s_t, a_t) = Q_t^A(s_t, a_t) + alpha_t(s_t, a_t) (r_t + gamma Q_t^B(s_(t+1), arg max_a Q_t^A(s_(t+1), a)) - Q_t^A(s_t, a_t))
Combined with deep learning in 2015, Double DQN outperformed DQN.

Others
Delayed Q-learning offers PAC learning. Greedy GQ ensures convergence with linear function approximation. Distributional Q-learning models return distributions for risk-sensitive control. Multi-agent Q-learning treats environments as passive or uses minimax Q-learning.

Limitations
Standard Q-learning applies to discrete spaces. Discretization for continuous spaces causes inefficiency due to the curse of dimensionality, mitigated by techniques like wire-fitted neural network Q-learning.

Autoencoder

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

An autoencoder is an artificial neural network for unsupervised learning, creating efficient codings of unlabeled data. It learns an encoding function to transform input data and a decoding function to recreate the input from the encoded representation, typically for dimensionality reduction or generating embeddings for other algorithms. Variants like sparse, denoising, and variational autoencoders enhance properties for tasks such as classification or generative modeling. Applications include facial recognition, feature detection, anomaly detection, and semantic word learning.

Mathematical Principles
Definition
An autoencoder comprises:
- Spaces: Decoded messages X (e.g., R^m) and encoded messages Z (e.g., R^n, n < m).
- Functions: Encoder E_phi: X to Z, parameterized by phi; Decoder D_theta: Z to X, parameterized by theta.
The encoder maps input x to code z = E_phi(x), and the decoder maps z to reconstructed message x' = D_theta(z). Typically, both are multilayer perceptrons, e.g., a one-layer encoder: E_phi(x) = sigma(W x + b), where sigma is an activation function, W is a weight matrix, and b is a bias vector.

Training an Autoencoder
Training uses a reference distribution mu_ref over X and a reconstruction quality function d(x, x') measuring difference. The loss is L(theta, phi) = E_x~mu_ref[d(x, D_theta(E_phi(x)))], minimized via gradient descent to find the optimal autoencoder. Often, mu_ref is an empirical distribution from a dataset {x_1, ..., x_N}, and d is L2 loss: d(x, x') = ||x - x'||_2^2, making the problem a least-squares optimization: min_theta,phi 1/N sum_i=1^N ||x_i - D_theta(E_phi(x_i))||_2^2.

Interpretation
An autoencoder encodes and decodes messages. Undercomplete autoencoders (Z dimension < X) compress data, ideally reconstructing perfectly (D_theta(E_phi(x)) = x). Overcomplete autoencoders (Z dimension >= X) may learn the identity function unless regularized. Variants enforce useful properties.

Variations
Variational Autoencoder (VAE)
VAEs use variational Bayesian methods, modeling data as a distribution p_theta(x) = integral_z p_theta(x, z) dz, with a latent space of distribution mixtures, suitable for generative tasks.

Sparse Autoencoder (SAE)
SAEs enforce sparse codes, with few active hidden units. K-sparse SAEs clamp all but the top-k activations to zero, using a k-sparse function. Relaxed SAEs add a sparsity regularization loss: min_theta,phi L(theta, phi) + lambda L_sparse(theta, phi), where L_sparse is the expected weighted sum of sparsity differences, often using Kullback-Leibler divergence or L1/L2 norms.

Denoising Autoencoder (DAE)
DAEs train with corrupted inputs T(x) from a noise distribution mu_T, optimizing: min_theta,phi E_x~mu_X,T~mu_T[d(x, D_theta(E_phi(T(x))))]. Noise (e.g., Gaussian, masking, salt-and-pepper) is applied only during training, enhancing robustness.

Contractive Autoencoder (CAE)
CAEs add a contractive regularization loss: min_theta,phi L(theta, phi) + lambda L_cont(theta, phi), where L_cont = E_x~mu_ref ||nabla_x E_phi(x)||_F^2, minimizing the encoder's Jacobian Frobenius norm to ensure small input changes yield small code changes.

Minimum Description Length Autoencoder (MDL-AE)
MDL-AEs minimize total description length (code length + reconstruction error) per the Minimum Description Length principle, ensuring compact, interpretable representations.

Concrete Autoencoder (CAE)
Concrete autoencoders select a fixed number of features in the latent space using a continuous relaxation of the categorical distribution, enabling backpropagation for optimal feature subsets.

Advantages of Depth
Deep autoencoders with multiple layers reduce computational cost, decrease required training data, and improve compression compared to shallow ones.

Training
Deep belief networks train deep autoencoders by treating layer pairs as restricted Boltzmann machines for pretraining, followed by backpropagation fine-tuning. Joint training optimizes the entire architecture, improving models and features, but requires careful regularization.

History
In 1982, Oja linked PCA to a single-layer autoencoder. Baldi and Hornik (1989) and Kramer (1991) generalized PCA to nonlinear autoencoders. Auto-association mode was proposed in 1986, implemented for speech and images by 1987-1988. Deep belief networks (2006) advanced deep autoencoders. Applications from the 1990s included dimensionality reduction, with modern uses in generative models like VAEs in Stable Diffusion and DALL-E.

Anomaly Detection

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Anomaly detection, also known as outlier or novelty detection, identifies rare items, events, or observations deviating significantly from most data, not conforming to normal behavior. Anomalies may indicate a different generating mechanism or inconsistency with the dataset. Applications span cybersecurity, medicine, machine vision, statistics, neuroscience, law enforcement, and financial fraud detection. Historically, anomalies were removed to aid statistical analysis or improve model predictions, but now they are often the primary interest, requiring separation from noise.

Three categories exist:
- Supervised: Uses labeled normal/abnormal data to train classifiers, rare due to limited labeled data and class imbalance.
- Semi-supervised: Assumes some labeled data, often modeling normal behavior to test test instance likelihood.
- Unsupervised: Assumes unlabeled data, most common, detecting anomalies without prior labeling.

Definition
Definitions include:
Ill-defined:
- Outliers deviate significantly, suggesting a different mechanism.
- Anomalies are rare instances with significantly different features.
- Outliers appear inconsistent with the dataset.
- Anomalies are distant points in multi-dimensional feature space.
- Anomalies do not conform to normal behavior patterns.
Specific:
- In a univariate Gaussian, a point O is an outlier if its z-score exceeds a threshold.
